Computing transcendence and linear relations of 1-periods
Emre Can Sertöz, Joël Ouaknine, James Worrell

Abstract
. A 1 1 -period is a complex number given by the integral of a univariate algebraic function, where all data involved — the integrand and the domain of integration — are defined over algebraic numbers. We give an algorithm that, given a finite collection of 1 1 -periods, computes the space of ℚ ¯ {\overline{\mathbb{Q}}} -linear relations among them. In particular, the algorithm decides whether a given 1 1 -period is transcendental, and whether two 1 1 -periods are equal. This resolves, in the case of 1 1 -periods, a problem posed by Kontsevich and Zagier, asking for an algorithm to decide equality of periods. The algorithm builds on the work of Huber and Wüstholz, who showed that all linear relations among 1 1 -periods arise from 1 1 -motives; we make this perspective effective by reducing the problem to divisor arithmetic on curves and providing the theoretical foundations for a practical and fully explicit algorithm. To illustrate the broader applicability of our methods, we also give an algorithmic classification of autonomous first-order (non-linear) differential equations.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
14Q05, 14C30, 14F40, 14H40

1.
Introduction

(1.0.1) Periods are the values of integrals of algebraic forms over semi-algebraic domains, both defined over algebraic numbers. These numbers appear broadly in the sciences and connect arithmetic algebraic geometry to transcendental number theory. The Kontsevich–Zagier
[
KZ01
]
and Grothendieck
[
Gro66
,
And89
,
Ayo14
]
period conjectures predict that equality between periods should be decidable by algebro-geometric means. Yet these conjectures remain far out of reach in this generality.

(1.0.2) Kontsevich and Zagier ask for an algorithm
[
KZ01
, Problem 1, p.7]
to decide equality of two periods. In this paper, we resolve this problem in the case of 1-periods: integrals of
univariate
algebraic forms. We construct an algorithm that can decide equality among 1-periods and even decide transcendence. Beyond mere decidability statements, we have devised an algorithm grounded in divisor arithmetic on curves allowing us to rely entirely on well-established, practical methods.

(1.0.3) The theoretical bedrock for our algorithm is the work of Huber and Wüstholz
[
HW22
]
, who proved that all
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
-linear relations among 1-periods arise from morphisms of 1-motives. While their results are not algorithmic, the algebro-geometric nature of the statement suggests that an effective version should be possible. Rather than attempting to translate their methods — which would require working with semi-abelian varieties embedded in high-dimensional projective spaces — we focus instead on generalised Jacobians and the mixed Hodge structures
\operatorname{H}_{1}(C\setminus{}D,E)
of punctured and marked curves. In doing so, we develop a range of computational techniques that may be of independent interest for the study of algebraic curves and their periods; see for instance (
1
).

(1.0.4) To give an emblematic example of a
1
-period, consider the identity

(1.0.4.1)

\pi=\int_{-1}^{1}2\sqrt{1-x^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x,

which expresses the area of the unit circle as the integral of an algebraic function over an interval with algebraic endpoints.

(1.0.5) In general, a
1
-period may be defined as an integral
\int_{a}^{b}f(x)\,\mathrm{d}x
, where
a,b
are real algebraic numbers and the integrand
f(x)\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{C}
is continuous and algebraic over
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
; that is, it satisfies a nonzero polynomial relation
P(x,f(x))=0
for some
P\in{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}[x,y]
. This definition recognisably includes classical examples such as elliptic integrals and special values of the Gauss hypergeometric function.

(1.0.6) While this formulation offers a concrete and familiar perspective, it is not the one we will adopt. For our purposes, a more flexible definition — better suited to the geometric and computational framework developed here — will be preferable. The two formulations are equivalent, though we will not need this fact.

(1.0.7) A
1
-period
is a complex number that can be realised as the value of an integral

\int_{\gamma}\omega,

where
C/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
is a smooth projective curve,
\omega
is a rational differential on
C
, and
\gamma
is a smooth
1
-chain on
C(\mathbb{C})\setminus\poles(\omega)
with boundary
\partial\gamma
supported on
C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
. We refer to the data
(\gamma,\omega)_{C}
as a
representation
of the
1
-period.

(1.0.8) To work with
1
-periods effectively, we must specify how the tuple
(\gamma,\omega)_{C}
is represented. The curve
C/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
is given by specifying a birational plane model for each irreducible component; equivalently, each component is represented by an irreducible polynomial
P(x,y)\in{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}[x,y]
. The rational differential
\omega
is likewise represented, per component, by a polynomial
Q(x,y)\in{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}[x,y]
such that the form is given by the restriction of
Q(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x
to
C
. We turn next to the representation of the chain
\gamma
.

(1.0.9) For the smooth
1
-chain
\gamma
, the value of the integral depends only on its homology class. Any representation of
\gamma
from which this class can be recovered is therefore acceptable — for instance, one that allows the integrals of rational forms over
\gamma
to be approximated to arbitrary precision (
4.5
). For the basis of homology, we use
1
-chains that are rectilinear in the
x
-coordinate; see §
4
.

(1.0.10) We fix an embedding
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}\subset\mathbb{C}
, and assume that algebraic numbers are represented by their minimal polynomial over
\mathbb{Q}
together with data isolating the intended complex root (e.g., a complex ball with rational center and radius).

(1.0.11)
Main Theorem.
Given representations
(\gamma_{1},\omega_{1})_{C_{1}},\dots,(\gamma_{k},\omega_{k})_{C_{k}}
of
1
-periods
\alpha_{i}=\int_{\gamma_{i}}\omega_{i}
, Algorithm (
2
) in Section
2
computes a
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
-basis for the space of
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
-linear relations

(1.0.11.1)

\rel_{{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left\{(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{k})\in{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{k}:\sum_{i=1}^{k}\beta_{i}\alpha_{i}=0\right\}.

(1.0.12)
Corollary.
Given a representation of a
1
-period
\alpha
, Algorithm (
2
) decides whether
\alpha
is transcendental, and when it is algebraic, produces a standard representation of
\alpha
as an algebraic number.

Proof.
Indeed,
1=\int_{[0,1]}\mathrm{d}x
is a
1
-period, so we may compute
\rel_{{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}(\alpha,1)
, which is the
0
vector space precisely when
\alpha\notin{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
. (The basis for the
0
vector space is the empty set.) If
\alpha\in{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
, then
\rel_{{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}(\alpha,1)
is a
1
-dimensional subspace of
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{2}
, and the algorithm will return a generator
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
such that
\alpha=-\beta_{2}/\beta_{1}
. ∎

(1.0.13)
Corollary.
Given representations of two
1
-periods
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
, Algorithm (
2
) decides whether
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}
.

Proof.
Equality holds precisely when
(1,-1)\in\rel_{{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
, which can be tested using a
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
-basis. ∎

(1.0.14)
Corollary.
The
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
-vector space of
1
-periods is effective.

Proof.
This means that the operations of addition, scalar multiplication, and equality are computable. The
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
-scaling of a representative is achieved by scaling the form:
\beta\cdot(\gamma,\omega)_{C}=(\gamma,\beta\cdot\omega)_{C}
. To add two representatives
(\gamma_{1},\omega_{1})_{C_{1}}
and
(\gamma_{2},\omega_{2})_{C_{2}}
, take the disjoint union of the underlying curves, and add the
1
-chains and
1
-forms there. Equality is checked as in Corollary (
1
).

(1.0.15)
Technical framework.
We construct a complete computational model for the mixed Hodge structure
\operatorname{H}_{1}(C\setminus D,E)
: the de Rham realisation via rational differentials over the base field §
3
; the Betti realisation via embedded graphs §
4
; and the comparison isomorphism via certified precision integration. To circumvent arithmetic on periods, we augment this structure with the Jacobian motive of
(C\setminus D,E)
, see §
6
. This is a toric extension
J_{C}^{D}
of the Jacobian
J_{C}
of
C
, marked by divisors supported on
E
. Divisor arithmetic on
C
enables exact computation with the points of
J_{C}^{D}
, which compensates for the inability to test identities among periods directly.

To obtain the flexibility needed for “symmetrizing” the motive while remaining within the domain of curves, as required in §
9
, we generalise slightly to push-pull Jacobian motives and their mixed Hodge structures. On the theoretical side, we give an effective description of the period relations of “sufficiently symmetric”
1
-motives; see §
5
. These developments require a full-stack construction: even classical components must be extended to accommodate the generality of
(C\setminus D,E)
, and functoriality and effectivity must be engineered to work together as a computable theory of
1
-periods.

(1.0.16)
Classification problem.
We expect the computational model for
\operatorname{H}_{1}(C\setminus{}D,E)
, together with the subroutines developed, will be of independent interest. To illustrate the point, we resolve the problem of algorithmically classifying first-order autonomous (non-linear) differential equations posed by Noordman, van der Put, and Top
[
NvdPT22
, p.1655]
. See §
10
for the precise statement and the algorithm.

1.1.
Connections to foundational work

(1.1.17) Another approach to deciding equality of
1
-periods is to determine effective separation bounds. Hirata-Kohno
[
Hir91
]
and Gaudron
[
Gau05
]
prove that for any given
1
-period
\alpha
, there exists a constant
c>0
— effective in terms of the data defining
\alpha
— such that
|\alpha|<c
implies
\alpha=0
. These results ultimately rely on Wüstholz’s analytic subgroup theorem
[
Wüs89
]
and its quantitative refinements by Masser–Wüstholz
[
MW93a
,
MW93
]
. In the classical setting of Baker periods (i.e., underlying curves have genus zero), the bound is completely explicit
[
WB93
]
. For periods of elliptic curves, explicit bounds have been obtained
[
Dav95
,
DH09
]
, and more recently, also in the case of abelian varieties
[
BG19
]
, corresponding to the situation in (
1
) where the
1
-form has no poles. In general, however, the constant
c
remains only effective in principle: computing it explicitly would require navigating a long chain of intermediate constants as well as determening subtle arithmetic constants; see
[
Gau05
, p.145]
and the discussion after
[
BW08
, Theorem 7.2]
for more on this point.

(1.1.18) Separation bounds permit, in principle, effective tests for equality of
1
-periods. But deciding transcendence requires exhaustive knowledge of all
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
-linear relations. In this sense, our algorithm provides the first effective method for determining transcendence of
1
-periods.

(1.1.19) Our algorithm ultimately relies on one of the main theorems of Huber and Wüstholz — the “Dimension estimate”
[
HW22
, Theorem 15.3]
— or rather on a reassembly of its proof, which gives a description of the space of period relations associated to a
saturated

1
-motive. Their arguments, which build around Wüstholz’s analytic subgroup theorem
[
Wüs89
]
, determine the
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
-dimension for the space of
1
-periods of a saturated
1
-motive. Explicitly relating the period relations of an arbitrary motive to those of a saturated motive involves substantial additional work. An important part of our contribution is an effective method to perform the saturation step, see §
9
, using only arithmetic on algebraic curves — a task that turns out to be technically intricate and requires the full machinery developed here.

(1.1.20) Recall that one of our key contributions is to base our computations on the divisor arithmetic on curves. To put this into context, we compare it to Chapter 14 of
[
HW22
]
, where the vanishing of periods (the case
k=1
for our algorithm) is studied from a curve-theoretic point of view. There, it is assumed that the underlying curve has a
simple Jacobian
, to eliminate the need for supersaturation — the most challenging part of our algorithm, see §
9
. Nevertheless, a full translation to arithmetic on curves is not attained there: even in the first nontrivial case, one ends up with a check involving the condition “(14.3)” of
[
HW22
, p.137]
involving the vanishing of a differential under a motivic pullback map. We are able to replace motivic abstractions with explicit, curve-theoretic computations by methodically building up subroutines to compute with the
\operatorname{H}_{1}
of punctured marked curves and building correspondences as the cornerstone of our morphisms. To demonstrate the effect, we unwind what our algorithm does in the aforementioned case (
8.4
).

(1.1.21) A crucial computational input to our algorithm is the endomorphism algebra of a Jacobian, represented explicitly in terms of algebraic correspondences. This plays a central role in the supersaturation step, where correspondences are used to generate additional relations. For this, we rely on the practical semi-algorithm of Costa–Mascot–Sijsling–Voight
[
CMSV18
]
, which computes the endomorphism algebra as a space of correspondences and has been successfully implemented. Its correctness is unconditional, and its termination is guaranteed under the Mumford–Tate conjecture, as shown by Costa–Lombardo–Voight
[
CLV21
]
. To ensure unconditional termination in theory, we cite the effective construction of Lombardo
[
Lom18
]
, though it is not intended for practical use. See §
7
for more details.

(1.1.22) We rely on many other subroutines, often implicitly. However, the computation of the Riemann–Roch space

(1.1.22.1)

L(D)=\{f\in\kappa(C)^{\times}\mid D+\divv(f)\geq 0\}\cup\{0\}\subset\kappa(C)

for a divisor
D
on an irreducible curve
C/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
with function field
\kappa(C)
lies at the heart of our approach. See
[
Hes02
]
for an efficient algorithm to compute
L(D)
and a historical overview of its development.

). JW is supported by UKRI Frontier Research Grant EP/X033813/1.

2.
The algorithm

(2.0.1)
Algorithm.

The following is the main algorithm of the paper, which computes all
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
-linear relations between a given finite collection of
1
-periods. Intermediate steps refer to constructions developed in the rest of the paper.

(2.0.2)
Input.
A tuple of representatives,
(\gamma_{i},\omega_{i})_{C_{i}}
,
i=1,\dots,k
, for
1
-periods
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k}
.

(2.0.3)
Output.
A basis for the space of
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
-linear relations between
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k}
, i.e., the kernel of the map
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{k}\to\mathbb{C}:(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{k})\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{k}\alpha_{i}\beta_{i}

(2.0.4) For each
i=1,\dots,k
, compute a finite set
D_{i}\subset C_{i}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
supporting the residual divisor of
\omega_{i}
and compute a finite set
E_{i}\subset C_{i}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
supporting the boundary divisor of
\gamma_{i}
, such that
D_{i}
and
E_{i}
are disjoint. Let
C=\coprod_{i=1}^{k}C_{i}
,
D=\coprod_{i=1}^{k}D_{i}
,
E=\coprod_{i=1}^{k}E_{i}
where we view
D,E\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
.

(2.0.5) Compute (
4.1
) a representative of the mixed Hodge structure

(2.0.5.1)

\operatorname{H}_{1}(C\setminus{}D,E)=(\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D,E)),\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)^{\vee},\mathfrak{c}_{{\operatorname{B}},{\operatorname{AdR}}}).

That means, we compute a basis of differentials for the algebraic de Rham cohomology
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)\simeq{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{m}
, a basis of
1
-chains for the Betti homology
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D,E)\simeq\mathbb{Q}^{m}
, and the representatives allow for an arbitrary precision approximation of the comparison isomorphism.

(2.0.6) Let
\gamma=\gamma_{1}+\dots+\gamma_{k}
be the
1
-chain on
C
obtained by summing the given
1
-chains. Determine (
4.5
) the coordinates of the homology class
[\gamma]
in
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D,E)
with respect to our basis of
1
-chains. View
\omega_{i}
as a
1
-form on
C
extended by
0
to components
C_{j}
,
j\neq i
, and compute (
3.7
) the coordinates of the cohomology class
[\omega_{i}]
in
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)
. We now have the matrix representation of the following map:

(2.0.6.1)

I\colon{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{k}\to\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D,E)\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)\simeq{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{m\times m}:\beta\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{k}\beta_{i}[\gamma]\otimes[\omega_{i}].

(2.0.7) Compute (
7.2
) a representation of the endomorphism algebra
\mathcal{E}=\End(J_{C})
of the Jacobian of
C
.

(2.0.8) Let
M=J_{C,E}^{D}
denote the Jacobian motive of
(C\setminus{}D,E)
. Compute (
9
), the supersaturation
M^{ss}
of
M
together with an explicit isogeny direct sum decomposition of
M^{ss}
into a Baker motive
M_{B}
and a saturated push-pull Jacobian
M_{1}=J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}
. The process is period effective, meaning that the period relations of
M
can be determined from those of
M^{ss}
, see (
9
). The motive
M_{1}
is computed together with the explicit
\mathcal{E}
-action
\mathcal{E}\otimes\operatorname{H}_{1}(M_{1})\to\operatorname{H}_{1}(M_{1})
.

(2.0.9) Since
M_{1}
is saturated, we can compute the period relations
\mathcal{R}(M_{1})\subset\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M_{1})\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{{\operatorname{AdR}}}(M_{1})
of
M_{1}
: this space is equal (
5.6
) to the expected period relations
\mathcal{R}_{e}(M_{1})
, which we can determined from the
\mathcal{E}
-action on
\operatorname{H}_{1}
above and from the trivial relations which are readily computed (
5.6
).

(2.0.10) Compute (
8.8
) the period relations
\mathcal{R}(M_{B})
of the Baker motive
M_{B}
.

(2.0.11) We now compute
\mathcal{R}(M^{ss})
, the space of period relations of the supersaturated motive. This space decomposes as follows (
5.6
), and each summand is now computable:

(2.0.11.1)

\mathcal{R}(M^{ss})=\mathcal{R}_{\triv}(M^{ss})+\mathcal{R}(M_{B})\oplus\mathcal{R}_{e}(M_{1})+\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M_{B})\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M_{1})+\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M_{1})\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M_{B}).

(2.0.12) Since the construction of
M^{ss}
from
M
was period effective (
9
), we can determine
\mathcal{R}(M)\subset\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M)\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M)
by a simple procedure from that of
\mathcal{R}(M^{ss})
. Compute
\mathcal{R}(M)
.

(2.0.13) From the very start we work with the identification
\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{C,E}^{D})=\operatorname{H}_{1}(C\setminus{}D,E)
. Therefore
\mathcal{R}(M)
lives in the codomain of
I
. Output a basis for the pullback
I^{-1}\mathcal{R}(M)\subset{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{k}
.

(2.0.14)
Remark.
We suspect that the most time consuming step will be (
2
), but we can rely on the streamlined implementation in
[
CMSV18
]
. The step (
2
) is terribly involved and could present another computational bottleneck.

3.
Relative algebraic de Rham cohomology of a punctured curve

(3.0.1) Let
C
be a smooth, proper curve defined over
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
, and let
D,E\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
be two finite, disjoint sets. The pair
(C\setminus{}D,E)
will be called a
punctured marked curve
. Topologically, this is to be viewed as
C\setminus{}D
relative to
E
.

(3.0.2) This section describes how to compute and effectively work with the algebraic de Rham cohomology
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)
of the punctured marked curve
(C\setminus{}D,E)
. Since (co)homology is additive over components of
C
, we will assume without loss of generality that
C
is irreducible, unless stated otherwise.

(3.0.3) The case
D=E=\emptyset
is well-known to the experts
[
Kat68
,
CG89
]
. Nevertheless, we spell-out the proof of the fact that
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C)
is isomorphic (as a
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
-vector space) to the space of second kind differentials on
C
modulo exact forms (over
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
). The pairing with singular homology is via integration (
3.4
). This is classical over
\mathbb{C}
and applicable in greater generality
[
Ros53
,
HA55
,
Mes75
]
. This allows for the practical identity

(3.0.3.1)

\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C)\simeq\operatorname{H}^{0}(C,\Omega_{C}^{1}((g+1)p))

where
p\in C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
is any non-Weierstrass point and
g
is the genus of
C
, see (
3.5
). We also give two different “reduction algorithms” to find the unique representative in
\operatorname{H}^{0}(C,\Omega_{C}^{1}((g+1)p))
of any given second type differential (see §
3.7
).

(3.0.4) For the general case when
D,E
are non-empty, we could not find a reference to a result analogous to (
3.0.3.1
). We therefore prove Proposition (
3.4
) and Corollary (
3.5
) which allows us to effectively compute a basis for
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)
(
3.6
). The aforementioned reduction algorithms in §
3.7
are applicable in this generality.

3.1.
Representing irreducible curves and their differentials

(3.1.5) In this subsection,
C/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
is an irreducible, smooth, proper curve.

(3.1.6) A representation of the curve
C
is a (reduced, irreducible) polynomial
P(x,y)\in{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}[x,y]
defining an affine plane curve
Z(P)\subset\mathbb{A}^{2}
birational to
C
. Without loss of generality, we will assume that the
y
-derivative
P_{y}
of
P
is non-zero. By default, our curves come with a finite map
x\colon C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
, the projection to the
x
-axis.

(3.1.7) Giving a plane model of
C
is equivalent to representing the function field
\kappa(C)
of
C
by exhibiting an element
x\in\kappa(C)
, transcendental over
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
, and an element
y\in\kappa(C)
algebraic over
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}(x)
and generating
\kappa(C)
. Thus,
\kappa(C)={\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}(x)[y]/P(x,y)
.

(3.1.8) The transcendental element
x\in\kappa(C)
realizes
C
as a finite cover
x\colon C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
of degree equal to the
y
-degree of
P
. The corresponding map
x\colon C(\mathbb{C})\to\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
between the underlying Riemann surfaces realizes
C(\mathbb{C})
as a branced cover of
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
.

(3.1.9) The rational differentials on
C
are the Kähler differentials
\Omega^{1}_{\kappa(C)/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}
of
\kappa(C)
over
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
. The relation
\mathrm{d}P=P_{x}\mathrm{d}x+P_{y}\mathrm{d}y=0
and our assumption
P_{y}\neq 0
allows us to eliminate
\mathrm{d}y
,

(3.1.9.1)

\Omega^{1}_{\kappa(C)/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}=\kappa(C)\mathrm{d}x=\left({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}(x)[y]/P(x,y)\right)\,\mathrm{d}x,

(3.1.10) We will represent a rational differential
\omega\in\Omega^{1}_{\kappa(C)/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}
by writing it as
\omega=f(x,y)\mathrm{d}x
where
f(x,y)\in\kappa(C)={\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}(x)[y]/P(x,y)
.

3.2.
Representing reducible curves and their differentials

(3.2.11) Assume
C/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
is a smooth proper curve with irreducible components
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
.

(3.2.12) We will represent
C
by representing the tuple
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
of its components, i.e., by exhibiting a tuple of polynomials
P_{i}\in{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}[x,y]
, for
i=1,\dots,k
, each with non-vanishing
y
-derivatives. Let
x_{i}
be the image of
x
in
\kappa(C_{i})={\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}(x)[y]/P_{i}(x,y)
.

(3.2.13) We define the algebra
\kappa(C)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\kappa(C_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus\kappa(C_{k})
of rational functions on
C
. Let
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\in\kappa(C)
be the tuple of transcendental generators. This is equivalent to identifying the codomains of the maps
x_{i}\colon C_{i}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
to form a finite map
x\colon C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

(3.2.14) The space of rational differentials
\Omega^{1}_{\kappa(C)/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}
on
C
is identified with
\kappa(C)\mathrm{d}x
. Equivalently, a rational differential
\omega
on
C
is a tuple of rational differentials
(\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{k})
with
\omega_{i}\in\Omega^{1}_{\kappa(C_{i})/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}
.

3.3.
Poles of forms and definition of algebraic de Rham cohomology

(3.3.15) Throughout this section,
C
is an
irreducible
smooth proper curve over
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
.

(3.3.16) Let
\mathcal{O}_{C}
be the sheaf of rational functions on
C
and
\Omega^{1}_{C}
the sheaf of Kähler differentials of
C/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
. For any divisor
\xi
on
C
and any open set
U\subset C
we have
\mathcal{O}_{C}(\xi)(U)\subset\kappa(C)
and
\Omega^{1}_{C}(\xi)(U)\subset\Omega^{1}_{\kappa(C)/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}
. In particular, we will view local regular sections as global rational sections. The
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
-dimension of the global sections of a sheaf on
C
will be denoted by
h^{0}
.

(3.3.17) For a rational differential
\omega
on
C
, the
polar locus
of
\omega
is the set of poles of
\omega
. A pole of order one is called a
simple pole
. The polar locus is a finite subset of
C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
and is readily to computed.

(3.3.18) For any point
p\in C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
we will write
\res_{p}\omega\in{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
for the residue of
\omega
at
p
. Note that the residue
\res_{p}\omega
can be computed by making a Laurent series expansion of
\omega
with respect to a local coordinate
z_{p}
at
p
and taking the coefficient of
\mathrm{d}z_{p}/z_{p}
. If
\res_{p}\omega\neq 0
then
p
is a
residual pole of
\omega
.

(3.3.19) We define the
residual divisor of
\omega
to be
\Res(\omega)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{p\in C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})}(\res_{p}\omega)p
. The support of
\Res(\omega)
is the
residual polar locus of
\omega
. Since the polar locus is finite, the residual divisor is readily computed.

(3.3.20) If
\omega
has no poles, it is
regular
; classically called a
differential of the first kind
. If
\omega
has no residual poles,
\Res(\omega)=0
, then
\omega
is a
differential of the second kind
. If all poles of
\omega
are simple, then
\omega
is a
differential of the third kind
. Every rational differential is the sum of a differential of the second and third kind, and this decomposition is unique modulo differentials of the first kind.

(3.3.21) A rational differential
\omega
is of second kind if and only if it is Zariski locally equivalent to a section of
\Omega^{1}_{C}
modulo an exact form. The latter condition means:
\forall p\in C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
there is an open neighborhood
U
of
p
and an exact form
\mathrm{d}f
such that
\omega+\mathrm{d}f
is regular on
U
.

(3.3.22) Let
D\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
be a finite set. If
\omega
is a differential of the third kind with poles supported on
D
then we will call
\omega
a
\log(D)
-regular differential. For an open set
U\subset C
, we will say
\omega
is
\log(D)
-regular on
U
if all poles of
\omega|_{U}
are simple and supported on
D\cap U
. Note that
\Omega^{1}_{C}(D)
is the sheaf of
\log(D)
-regular differentials.

(3.3.23)
Remark.
In the case of curves, the sheaf
\Omega^{1}_{C}(D)
coincides with the sheaf
\Omega^{1}_{C}(\log(D))
of logarithmic differentials at
D
. The latter is needed for algebraic de Rham cohomology in arbitrary dimensions but the former is standard when discussing linear systems on curves. We will stick to curves and use the former notation throughout.

(3.3.24) Let
D,E\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
be finite disjoint sets. We recall (
[
Gro66
]
,
[
PS08
, §5.5]
,
[
GF
, §2.2.6]
) that the algebraic de Rham complex on
(C\setminus{}D,E)
is

(3.3.24.1)

\Omega_{(C\setminus{}D,E)}^{\bullet}:0\to\mathcal{O}_{C}\to\Omega^{1}_{C}(D)\oplus\mathcal{O}_{E}\to 0

where the non-zero map is
f\mapsto(\mathrm{d}f,-f|_{E})
. The hyper cohomology of (
3.3.24.1
) is the algebraic de Rham cohomology of
(C\setminus{}D,E)
. Our focus is on the first cohomology:

(3.3.24.2)

\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{H}^{1}(C,\Omega_{(C\setminus{}D,E)}^{\bullet}).

3.4.
Algebraic de Rham cohomology in terms of second type differentials

(3.4.25) Fix
D,E\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
finite and disjoint.

(3.4.26) A rational differential
\omega
on
C
will be called
\log(D)
-second kind if
\omega
is Zariski locally equivalent to a section of
\Omega^{1}_{C}(D)
modulo exact forms (compare with (
3.3
)).

(3.4.27)
Proposition.
We have a canonical isomorphism

(3.4.27.1)

\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D)\simeq\frac{\{\text{$\log(D)$-second kind differentials on $C$}\}}{\{\text{exact forms on $C$}\}},

with the nontrivial parts of the Hodge and weight filtration given by

(3.4.27.2)

\displaystyle F^{1}\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{H}^{0}(C,\Omega^{1}_{C}(D))=\{\text{$\log(D)$-regular differentials on $C$}\},

(3.4.27.3)

\displaystyle W_{1}\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C)=\frac{\{\text{second kind differentials on $C$}\}}{\{\text{exact forms on $C$}\}}.

Proof.
We continue to assume
C
is irreducible without loss of generality. Let
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\}
be an affine open cover of
C
. As usual we let
U_{ij}=U_{i}\cap U_{j}
, etc. With respect to
\mathcal{U}
, the Čech-to-de-Rham double complex of (
3.3.24.1
) where
E=\emptyset
is

(3.4.27.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{O}_{C}(U_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i}\Omega^{1}_{C}(D)(U_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{ij}\mathcal{O}_{C}(U_{ij})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{ij}\Omega^{1}_{C}(D)(U_{ij})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}

from which we form the single complex
\mathcal{E}_{0}\overset{\mathrm{d}_{0}}{\to}\mathcal{E}_{1}\overset{\mathrm{d}_{1}}{\to}\dots
, which is explicitly

(3.4.27.5)

{\bigoplus}_{i}\mathcal{O}_{C}(U_{i})\overset{\mathrm{d}_{0}}{\to}\left({\bigoplus}_{ij}\mathcal{O}_{C}(U_{ij}){\bigoplus}_{k}\Omega^{1}_{C}(D)(U_{k})\right)\overset{\mathrm{d}_{1}}{\to}\dots

Then, we find

(3.4.27.6)

\displaystyle\ker d_{1}

\displaystyle=\{(a_{ij};b_{k})\in\mathcal{E}_{1}:\text{$(a_{ij})$ is a \v{C}ech cocycle, $b_{i}-b_{j}=\mathrm{d}a_{ij}$}\}

(3.4.27.7)

\displaystyle\image d_{0}

\displaystyle=\{(a_{i}-a_{j};\mathrm{d}a_{k})\in\mathcal{E}_{1}:a_{i}\text{ is a regular function on $U_{i}$}\}.

The quotient
\ker d_{1}/\image d_{0}
computes
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D)
. We will take the limit over
\mathcal{U}
to account for all possible covers.

If
\omega
is a
\log(D)
-second kind differential, let
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\}
be an open affine cover for which
\omega|_{U_{i}}=\eta_{i}+\mathrm{d}f_{i}
, where
\eta_{i}\in\Omega^{1}_{C}(U_{i})
,
f_{i}\in\kappa(C)
. Then
\eta_{i}-\eta_{j}=\mathrm{d}(f_{i}-f_{j})
implies that
f_{i}-f_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{C}(U_{ij})
. We may thus consider the tuple
(f_{i}-f_{j};\eta_{k})\in\ker d_{1}
. Another representation
\omega|_{U_{i}}=\mu_{i}+\mathrm{d}g_{i}
will give rise to a tuple
(g_{i}-g_{j};\mu_{k})
where the difference
((f_{i}-g_{i})-(f_{j}-g_{j});\eta_{k}-\mu_{k})
lies in
\image d_{0}
since
\eta_{i}-\mu_{i}=\mathrm{d}(f_{i}-g_{i})
. Furthermore,
\omega
lands in
\ker d_{0}
only if it is locally exact,
\omega|_{U_{i}}=\mathrm{d}f_{i}
, but then
\omega
is exact since
C
is connected. This proves that the map from right to left of (
3.4.27.1
) is well defined and injective.

To prove surjectivity, take the class of
(a_{ij};b_{k})\in\mathcal{E}_{1}
, fix an index
0
and observe that
\omega\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}b_{0}
viewed as a global rational differential is of the second kind:
\omega|_{U_{j}}=b_{j}+\mathrm{d}a_{0j}
.

The statements about the filtrations are straightforward to prove. For (
3.4.27.2
) we simply note that a
\log(D)
-regular differential is never exact. As for (
3.4.27.3
), the first identity can be deduced from the long exact sequence for the inclusion
C\setminus{}D\to C
and the second is deduced from (
3.4.27.1
) for
D=\emptyset
.
∎

(3.4.28) Let
E\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
be a finite set. There is a restriction map

(3.4.28.1)

\operatorname{H}^{0}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C)\to\operatorname{H}^{0}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(E)

whose cokernel
\operatorname{H}^{0}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(E)_{0}
is the
primitive cohomology
of
E
. This notation is also used when
C
is reducible.

(3.4.29) Let
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{E}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathop{Hom}\nolimits_{\text{(Set)}}(E,{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
be the
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
-valued functions on
E
. Thus
\operatorname{H}^{0}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(E)={\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{E}
. Let
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{E}_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{E}/\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C)
where we mod out by restriction of locally constant functions on
C
. When
C
is irreducible
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C)={\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
and the quotient is via the diagonal action of
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
, i.e., by functions globally constant on
E
. For a tuple
a\in{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{E}
write
[a]\in{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{E}_{0}
for the class of
a
. The primitive cohomology
\operatorname{H}^{0}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(E)_{0}
coincides with
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{E}_{0}
.

(3.4.30) We map
\kappa(C)
on to the direct sum

(3.4.30.1)

\{\text{$\log(D)$-second kind differentials on $C$}\}\oplus{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{E}

by
f\mapsto(\mathrm{d}f,-f|_{E})
. We will refer to the image as
exact forms on
(C,E)
.

(3.4.31) Using the identification in Proposition (
3.4
) and the one in (
3.4
), the obvious maps give an exact sequence

(3.4.31.1)

0\to\operatorname{H}^{0}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(E)_{0}\to\frac{\{\text{$\log(D)$-second kind differentials on $C$}\}\oplus{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{E}}{\{\text{exact forms on $(C,E)$}\}}\to\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D)\to 0.

(3.4.32)
Lemma.
We have a canonical isomorphism

(3.4.32.1)

\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)\simeq\frac{\{\text{$\log(D)$-second kind differentials on $C$}\}\oplus{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{E}}{\{\text{exact forms on $(C,E)$}\}}.

Proof.
Both sides of (
3.4.32.1
) fit into the center of the short exact sequence (
3.4.31.1
). To deduce the isomorphism, it will be sufficient to prove there is an isomorphism from right to left of (
3.4.32.1
) respecting the extension (
3.4.31.1
).

We continue with the notation of the proof of (
3.4
) and make minor modifications to compute the Čech-to-de-Rham double complex of (
3.3.24.1
). If
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\}
is an affine open cover of
C
, let
E_{i}=E\cap U_{i}
. Then the associated single complex is of the form

(3.4.32.2)

{\bigoplus}_{i}\mathcal{O}_{C}(U_{i})\overset{\mathrm{d}_{0}}{\to}\left({\bigoplus}_{ij}\mathcal{O}_{C}(U_{ij}){\bigoplus}_{k}\Omega^{1}_{C}(D)(U_{k})\oplus{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{E_{k}}\right)\overset{\mathrm{d}_{1}}{\to}\dots

Once again we write

\displaystyle\ker d_{1}

\displaystyle=\{(a_{ij};b_{k},c_{k}):\text{$(a_{ij})$ is a \v{C}ech cocycle, $b_{i}-b_{j}=\mathrm{d}a_{ij}$, $(c_{i}-c_{j})|_{E_{ij}}=-a_{ij}|_{E_{ij}}$}\},

\displaystyle\image d_{0}

\displaystyle=\{(a_{i}-a_{j};\mathrm{d}a_{k}):a_{i}\text{ is a regular function on $U_{i}$}\}.

The quotient
\ker\mathrm{d}_{1}/\image\mathrm{d}_{0}
computes
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)
. We will take the limit over
\mathcal{U}
to account for all possible covers.

Let
(\omega,c)\mod\mathrm{d}\kappa(C)
be an element in the right hand side of (
3.4.32.1
). As in the proof of Proposition (
3.4
),
\omega
defines an affine open cover
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\}
, sections
\eta_{i}\in\Omega^{1}_{C}(D)(U_{i})
, and
f_{i}\in\kappa(C)
, with
\omega|_{U_{i}}=\eta_{i}+\mathrm{d}f_{i}
. The element
c\in{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{E}
can be restricted to
c_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}c|_{E_{i}}
. As in Proposition (
3.4
), this gives an element
(f_{i}-f_{j};\eta_{k},c_{k})\in\ker\mathrm{d}_{1}
which is well defined modulo
\image\mathrm{d}_{0}
. Compatibility of this map with both sides of (
3.4.31.1
) is clear.
∎

(3.4.33) Let
\omega
be a
\log(D)
-second type differential on
C
and
a\in{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{E}
. Let
\gamma
be a smooth
1
-chain on
C(\mathbb{C})
, avoiding the poles of
\omega
, and with boundary
\partial\gamma
supported on
E
. Extend
a
linearly to divisors supported on
E
,
a(\sum n_{i}p_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum n_{i}a(p_{i})
. It is easy to check that the integration pairing

(3.4.33.1)

\int_{\gamma}(\omega,a)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\int_{\gamma}\omega+a(\partial\gamma),

is well-defined on the singular homology class
[\gamma]\in\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D,E)
and on the class of
(\omega,a)
modulo
(C,E)
-exact forms. Via the isomorphism in Lemma (
3.4
), we can describe the “period pairing”

(3.4.33.2)

\displaystyle\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D,E)\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{C}

(3.4.33.3)

\displaystyle[\gamma]\otimes[\omega,a]\mapsto\int_{\gamma}(\omega,a).

This pairing agrees with the usual comparison isomorphism between
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}\otimes\mathbb{C}
and
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{B}}\otimes\mathbb{C}
, as can be checked by expanding the proof of the comparison isomorphism
[
GF
, Theorem 2.150]
.

3.5.
An effective basis for cohomology

(3.5.34)
Definition.
A tuple
(\omega_{1},a_{1}),\dots,(\omega_{m},a_{m})
is called a
differential basis
for the cohomology
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)
if
\omega_{i}
’s are
\log(D)
-second type differentials,
a_{i}\in{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{E}
are functions on
E
, and the
(\omega_{i},a_{i})
’s map to a basis via the identification (
3.4
). We will refer to a differential basis as a representation of
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)
.

(3.5.35) In this subsection and the next we will show how to compute a differential basis together with the Hodge and weight filtrations on cohomology. See (
3.6
) for a summary.

(3.5.36) A point
p\in C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
is a Weierstrass point if
h^{0}(gp)>1
where
g
is the genus of
C
. There are at most
g(g^{2}-1)
Weierstrass points on a curve
[
GH94
, p.274]
, hence a non-Weierstrass point can be found in finite time by trial-and-error.

(3.5.37)
Proposition.
Let
C
be an irreducible curve of genus
g
and
p\in C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
a non-Weierstrass point. Then, there is a natural isomorphism

(3.5.37.1)

\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C)\simeq\operatorname{H}^{0}(C,\Omega^{1}_{C}((g+1)p)).

If
p\in C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
is arbitrary, then there exists an integer
d_{0}
,
g<d_{0}\leq 2g
, such that for every
d\geq d_{0}
we have
h^{0}((d-1)p)=d-g
. Then, for all
d\geq d_{0}
, we have a natural isomorphism

(3.5.37.2)

\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C)\simeq\frac{\operatorname{H}^{0}(C,\Omega^{1}_{C}(dp))}{\mathrm{d}\operatorname{H}^{0}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C}((d-1)p))}.

Proof.
All cohomology classes can be represented by a differential of the second kind with poles only on
p
. To see this repeat the proof of Proposition (
3.4
) with the cover
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{0},U_{1}\}
where
U_{0}=C\setminus{}p
and
U_{1}
an affine neighbourhood of
p
.

All global sections of
\Omega^{1}_{C}(dp)
for
d\geq 0
are of the second kind since, by the residue theorem, a rational form with poles supported at a single point can not have any residue.

Thus, we have a natural isomorphism

(3.5.37.3)

\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C)\simeq\frac{\operatorname{H}^{0}(C,\Omega^{1}_{C}(dp))}{\mathrm{d}\operatorname{H}^{0}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C}((d-1)p))},\quad d\gg 0.

Since
\dim\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C)=2g
, we can pick any
d
for which the right hand side is of dimension
2g
.

By Riemann–Roch we have

(3.5.37.4)

h^{0}(\Omega^{1}_{C}((g+1)p))=2g.

If
p
is non-Weierstrass, then
h^{0}(gp)=1
and the space
\operatorname{H}^{0}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C}(gp))
consists only of constants, which are killed by derivation
\mathrm{d}
. Thus we may take
d=g+1
and this proves (
3.5.37.1
).

We now consider the case when
p
is arbitrary. The space in the denominator of (
3.5.37.3
) is of dimension
h^{0}((d-1)p)-1
since derivation kills constants. The space in the numerator is of dimension
h^{0}(\Omega^{1}_{C}(dp))=g+d-1
for
d>0
by Riemann–Roch. We want to solve for
d>0
such that

(3.5.37.5)

2g=h^{0}(\Omega^{1}_{C}(dp))-h^{0}((d-1)p)+1,

or equivalently
h^{0}((d-1)p)=d-g
. By Riemann–Roch this holds precisely when

(3.5.37.6)

h^{0}(\Omega^{1}_{C}((-d+1)p)=0.

This is true whenever
d\geq 2g
but it can never hold unless
g<d
since
h^{0}(\Omega^{1}_{C})=g
. Hence the minimal
d
will satisfy
g<d\leq 2g
.
∎

(3.5.38)
Remark.
A restatement of Proposition (
3.5
), in light of Proposition (
3.4
), is that a differential of the second kind on
C
is equivalent, modulo exact forms, to a
unique
differential with poles only at
p
, provided
p
is non-Weierstrass. This is analogous to the situation with forms of the first kind where regular differentials uniquely represent their cohomology classes.

(3.5.39) For a non-Weierstrass point
p\in C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\setminus{}D
, define

(3.5.39.1)

W_{D,p}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\ker\left(\res_{p}\colon\operatorname{H}^{0}(C,\Omega^{1}_{C}(D+(g+1)p))\to{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}\right).

By construction, any element of
W_{D,p}
is regular on
C\setminus{}\left(D\cup\{p\}\right)
, has at most a simple poles on
D
and any pole on
p
is non-residual. In particular,
W_{D,p}
consists of differentials of the
\log(D)
-second kind.

(3.5.40)
Proposition.
For
p\in C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\setminus{}D
non-Weierstrass, the identification (
3.4
) gives a natural isomorphism,

(3.5.40.1)

W_{D,p}\to\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D):\eta\mapsto\eta\text{ modulo exact forms}.

Proof.

Let
s\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\#D-1
. Using Riemann–Roch and the residue theorem we find

(3.5.40.2)

\dim W_{D,p}=\begin{cases}2g&D=\emptyset\\
2g+s&s\geq 0\end{cases}.

This agrees with the dimension of
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D)
since it fits into the exact sequence

(3.5.40.3)

0\to\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C)\to\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D)\overset{\Res}{\to}{}_{0}{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{D}\to 0,

where
{}_{0}{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{D}\subset{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{D}
is the space of functions on
D
whose value on
D
sum to
0
(the dual of
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{D}_{0}
).

Therefore, to show that (
3.5.40.1
) is an isomorphism, it will be sufficient to show that it is surjective.

We proved in Proposition (
3.5
) that the subspace

(3.5.40.4)

\operatorname{H}^{0}(C,\Omega^{1}_{C}((g+1)p))\subset W_{D,p}

surjects onto
\operatorname{H}^{1}(C)
. And when
s>0
,
h^{1}(\Omega^{1}_{C}(D))=0
implies
\operatorname{H}^{0}(C,\Omega^{1}_{C}(D))\subset W_{D,p}
surjects onto
{}_{0}{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{D}
.
∎

(3.5.41)
Corollary.
For
p\in C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\setminus{}D
non-Weierstrass, we have a natural isomorphism

(3.5.41.1)

\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)\simeq W_{D,p}\oplus{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{E}_{0},

which gives a splitting of the short exact sequence

(3.5.41.2)

0\to\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{AdR}}^{0}(E)_{0}\to\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)\to\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D)\to 0

that depends only on
p
(but it
does
depend on
p
).

Proof.
Since (
3.5.40.1
) is an isomorphism, there are no exact forms in
W_{D,p}
. Thus the map
W_{D,p}\to\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E):\eta\mapsto[\eta,0]
, using the identification (
3.4.32.1
), is injective. This gives the splitting in question, in light of Proposition (
3.5
). The identification
\operatorname{H}_{\operatorname{AdR}}^{0}(E)_{0}\simeq{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{E}_{0}
is definitional (
3.4
).
∎

(3.5.42) Using the splitting in (
3.5.41.1
), we can make the weight and Hodge filtrations on
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)
explicit. The non-trivial pieces are thus:

(3.5.42.1)

W_{0}=0\oplus{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{E}_{0},\quad W_{1}=W_{\emptyset,p}\oplus{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{E}_{0},\quad F^{1}=\operatorname{H}^{0}(C,\Omega^{1}_{C}(D))\oplus 0.

3.6.
Computing a basis for algebraic de Rham cohomology

(3.6.43) In this subsection, we will give references to algorithms that allow us to work effectively with linear systems on curves. We wish to emphasize that a basis for
W_{D,p}
(
3.5.39.1
) can be computed effectively as a result.

(3.6.44) For
f\in\kappa(C)
let
\divv(f)
be the associated divisor of zeros and poles of
f
. For any divisor
D^{\prime}
on
C
, we write

(3.6.44.1)

L(D^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{f\in\kappa(C)\mid\divv(f)+D^{\prime}\geq 0\}\subset\kappa(C),

and note that
L(D^{\prime})=\operatorname{H}^{0}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C}(D^{\prime}))
.

(3.6.45) We can algorithmically
[
Hes02
]
evaluate the map

(3.6.45.1)

D^{\prime}\mapsto L(D^{\prime}),

which takes a divisor supported on
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
points of
C
and outputs (a basis for) the linear system
L(D^{\prime})
.

(3.6.46) Given
C
(or, rather, a birational plane model
P(x,y)=0
) we can determine a basis for the global sections of the canonical bundle as lying in the space of rational differentials:

(3.6.46.1)

\operatorname{H}^{0}(C,\Omega^{1}_{C})\subset\Omega^{1}_{\kappa(C)/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}=\kappa(C)\mathrm{d}x,

this is explained in
[
ACGH85
, Appendix A, §2]
.

(3.6.47) For any divisor
D_{1}
we can compute a basis for the global sections of
\Omega^{1}_{C}(D_{1})
as follows. Find (
3.6
) a non-zero section
\omega_{0}\in\operatorname{H}^{0}(C,\Omega^{1}_{C})
and determine its divisor of zeros
D_{0}
. The space
\operatorname{H}^{0}(C,\Omega^{1}_{C}(D_{1}))
is identified with
L(D_{0}+D_{1})\subset\kappa(C)
through multiplication with
\omega_{0}
, i.e.,

(3.6.47.1)

\operatorname{H}^{0}(C,\Omega^{1}_{C}(D_{1}))=L(D_{0}+D_{1})\cdot\omega_{0}\subset\Omega^{1}_{\kappa(C)/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}.

Now use (
3.6
).

(3.6.48)
Proposition.
Given disjoint finite sets
D,E\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
, we can effectively compute a differential basis for
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)
together with the Hodge and weight filtrations.

Proof.
Find a non-Weierstrass point
p\in C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\setminus{}D
(
3.5
).
Compute a basis for
\operatorname{H}^{0}(C,\Omega^{1}_{C}(D+(g+1)p))
(
3.6
). Compute the residue at
p
for each basis element (
3.3
) and compute the kernel
W_{D,p}
. Now use the identity (
3.5.41.1
) to get a differential basis. The Hodge and weight filtrations are (
3.5
). ∎

3.7.
Reduction algorithms

(3.7.49) A crucial point for our algorithms is to represent a rational differential in terms of a basis for an algebraic de Rham cohomology. We will call this a reduction algorithm.

(3.7.50) The curve
C
is irreducible smooth projective and defined by a plane model
P(x,y)=0
. Fix
D,E\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
, disjoint finite sets. Let
p\in C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\setminus{}D
be non-Weierstrass.

(3.7.51) Any rational differential
\mu\in\Omega^{1}_{\kappa(C)/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}
with
\Res(\mu)
supported on
D
defines a cohomology class
[\mu,0]\in\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)
via (
3.4
). By Corollary (
3.5
), there exists a unique element
\eta\in W_{D,p}
and a rational function
f\in\kappa(C)
such that
\eta\equiv\mu+\mathrm{d}f
. Thus
(\mu,0)\equiv(\eta,-f|_{E})
in
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)
. A
reduction algorithm
takes
\mu
as input and returns the pair
\eta\in W_{D,p}
and
[-f|_{E}]\in{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{E}_{0}
.

(3.7.52)
A symbolic reduction algorithm.

Express
\mu=h\mathrm{d}x
and write
\mathrm{d}f=f^{\prime}\mathrm{d}x
(for unknown
f
and
f^{\prime}
, where
f^{\prime}=f_{x}-f_{y}P_{x}/P_{y}
). We recall that
W_{D,p}\subset\operatorname{H}^{0}(C,\Omega^{1}_{C}(D+(g+1)p))
.

We have
\eta=\mu+\mathrm{d}f=(h+f^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x
. Here
f^{\prime}
is uniquely determined by the property that
\divv(\eta)+D+(g+1)p\geq 0
, that is

(3.7.52.1)

\divv(h+f^{\prime})+D^{\prime}\geq 0,\quad\text{where }D^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\divv(\mathrm{d}x)+D+(g+1)p.

But
\divv(f^{\prime})\geq\min(\divv(h),\divv(h+f^{\prime}))
, where minimum is applied pointwise. Let

(3.7.52.2)

D^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\min(\divv(h),-D^{\prime}).

Therefore,
f^{\prime}\in L(D^{\prime\prime})
is the unique element (up to scaling) such that
\divv(h+f^{\prime})+D^{\prime}\geq 0
. Note that the residue at
p
is automatically
0
.

We can compute the Laurent tails of
h
and of a basis for
L(D^{\prime\prime})
along the negative part of
D^{\prime\prime}
. Since
f^{\prime}
is the element whose Laurent tail kills the offending poles of
h
, we can now determine the correct multiple of
f^{\prime}
.

Given the
\log(D)
-second kind differential
\mu=h\mathrm{d}x
, the output of this computation will be
\mathrm{d}f=f^{\prime}\mathrm{d}x
such that
\eta\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mu+\mathrm{d}f\in W_{D,p}
.

From the poles of
f^{\prime}
, we can determine a finite dimensional linear system containing
f
, and thus determine
f
up to additive constants. Restricting
f
gives the well-defined class
[f|_{E}]\in{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{E}_{0}
.

(3.7.53)
A numerical reduction algorithm.
This time, we will reconstruct
\eta
from numerical approximations. Despite the heuristic intermediate steps, the output certifies itself.

Find a basis
\{\omega_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
for
W_{D,p}\subset\operatorname{H}^{0}(C,\Omega^{1}_{C}(D+(g+1)p))
and a basis of rectilinear chains for
\{\gamma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
for
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D,E)
avoiding
p
(
4.4
).
Compute (a numerical approximation of) the period matrix
\left(\int_{\gamma_{j}}\omega_{i}\right)_{i,j=1,\dots,k}
and (a numerical approximation of) the row vector
\left(\int_{\gamma_{j}}\mu\right)_{j=1,\dots,k}
. Let
a=(a_{i})\in{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{k}
be the row vector satisfying

(3.7.53.1)

a=\left(\int_{\gamma_{j}}\mu\right)_{j=1,\dots,k}\left(\int_{\gamma_{j}}\omega_{i}\right)_{i,j=1,\dots,k}^{-1}.

Then
\eta=\sum_{i}a_{i}\omega_{i}
.

In practice, we will reconstruct, i.e., guess,
a
from a numerical approximation. To prove our guess, we make the following observations. If
\eta=\mu+\mathrm{d}f
then

(3.7.53.2)

\divv(f^{\prime})=\divv(\eta-\mu)-\divv(\mathrm{d}x)=:D^{\prime\prime\prime}

Therefore,
D^{\prime\prime\prime}
is a principal divisor and
f^{\prime}\in L(D^{\prime\prime\prime})\simeq{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
. We can recover the correct multiple of
f^{\prime}
in
L(D^{\prime\prime\prime})
by making sure the identity
f^{\prime}\mathrm{d}x=\eta-\mu
holds at a general point
q\in C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
.

For
\eta
that is merely formulated as a guess, we can construct the linear system
L(D^{\prime\prime\prime})
and try to find
f^{\prime}
such that
\eta=\omega+f^{\prime}\mathrm{d}x
. Failure to find
f^{\prime}
means the guess is incorrect. We must go back and try to reconstruct
a
using higher precision and try to find
f^{\prime}
again. Since
\eta
with desired properties exists, this process must terminate.

3.8.
Cohomological pullback and the transfer map

(3.8.54) Let
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
be two irreducible smooth proper curves over
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
. Let
f\colon C_{1}\to C_{2}
be a non-constant map between the curves. The curves are represented by their function fields
\kappa(C_{i})
(
3.1
) and so we define a
representation
of
f
to be the inclusion of function fields
f^{*}\colon\kappa(C_{2})\to\kappa(C_{1})
in terms of the generators. The effective pullback map on functions gives an effective pullback map on differential forms
f^{*}\colon\Omega^{1}_{\kappa(C_{1})/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}\to\Omega^{1}_{\kappa(C_{2})/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}
via
f^{*}(r(x,y)\mathrm{d}x)=f^{*}(r(x,y))\mathrm{d}f^{*}(x)
.

(3.8.55) For each
i=1,2
let
D_{i},E_{i}\subset C_{i}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
be disjoint sets. Suppose
f^{-1}(D_{2})\subset D_{1}
and
f(E_{1})\subset E_{2}
. Then,
f
can be viewed as a map
f\colon(C_{1}\setminus{}D_{1},E_{1})\to(C_{2}\setminus{}D_{2},E_{2})
. Thus, we have the pullback map on cohomology
f^{*}_{\operatorname{AdR}}\colon\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C_{2}\setminus{}D_{2},E_{2})\to\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C_{1}\setminus{}D_{1},E_{1})
.

(3.8.56)
Proposition.
Given
f
as in (
3.8
) and bases for the cohomology groups
\operatorname{H}^{i}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C_{i}\setminus{}D_{i},E_{i})
as in (
3.5
), we can determine the matrix representing the cohomological pullback
f^{*}_{\operatorname{AdR}}
with respect to the given bases.

Proof.

Using the representation of
f
(
3.8
), compute the pullback
f^{*}\colon W_{D_{2},p_{2}}\to\Omega^{1}_{\kappa(C_{1})/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}
. Now apply a reduction algorithm (
3.7
) to express a basis for
W_{D_{2},p_{2}}
in terms of the basis for
W_{D_{1},p_{1}}\oplus{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}_{0}^{E_{1}}
. The map
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}_{0}^{E_{2}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}_{0}^{E_{1}}
descends from the pullback map of
f|_{E_{1}}\colon E_{1}\to E_{2}
.
∎

(3.8.57) Given non-constant
f\colon C_{1}\to C_{2}
, the field extension
f^{*}\colon\kappa(C_{2})\to\kappa(C_{1})
is finite and hence we have the
trace map

\tr_{f}\colon\kappa(C_{1})\to\kappa(C_{2})
. The trace is effective on individual elements: given a representation of
f
and an element
a\in\kappa(C_{1})
we can compute the trace
\tr_{f}(a)
.

(3.8.58) The trace map extends to Kähler differentials
\tr_{f}\colon\Omega^{1}_{\kappa(C_{1})/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}\to\Omega^{1}_{\kappa(C_{2})/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}
where we have
\tr_{f}(r\mathrm{d}x)=\tr_{f}(r)\mathrm{d}\tr_{f}(x)
.

(3.8.59) For each
i=1,2
let
D_{i},E_{i}\subset C_{i}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
be disjoint sets. This time, suppose
f^{-1}(E_{2})\subset E_{1}
and
f(D_{1})\subset D_{2}
. The trace map on differentials induces the
transfer map

(3.8.59.1)

f_{!}^{\operatorname{AdR}}\colon\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C_{1}\setminus{}D_{1},E_{1})\to\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C_{2}\setminus{}D_{2},E_{2}).

(3.8.60)
Remark.
The transfer map
f_{!}^{\operatorname{AdR}}
is also known as the “integration over fibers” or the “Gysin” map.

(3.8.61)
Remark.
Observe that our hypotheses give a map
f\colon(C_{1}\setminus{}E_{1},D_{1})\to(C_{2}\setminus{}E_{2},D_{2})
. The Poincaré–Lefschetz duality takes the form
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C_{i}\setminus{}E_{i},D_{i})\simeq\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C_{i}\setminus{}D_{i},E_{i})^{\vee}
. Taking the dual of cohomological pullback
f^{*}_{\operatorname{AdR}}
and composing with the Poincaré–Lefschetz dualities gives the transfer map.

(3.8.62)
Proposition.
As in (
3.8
), we can compute the matrix representing the transfer map
f_{!}^{\operatorname{AdR}}
in given bases.

Proof.

The precise statement and the proof is similar to that of (
3.8
). On
W_{C_{1},p_{1}}\to\Omega^{1}_{\kappa(C_{2})/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}
we use the trace map and then apply the reduction algorithm. Write
f^{*}\colon\Div(C_{2})\to\Div(C_{1})
for the pullback on divisors and extend the action of
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{E_{1}}
additively to divisors supported on
E_{1}
. Then the restriction
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{E_{1}}_{0}\to{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{E_{2}}_{0}
of the trace map descends from

(3.8.62.1)

a\mapsto\left(e\mapsto a(f^{*}(e)\right),

which is clearly effective.
∎

4.
Betti homology and the mixed Hodge structure of a punctured marked curve

(4.0.1) Let
(C\setminus D,E)
be a punctured marked curve over
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
. In this section, we complete the effective construction of the mixed Hodge structure

(4.0.1.1)

\operatorname{H}_{1}(C\setminus D,E)=(\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D,E),\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)^{\vee},\mathfrak{c}_{{\operatorname{B}},{\operatorname{AdR}}})

by giving an algorithm to compute a basis for the Betti (singular) homology (
4.4
) so that the comparison isomorphism can be approximated to arbitrary precision (
4.1
). We also give an algorithm to compute the coordinates of the homology class of a smooth
1
-chain in the given basis of homology (
4.5
). Furthermore, for a map
p
between punctured marked curves, we will describe how to compute the pushforward
p_{*}
and transfer
p^{!}
maps in homology, see (
4.5
) and (
4.5
). Combined with the corresponding results for
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}
, we get effective pushforward and transfer maps on the mixed Hodge structure
\operatorname{H}_{1}
of a punctured marked curve. In §
7
, we will use these to determine the action of correspondances on
\operatorname{H}_{1}
.

(4.0.2) The idea to compute the Betti homology is to embed a graph
\Gamma
in
C\setminus{}D
with “rectilinear edges” (
4.3
) and containing
E
such that cycles on
(\Gamma,E)
generate the integral Betti homology
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D,E;\mathbb{Z})
. We construct the graph so that the bounding cycles on
C\setminus{}D
are evident (
4.4
). The graph can be constructed to avoid a given finite set
S
of points in
C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\setminus{}E
. We pick
S
to support the poles of a differential basis (
3.5
) for
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)
. Thus, we may pair the cycles on
(\Gamma,E)
against the differential basis via integration to determine the period pairing (
3.4
).

(4.0.3) The problem of computing the singular homology of an algebraic curve from its defining equation, and pairing it with algebraic de Rham cohomology, has a long history, even algorithmically. Foundational work by Tretkoff–Tretkoff
[
TT84
]
introduced combinatorial methods via monodromy representations; later, Deconinck–van Hoeij
[
DvH01
]
developed a general symbolic-numeric algorithm for computing homology bases and period matrices. More recent work use embedded graphs and certified analytic continuation to construct homology bases, particularly for superelliptic or general plane curves, enabling high-precision period computations
[
BSZ19
,
MN18
]
.

(4.0.4) Our construction of
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus D,E)
generalizes and streamlines several of the ideas outlined above. The strategy is inspired by the work of Bruin, Sijsling, and Zotine
[
BSZ19
]
, who embed a graph on the curve to facilitate homology computations. We depart from their method by explicitly constructing bounding cycles supported on the graph, rather than computing the radical of an intersection pairing. Our formulation is also slightly more abstract, and we refer to their work for implementation-level details.

4.1.
Representing the mixed Hodge structure

(4.1.5) The
k
-th Betti homology group
\operatorname{H}_{k}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus D,E;\mathbb{Z})
denotes the singular homology of
C(\mathbb{C})\setminus D
relative to
E
, with integral coefficients. We will describe how to compute a representation of this homology group later in the section. We write
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{B}}
for the dual
(\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}})^{\vee}
. The tuple
\operatorname{H}_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}((\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{B}},W),(\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}},W,F),\varphi_{{\operatorname{AdR}},{\operatorname{B}}})
is a
(\mathbb{Q},{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
-mixed Hodge structure. Here, we explain how to represent the mixed Hodge structure.

(4.1.6) We
represent
the mixed Hodge structure

(4.1.6.1)

\operatorname{H}_{1}(C\setminus D,E)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left(\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus D,E),\,\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus D,E)^{\vee},\,\mathfrak{c}_{{\operatorname{B}},{\operatorname{AdR}}}\right)

by representing
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)
(
3.5
) and
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D,E)
(
4.4
).

(4.1.7) We can compute a representation of
\operatorname{H}_{1}(C\setminus D,E)
. Compute (
3.6
) a differential basis
(\omega_{1},a_{1}),\dots,(\omega_{m},a_{m})
for
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)
. Let
S\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\setminus(D\cup E)
be a finite set supporting the poles of
\omega_{i}
’s, e.g.,
S
consists of one non-Weierstrass point per component. Compute (
4.4
) a rectilinear basis
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{m}
for
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D,E;\mathbb{Z})
avoiding
S
.

(4.1.8) A representation of
\operatorname{H}_{1}(C\setminus{}D,E)
allow us to determine the comparison isomorphism
\mathfrak{c}_{{\operatorname{B}},{\operatorname{AdR}}}
up to desired precision. If the given rectilinear basis
\gamma_{i}
’s do not avoid the poles
S
of the differential basis, we can compute a rectilinear basis that does, and then compute (
4.5
) the change of coordinates between the two rectilinear bases. Therefore, we will now assume our rectilinear avoids the poles of the differential basis.

(4.1.9) The period pairing (
3.4
) is represented by the matrix

(4.1.9.1)

\begin{pmatrix}\int_{\gamma_{j}}(\omega_{i},a_{i})\end{pmatrix}_{i,j=1,\dots,N}

The integrals
\int_{\gamma_{j}}\omega_{i}
are readily approximated to desired degree of precision from the representations of
\gamma_{j}
and
\omega_{i}
, see for instance
[
BSZ19
]
. In fact, we can compute the integrals with rigorous error bounds, i.e., as complex balls containing the true value
[
Mez16
,
LMS19
]
.

(4.1.10) The period matrix represents the comparison isomorphism

(4.1.10.1)

\mathfrak{c}_{{\operatorname{B}},{\operatorname{AdR}}}\colon\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D,E;\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}\overset{\sim}{\to}\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)^{\vee}\otimes_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}\mathbb{C}.

(4.1.11) In theory, we typically work with the rational vector space
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus D,E)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus D,E;\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
, but the integral structure is crucial for algorithms involving lattices.

4.2.
Rectilinear chains

(4.2.12)
Definition.
Consider the interval
[0,1]\subset\mathbb{R}
embedded into
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
via
t\mapsto[t:1]
. Composing by a linear map
A\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
defines a smooth path
\ell_{A}\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
, which we will call a
line segment
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

(4.2.13) The end points
\ell_{A}(0)=A([0:1]),\,\ell_{A}(1)=A([1:1])\in\mathbb{P}^{1}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
of a line segment are necessarily algebraic.

(4.2.14) We will
represent
a line segment
\ell_{A}
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
by a
2\times 2
matrix of algebraic numbers inducing the linear map
A
.

(4.2.15) Let
C/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
be a smooth proper curve and
x\colon C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
a finite map. Let
d=\deg x
and
B\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
be the branch locus of
x
.

(4.2.16) Let
\ell_{A}
be a line segment on
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
with the interior
\ell_{A}(0,1)
disjoint from
B
. Then
x^{-1}(\ell_{A}(0,1))
has
d
disjoint components. The choice of a point
r\in x^{-1}(\ell_{A}(1/2))
identifies the unique component of
x^{-1}(\ell_{A}(0,1))
passing through
r
. Let
\ell_{A,r}\colon[0,1]\to C(\mathbb{C})
be the analytic continuation of
x^{-1}\circ\ell_{A}
passing through
r
. The map
\ell_{A,r}
is smooth on
(0,1)
and continuous at the boundary.

(4.2.17)
Definition.
Any map of the form
\ell_{A,r}
is a
line segment
on
C
with respect to
x
. We will not mention
x
when it is clear from context. The tuple
(A,r)

represents

\ell_{A,r}
.

(4.2.18) Observe that
\ell_{A}(1/2)\in\mathbb{P}^{1}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
and thus
r\in C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
. Therefore, the tuple
(A,r)
is finitely presented.

(4.2.19) Let
\omega
be a rational differential on
C
and suppose that the line segment
\ell_{A,r}
avoids the poles of
\omega
. Then
\ell_{A,r}^{*}\omega
is continuous on
[0,1]
because the
x
-coordinate of
\ell_{A,r}
is smooth at the boundary and
\omega=f(x,y)\mathrm{d}x
. In particular, the integral of
\omega
over
\ell_{A,r}
is well-defined.

(4.2.20)
Definition.
A
rectilinear chain

\gamma=\sum_{i}a_{i}\ell_{A_{i},r_{i}}
on
C
with respect to
x
is a formal
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combination of line segments on
C
with respect to
x
. We will not mention
x
when it is clear from context. The
representation of
\gamma
is the formal
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combination
\sum_{i}a_{i}(A_{i},r_{i})
of the representations of the line segments.

(4.2.21) All “corners,” and in particular the end points, of a rectilinear chain are algebraic (
4.2
).

4.3.
Rectilinear embedded graphs in curves

(4.3.22)
Definition.
A
rectilinear basis
for
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D,E)
is a tuple
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{m}
of rectilinear chains (
4.2
) whose homology classes form a basis. We will say that a rectilinear basis
avoids a set
S\subset C(\mathbb{C})
if the support of the chains are disjoint from
S
.

(4.3.23) In this subsection, we will reformulate the problem of finding a rectilinear basis (
4.3
). In the next subsection, we will show how to compute a rectilinear basis (
4.4
).

(4.3.24) A finite oriented graph
\Gamma=(s,t\colon\Gamma_{E}\to\Gamma_{V})
is an ordered pair of maps between two finite sets, from the “edge set”
\Gamma_{E}
to the “vertex set”
\Gamma_{V}
, with the maps
s,t
assigning the two “ends” of an edge.

(4.3.25) Let
\mathbb{Z}[\Gamma_{E}],\mathbb{Z}[\Gamma_{V}]
be the formal
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combinations of the edges and vertices of
\Gamma
.
The simplicial homology of the associated topological space
|\Gamma|
is computed via the homology of the complex

(4.3.25.1)

0\to\mathbb{Z}[\Gamma_{E}]\overset{t-s}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{Z}[\Gamma_{V}]\to 0.

Thus
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(|\Gamma|;\mathbb{Z})\simeq\ker(t-s)
and
\operatorname{H}_{0}^{\operatorname{B}}(|\Gamma|;\mathbb{Z})\simeq\coker(t-s)
. Evidently, given
\Gamma
, the homology groups are computable.

(4.3.26) Let
A\subset\Gamma_{V}
be a subset. The relative homology of the pair
(|\Gamma|,A)
is computed by the complex attained by replacing
\mathbb{Z}[\Gamma_{V}]
with
\mathbb{Z}[\Gamma_{V}]/\mathbb{Z}[A]
in (
4.3.25.1
).

(4.3.27) An
embedded graph
of the punctured marked curve
(C\setminus{}D,E)
is a finite oriented graph
\Gamma
, a subset
A\subset\Gamma_{V}
, and a topological embedding
\iota\colon(|\Gamma|,A)\to(C\setminus{}D,E)
.

(4.3.28) For each edge
e\in\Gamma_{E}
of
\Gamma
, denote the corresponding
1
-simplex by
\phi_{e}\colon[0,1]\to|\Gamma|
.

(4.3.29) Let
x\colon C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
be a finite map. An embedded graph
(\Gamma,A,\iota)
is rectilinear with respect to
x
if each parametrized edge
\iota\circ\phi_{e}\colon[0,1]\to C(\mathbb{C})
is a line segment with respect to
x
(
4.2
).

(4.3.30)
Definition.
A
representation
of
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D,E;\mathbb{Z})
is a rectilinear embedded graph
\iota\colon(\Gamma,A)\hookrightarrow(C\setminus{}D,E)
and a subgroup
K\subset\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(|\Gamma|,A;\mathbb{Z})
such that the pushforward map

(4.3.30.1)

\iota_{*}\colon\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(|\Gamma|,A;\mathbb{Z})\to\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D,E;\mathbb{Z})

induces an isomorphism

(4.3.30.2)

\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(|\Gamma|,A;\mathbb{Z})/K\simeq\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D,E;\mathbb{Z}).

(4.3.31) Given a representation of homology, we can immediately determine a rectilinear basis for homology. Indeed, using the notation from (
4.3
), find a basis for
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(|\Gamma|,A;\mathbb{Z})/K
, split the surjection
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(|\Gamma|,A;\mathbb{Z})\to\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(|\Gamma|,A;\mathbb{Z})/K
, and embed the generators to
C(\mathbb{C})
via
\iota
.

4.4.
Constructing a representation of the homology of a curve

(4.4.32) Let
C
be a smooth proper curve and
D,E\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
disjoint sets. Fix a finite map
x\colon C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
and a finite set
S\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\setminus E
. Let
B\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
be the branch locus of
x
. This subsection is dedicated to proving the following statement.

(4.4.33)
Proposition.
Given
(C\setminus{}D,E)
we can effectively compute a rectilinear basis (
4.3
) for
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D,E;\mathbb{Z})
avoiding
S
. The weight filtration (
4.4
) on homology is effective.

(4.4.34) In light of (
4.3
), it suffices to construct a representation of homology. We will first perform this construction when
D=E=\emptyset
.

(4.4.35) If
B=\emptyset
then
C
is a disjoint union of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
’s and
x
is an isomorphism on each component. In this case,
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C)=0
and we may take the empty graph to represent the homology. We will exclude this case below by assuming
B\neq\emptyset
.

(4.4.36) Let
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus{}B
be a rectilinear embedded graph and a deformation retract of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus{}B
. This is effective, e.g., take the boundary of a Voronoi diagram around
B\neq\emptyset
. For each
b\in B
, there is a unique component
R_{b}
of
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})\setminus{}\Gamma
containing
b
. Each
R_{b}
is homeomorphic to a disk with boundary
\partial R_{b}
on
\Gamma
. We thus realize
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
as a
2
-dimensional CW-complex.

(4.4.37) The preimage
\widetilde{\Gamma}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}x^{-1}\Gamma
in
C(\mathbb{C})
is a rectilinear embedded graph of
C
. Define
K\subset\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{\Gamma}_{E}]
to be the subgroup generated by the boundary components of
x^{-1}R_{b}
for each
b\in B
.

(4.4.38)
Remark.
Lifting the edges constituting
\partial R_{b}
and describing how they connect is part of the computation required in constructing
\widetilde{\Gamma}
, thus obtaining
K
is essentially free.

(4.4.39)
Proposition.
Then,
\widetilde{\Gamma}\hookrightarrow C
together with
K
as constructed in (
4.4
) is a representation (
4.3
) of
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C;\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

For each
b\in B
, the map
x^{-1}R_{b}\to R_{b}
is a finite cover of a disk branched only over
b
. The Galois theory of a punctured disk implies that
x^{-1}R_{b}
is a union of disks (with boundary on
\widetilde{\Gamma}
). The graph
\widetilde{\Gamma}
together with the components of
x^{-1}R_{b}
for each
b
give
C
the structure of a CW-complex. The
1
-cells are
\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{\Gamma}_{E}]
and the boundary
1
-cells
K
are precisely those coming from the boundaries of
x^{-1}R_{b}
’s.
∎

(4.4.40)
Remark.
In
[
BSZ19
]
, the kernel
K
is computed by finding a lift of the intersection product on
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C;\mathbb{Z})
to
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(|\Gamma|;\mathbb{Z})
and then taking
K
to be the radical of this intersection pairing.

(4.4.41)
Remark.
There is another strategy for constructing a graph
\Gamma
in
C
to determine its homology, which has proven exceptionally efficient for “superelliptic” covers of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Instead of the pullback of a Voronoi diagram in
\mathbb{P}^{1}
around branch points, this construction takes a tree with vertices on the branch locus. The complement of the tree is homeomorphic to a disk and its preimage in
C
will be (unbranched) copies of that disk. The boundaries of those disks will give the kernel
K
.

(4.4.42) Now we fix two finite disjoint sets
D,E\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
.

(4.4.43) Start with a rectilinear graph
\Gamma
in
\mathbb{P}^{1}
which is a deformation retract of
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})\setminus{}\left(B\cup x(D)\right)
. Enlarge
\Gamma
by adding, for each point
q
in
x(E)
, a new vertex at
q
and a line segment connecting
q
to the nearest vertex of
\Gamma
(if
q
is not already on an edge, otherwise split the edge at
q
). Let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be this new graph. Once again, for each
b\in B\cup x(D)
there is a unique component
R_{b}
of
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})\setminus{}\Gamma^{\prime}
containing
b
and homeomorphic to a disk.

(4.4.44) Let
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}x^{-1}\Gamma^{\prime}
be the preimage of
\Gamma^{\prime}
.
The pair
(\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\prime},E)
is a rectilinear embedded graph of
(C\setminus{}D,E)
. Let
K\subset\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{E}]
be spanned by the boundaries of the components of
x^{-1}R_{b}
for
b\in(B\cup x(D))

except
when the component contains a point of
D
, in which case we do not add its boundary to
K
.

(4.4.45)
Proposition.
The embedded graph
(\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\prime},E)
of
(C\setminus{}D,E)
together with the kernel
K
constructed above is a
representation
of the homology
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D,E;\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to that of Proposition (
4.4
). This time, we realized a deformation retract of
C\setminus{}D
as a CW complex (by omitting the disks containing points of
D
). This completes the proof when
E=\emptyset
.

In general, on
(C\setminus{}D,E)
we will have a relative cycle connecting any two points of
E
lying on the same component of
C
.

We only need to show that the same is true on
(\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\prime},E)
. Which follows if
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\prime}
is connected whenever
C
is connected. But the
\operatorname{H}_{0}^{\operatorname{B}}
of both
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\prime}
and
C
agree as the
2
-cells of
C
do not change
\operatorname{H}_{0}
.
∎

(4.4.46)
Remark.
Clearly, we can choose
\Gamma^{\prime}
so that it avoids the images of a given finite set
S\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\setminus{}E
of points. For instance, start with a Voronoi diagram
\Gamma
around
B\cup x(D)\cup x(S)
, form
\Gamma^{\prime}
by adding edges ending in
E
as before (
4.4
). Then
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\prime}
is obtained by deleting from
x^{-1}\Gamma^{\prime}
any leaves ending in
S
. These leaves appear only when
x(S)
and
x(E)
intersect. The preimages of the
2
-cells in
\mathbb{P}^{1}
provide us with the generators for
K
as before.

(4.4.47) The weight filtration on
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D,E;\mathbb{Z})
is as follows

(4.4.47.1)

\begin{array}[]{ccc}W_{-2}&W_{-1}&W_{0}\\
\operatorname{H}_{0}^{\operatorname{B}}(D)_{0}&\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D)&\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D,E)\end{array}

where
\operatorname{H}_{0}^{\operatorname{B}}(D)_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{H}_{0}^{\operatorname{B}}(D)/\operatorname{H}_{0}^{\operatorname{B}}(C)
denotes the primitive homology of
D
in
C
, and its image in the homology of
C\setminus{}D
is generated by the loops around
D
.

(4.4.48) Our graph
(\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\prime},E)
can be used to determine the weight filtration on
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\prime},E)/K
, simply take
W_{-2}
to be generated by the loops around
D
and
W_{-1}
to be generated by cycles without boundary.

(4.4.49) This concludes the proof of Proposition (
4.4
).

4.5.
Reduction, pushforward, and transfer maps in homology

(4.5.50) Let
f\colon C_{1}\to C_{2}
be a morphism of smooth proper curves over
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
. For each
i=1,2
, let
D_{i},E_{i}\subset C_{i}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
be finite disjoint sets. Recall that
f
is represented by giving the inclusion of the function fields of respective components.

(4.5.51) If
f^{-1}(D_{2})\subset D_{1}
and
f(E_{1})\subset E_{2}
then we have a map
f\colon(C_{1}\setminus{}D_{1},E_{1})\to(C_{2}\setminus{}D_{2},E_{2})
which induces the homological pushforward map

(4.5.51.1)

f_{*}^{\operatorname{B}}\colon\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C_{1}\setminus{}D_{1},E_{1})\to\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C_{2}\setminus{}D_{2},E_{2}).

(4.5.52)
Proposition.
Suppose that for each
i=1,2
, we are given a rectilinear basis (
4.3
) for the homology of
(C_{i}\setminus{}D_{i},E_{i})
with respect to a map
x_{i}\colon C_{i}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Given a representation of
f\colon(C_{1}\setminus{}D_{1},E_{1})\to(C_{2}\setminus{}D_{2},E_{2})
