, we can effectively compute the integer matrix representing the homological pushforward
f_{*}^{\operatorname{B}}
in the given rectilinear bases. In particular, when
f
is the identity map, we can compute the change of coordinates between two bases for the homology.

Proof.

Compute (
3.6
) a basis for the cohomology groups
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C_{i}\setminus{}D_{i},E_{i})
and compute (
3.8
) the matrix representing the cohomological pullback
f^{*}_{\operatorname{AdR}}
.

Integrating the cohomology basis against the rectilinear bases, compute (
4.1
) a complex ball matrix representing the comparison isomorphisms

(4.5.52.1)

\mathfrak{c}_{i}\colon\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C_{i}\setminus{}D_{i},E_{i})\otimes\mathbb{C}\overset{\sim}{\to}\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{B}}(C_{i}\setminus{}D_{i},E_{i})\otimes\mathbb{C}.

Compatibility between the pullback maps in cohomology gives the identity

(4.5.52.2)

f^{*}_{\operatorname{B}}=\mathfrak{c}_{1}\circ f^{*}_{\operatorname{AdR}}\circ\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{-1}.

Our approximation of the
\mathfrak{c}_{i}
’s will give a complex ball matrix representing the right hand side of (
4.5.52.2
) in the basis dual to the rectilinear bases. Increasing the precision of integration if necessary, we may assume the radius of the complex balls are less than
1/2
. The unique integers contained in each of these complex balls recovers the integer matrix representing
f^{*}_{\operatorname{B}}
, with transpose
f_{*}^{\operatorname{B}}
.
∎

(4.5.53)
Remark.
Even when
C\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}C_{1}=C_{2}
and
f=\id
, if
x_{1},x_{2}\colon C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
are different maps, it would be extremely cumbersome to try and find a homological equivalence between two rectilinear bases with respect to
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
. This would involve constructing
2
-chains bounded by “semi-algebraic”
1
-chains. The path through cohomology is cleaner.

(4.5.54) Suppose
f\colon C_{1}\to C_{2}
is such that
f^{-1}(E_{2})\subset E_{1}
and
f(D_{1})\subset D_{2}
. Then we get the pushforward map

(4.5.54.1)

f^{*}_{\operatorname{B}}\colon\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C_{1}\setminus{}E_{1},D_{1})\to\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C_{2}\setminus{}E_{2},D_{2}).

Take duals and use the Poincaré–Lefschetz duality
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C_{i}\setminus{}E_{i},D_{i})^{\vee}\simeq\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C_{i}\setminus{}D_{i},E_{i})
to get the
homological transfer map

(4.5.54.2)

f^{!}_{\operatorname{B}}\colon\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C_{2}\setminus{}D_{2},E_{2})\to\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C_{1}\setminus{}D_{1},E_{1}).

Unwinding the definition, this map can be described by taking the total preimage of chains from
C_{2}
to
C_{1}
.
The dual of this map is called the
cohomological transfer map

f_{!}^{\operatorname{B}}
.

(4.5.55)
Proposition.
We can effectively compute the integer matrix representing the homological transfer map with respect to given rectilinear homology bases.

Proof.

Use the same outline of proof as (
4.5
). Except, we evoke the compatibility of the transfer maps
f_{!}^{\operatorname{AdR}}
and
f_{!}^{\operatorname{B}}
.
∎

(4.5.56)
Reduction algorithm for
1
-chains.
Let
\gamma
be a smooth
1
-chain on
C(\mathbb{C})\setminus D
with boundary on
E
. Given a rectilinear basis for
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D,E)
, we can effectively compute the integer coordinates of the homology class
[\gamma]
in the given basis. The only assumption on the representation of
\gamma
is that we should be able to approximate integrals of rational differentials along
\gamma
to arbitrary precision.

Proof.

We may assume
C
is irreducible. Find a non-Weierstrass
p\in C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
not lying on
\gamma
and not on the given rectilinear basis (
3.5
). Compute a basis for
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)
with poles only on
p
(
3.5.41.1
), (
3.6
). By integration (
4.1
), approximate the comparison isomorphism
\mathfrak{c}_{{\operatorname{B}},{\operatorname{AdR}}}
with respect to the bases at hand. Integrate the cohomology basis against
\gamma
to get a complex ball vector
\int_{\gamma}\in(\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}\otimes\mathbb{C})^{\vee}
. Now apply the inverse of
\mathfrak{c}_{{\operatorname{B}},{\operatorname{AdR}}}
to
\int_{\gamma}
. The unique integer vector in the resulting complex ball vector containing
\mathfrak{c}_{{\operatorname{B}},{\operatorname{AdR}}}^{-1}(\int_{\gamma})
gives the desired coordinates. If the integer vector in the complex ball vector is not unique, increase the precision with which
\mathfrak{c}_{{\operatorname{B}},{\operatorname{AdR}}}
is approximated and repeat.
∎

5.
Relations between the periods of
1
-motives

(5.0.1) This section first introduces the notation we will use when working with
1
-motives. It then proves Theorem (
5.6
), which states that, roughly speaking, if a
1
-motive
M
is at least as symmetric as the underlying abelian variety, then all relations between the periods of
M
arise from endomorphisms of
M
and from trivial relations (
5.6
).

(5.0.2) The focus of this section is purely theoretical: we do not concern ourselves with effectivity problems here. Our main result, Theorem (
5.6
), is a reinterpretation of parts of the arguments used in the proof of the “Dimension estimate”
[
HW22
, Theorem 15.3]
, one of the main theorems of
loc.cit.
Instead of estimating the dimension of the space of periods, we give a direct and explicit description of the space of period relations. We will later use this theoretical description to compute period relations explicitly, see Remark
5.6
.

5.1.
Definition of a
1
-motive

(5.1.3) Throughout this section, all varieties are defined over
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
. We set up the basic notions needed to define
1
-motives.

(5.1.4) A
lattice

L\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{\rk L}
is a free, finitely generated abelian group. An
algebraic torus

T
is an algebraic group such that
T\simeq\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\dim T}
, where
\mathbb{G}_{m}=\Spec({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}[t,t^{-1}])
denotes the multiplicative group.

(5.1.5) A
semi-abelian variety

G
is an algebraic group that is an extension of an abelian variety
A
by an algebraic torus
T
, that is,
0\to T\to G\to A\to 0
is exact.

(5.1.6) A
marking
on a semi-abelian variety
G
is a group homomorphism
\varphi\colon L\to G({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
from a lattice
L
to the group of
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
-points of
G
.

(5.1.7) A
1
-motive

M
is a two-term complex
[L\overset{\varphi}{\to}G]
, where
L
is a lattice,
G
is a semi-abelian variety, and
\varphi
is a marking.

(5.1.8) A
morphism
of
1
-motives is a morphism of complexes, i.e., a pair of morphisms between the underlying semi-abelian varieties and lattices that commute with the markings. We denote the category of
1
-motives by
1\text{-Mot}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. Morphisms in
1\text{-Mot}_{\mathbb{Z}}
are denoted
\mathop{Hom}\nolimits_{\mathbb{Z}}
(“integral morphisms”), and endomorphisms by
\End_{\mathbb{Z}}
.

(5.1.9) The underlying lattice
L
, the semi-abelian variety
G
, the toric part
T
, and the abelian core
A
are all functorially assigned to a
1
-motive. When discussing a
1
-motive
M
, we will refer to these pieces by the corresponding letters
L,\,T,\,A
.

(5.1.10) To work up to isogeny, we pass to the category
1\text{-Mot}
, the isogeny category of
1\text{-Mot}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, which is an abelian category
[
Del74
]
. Unless specified otherwise, we work with
1
-motives up to isogeny, i.e., in
1\text{-Mot}
. In particular,
\mathop{Hom}\nolimits=\mathop{Hom}\nolimits_{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes\mathbb{Q}
and
\End=\End_{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes\mathbb{Q}
refer to morphisms and endomorphisms in
1\text{-Mot}
as opposed to
1\text{-Mot}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. If we want to emphasize that we are in
1\text{-Mot}
, we will write
\mathop{Hom}\nolimits_{\mathbb{Q}}
and
\End_{\mathbb{Q}}
, in particular for abelian varieties considered up to isogeny.

5.2.
The mixed Hodge structure associated to a
1
-motive

(5.2.11) Let
(\mathbb{Z},{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\text{-MHS}
denote the category of
(\mathbb{Z},{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
-mixed Hodge structures. Its isogeny category is
(\mathbb{Q},{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\text{-MHS}
, the (abelian) category of
(\mathbb{Q},{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
-mixed Hodge structures. There is a duality functor
\bullet^{\vee}=\mathop{Hom}\nolimits_{(\mathbb{Q},{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\text{-MHS}}(\bullet,\mathbb{Q}(0))
, which commutes with the duality functors on (bi)filtered vector spaces.

(5.2.12) There is an “integral homology” functor

(5.2.12.1)

\operatorname{H}_{1}(\bullet;\mathbb{Z})\colon 1\text{-Mot}_{\mathbb{Z}}\to(\mathbb{Z},{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\text{-MHS},

which induces a fully faithful functor

(5.2.12.2)

\operatorname{H}_{1}\colon 1\text{-Mot}\to(\mathbb{Q},{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\text{-MHS},

and which restricts to the usual homology functor on semi-abelian varieties. The construction of
\operatorname{H}_{1}
over the complex numbers is due to
[
Del74
, (10.1.3)]
; the generalization to arbitrary characteristic zero fields appears in
[
And19
]
. See also
[
HW22
, Prop. 8.17]
for a proof over
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
.

(5.2.13) Composing
\operatorname{H}_{1}
with the duality functor yields a cohomology functor, which we denote by

(5.2.13.1)

\operatorname{H}^{1}(\bullet)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{H}_{1}(\bullet)^{\vee}.

(5.2.14) We denote by
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M)
the filtered
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space underlying
\operatorname{H}_{1}(M)
, and by
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M;\mathbb{Z})
the underlying filtered lattice. The bifiltered
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
-vector space underlying
\operatorname{H}_{1}(M)
is denoted by
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{AdR}}(M)
. Dually, we use
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{B}}
and
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}
for the corresponding components of
\operatorname{H}^{1}
.

(5.2.15)
Relation to curves.
Let
(C\setminus{}D,E)
be a relative curve. There is a functorial
1
-motive
J_{C,E}^{D}
associated to
(C\setminus{}D,E)
, whose abelian core is the Jacobian
J_{C}
of
C
; see (
6.1
). There is a natural isomorphism

(5.2.15.1)

\operatorname{H}^{1}(J_{C,E}^{D};\mathbb{Z})\simeq\operatorname{H}^{1}(C\setminus{}D,E;\mathbb{Z}),

see the proof of Lemma 12.9 in
[
HW22
]
. In practice, we will use the identification (
5.2.15.1
) and pushout/pullback constructions to represent the cohomology of motives via the cohomology of curves; see §
6
.

5.3.
Subgroup theorem for
1
-motives

(5.3.16) For a
1
-motive
M
, let
\mathcal{F}(M)=\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M)\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M)
denote the tensor product of Betti and algebraic de Rham realizations, called the
space of formal periods
of
M
. We define
\mathcal{F}_{1}(M)\subset\mathcal{F}(M)
to be the cone of rank-
1
tensors:

(5.3.16.1)

\mathcal{F}_{1}(M)=\{\gamma\otimes\omega:\gamma\in\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M),\,\omega\in\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M)\}.

(5.3.17)
Space of period relations.
The comparison isomorphism between Betti and de Rham realizations induces a
period pairing

(5.3.17.1)

\wp_{M}\colon\mathcal{F}(M)\to\mathbb{C}.

The kernel of
\wp_{M}
, denoted
\mathcal{R}(M)
, is the
space of period relations
of
M
. We define the
cone of rank-
1
relations
as
\mathcal{R}_{1}(M)=\mathcal{R}(M)\cap\mathcal{F}_{1}(M)
.

(5.3.18)
Functoriality of the period map.
The period pairing is compatible with morphisms of
1
-motives. If
f\colon M_{1}\to M_{2}
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-morphism, then for
\gamma\in\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M_{1})
and
\omega\in\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M_{2})
we have

(5.3.18.1)

\wp_{M_{2}}(f_{*}\gamma\otimes\omega)=\wp_{M_{1}}(\gamma\otimes f^{*}\omega),

where
f_{*}
and
f^{*}
denote the induced maps on Betti and de Rham realizations, respectively.

(5.3.19) Consider a short exact sequence

(5.3.19.1)

0\to M^{\prime}\overset{\iota}{\to}M\overset{\jmath}{\to}M^{\prime\prime}\to 0,

and elements
\gamma^{\prime}\in\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M^{\prime})
,
\omega^{\prime\prime}\in\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M^{\prime\prime})
. Then we have

(5.3.19.2)

\wp_{M}(\iota_{*}\gamma^{\prime}\otimes\jmath^{*}\omega^{\prime\prime})=\wp_{M^{\prime\prime}}(\jmath_{*}\iota_{*}\gamma^{\prime}\otimes\omega^{\prime\prime})=\wp_{M^{\prime\prime}}(0\otimes\omega^{\prime\prime})=0.

Therefore,
\iota_{*}\gamma^{\prime}\otimes\jmath^{*}\omega^{\prime\prime}
belongs to
\mathcal{R}_{1}(M)
.

(5.3.20) Let us denote by
\mathcal{R}_{\ses}(M)\subset\mathcal{R}_{1}(M)
the cone of all relations
\iota_{*}(\gamma^{\prime})\otimes\jmath^{*}(\omega^{\prime\prime})
arising from short exact sequences as in (
5.3
). The following is one of the key results of
[
HW22
]
.

(5.3.21)
Theorem.
(
[
HW22
, Theorem 9.7]
) There is an equality
\mathcal{R}_{\ses}(M)=\mathcal{R}_{1}(M)
. ∎

(5.3.22)
The case of abelian varieties.
When
M=[0\to A]
is an abelian variety, all short exact sequences with
M
in the middle split up to isogeny. Consequently, short exact sequences involving
A
in
1\text{-Mot}
are parametrized by the endomorphism algebra
\End(A)
, a finite-dimensional
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra. By Theorem (
5.3
), the space
\mathcal{R}_{1}(A)
can be computed using
\End(A)
and its action on homology and cohomology.

(5.3.23) In contrast to abelian varieties, not every short exact sequence of
1
-motives splits up to isogeny. Therefore, the computation of period relations will be more involved. We turn to this problem next.

5.4.
Cartier duality

(5.4.24) Cartier duality is a contravariant duality functor
M\mapsto M^{\vee}
on
1\text{-Mot}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, satisfying a natural equivalence
M\simeq(M^{\vee})^{\vee}

[
Del74
, (10.2.11)]
.

(5.4.25) Cartier duality on
1
-motives agrees with a Tate twist of duality on mixed Hodge structures. More precisely, there is a natural equivalence

(5.4.25.1)

\operatorname{H}_{1}(M^{\vee};\mathbb{Z})\simeq\Hom_{(\mathbb{Z},{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\text{-MHS}}(M,\mathbb{Z}(1)),

see
[
Del74
, (10.2.10)]
.

(5.4.26) The Cartier dual of an algebraic torus
T
is the motive
[\Xi(T)\to 0]
, where
\Xi(T)=\mathop{Hom}\nolimits(T,\mathbb{G}_{m})\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{\dim T}
is the
character lattice
of
T
.

(5.4.27) The Cartier dual of a lattice motive
[L\to 0]
is the algebraic torus
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{L}=\mathop{Hom}\nolimits(L,\mathbb{G}_{m})\simeq\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\rk L}
.

(5.4.28) If
G
is a semi-abelian variety with toric part
T\simeq\mathbb{G}_{m}
and abelian core
A
, then
G
can be viewed as a torsor under
\mathbb{G}_{m}
over
A
, defining an element of
\operatorname{H}^{1}(A,\mathcal{O}_{A}^{\times})
, and hence a point
p_{G}
on
A^{\vee}=\Pic^{0}(A)
. The Cartier dual of
G
is the
1
-motive

(5.4.28.1)

G^{\vee}=[\mathbb{Z}\overset{1\mapsto p_{G}}{\to}A^{\vee}].

(5.4.29) In general, the Cartier dual of a semi-abelian variety
G
, extending
A
by a torus
T
, is the
1
-motive

(5.4.29.1)

G^{\vee}=[\Xi(T)\to A^{\vee}],

where the marking
\Xi(T)\to A^{\vee}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
is defined as follows: for each
\ell\in\Xi(T)
, the pushout
\ell_{*}G
is an extension of
A
by
\mathbb{G}_{m}
, and the associated point
p_{\ell_{*}G}\in A^{\vee}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
defines the image of
\ell
.

(5.4.30) By (
5.4
), and the fact that Cartier duality is an equivalence, we see that it establishes a contravariant equivalence between
1
-motives of the second and third kind.

(5.4.31) If
M=[L\to G]
is a
1
-motive with toric part
T
and abelian core
A
, then the Cartier dual is a
1
-motive of the form

(5.4.31.1)

M^{\vee}=[\Xi(T)\to[L\to A]^{\vee}],

where
[L\to A]^{\vee}
is the Cartier dual of
[L\to A]
. Moreover, the quotient of
M^{\vee}
by its toric part
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{L}
recovers
G^{\vee}=[\Xi(T)\to A^{\vee}]
.

(5.4.32)
Explicit Cartier duality for curves.
Combining (
5.4
), Poincaré–Lefschetz duality (
3.8
), and the fact that
\operatorname{H}_{1}
is fully faithful (
5.2
), we see that the
1
-motive
J_{C,E}^{D}
associated to
(C\setminus{}D,E)
is Cartier dual to the motive
J_{C,D}^{E}
associated to
(C\setminus{}E,D)
. This will allow us to represent duals explicitly.

5.5.
Endomorphism types of motives

(5.5.33) An abelian variety is called a
1
-motive of the
first kind
(
T=0
and
L=0
). An abelian variety with a marking is a
1
-motive of the
second kind
(
T=0
). A semi-abelian variety is a
1
-motive of the
third kind
(
L=0
).

(5.5.34)
Definition.
A
1
-motive
M
of the second or third kind is
reduced
if and only if the natural map
\End(M)\to\End(A)
is injective, where
A
is the abelian core of
M
. A general
1
-motive
M=[L\to G]
is reduced if and only if its underlying second and third type motives,
M/T=[L\to A]
and
G
, are reduced.

(5.5.35)
Definition.
A
1
-motive
M
with abelian core
A
is
super-saturated
if the natural map
\End(M)\to\End(A)
is surjective. If
M
is reduced and super-saturated, then it is called
saturated
.

(5.5.36)
Definition.
A motive is a
Baker motive
if its abelian core is trivial,
A=0
.

(5.5.37)
Definition.
A motive is
composite
if it is a direct sum of a Baker motive and a saturated motive.

(5.5.38)
Remark.
Our definition of reduced agrees with that of Huber and Wüstholz (
5.5
). It is clear that our definition of saturated agrees with theirs.

(5.5.39)
Lemma.
A
1
-motive of the second kind
M=[L\to A]
is reduced if and only if the induced map

(5.5.39.1)

L\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}\to A({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}

is injective. A
1
-motive of the third kind
G
is reduced if and only if its Cartier dual
G^{\vee}=[\Xi(T)\to A^{\vee}]
is reduced.

Proof.
If (
5.5.39.1
) is injective, then any
\mathbb{Q}
-endomorphism of
A
that lifts, lifts uniquely to
L\otimes\mathbb{Q}
. Therefore, injectivity of the
\mathbb{Q}
-marking implies injectivity of
\End(M)\to\End(A)
for a second kind motive. Conversely, if there is a nontrivial kernel in the
\mathbb{Q}
-marking, then nonzero
\mathbb{Q}
-endomorphisms of the kernel can be extended to
M
in a way that maps to zero on
A
. For a third kind motive, apply the same argument to the Cartier dual.
∎

(5.5.40)
Remark.
A motive
M
can satisfy
\End(M)\simeq\End(A)
and still be non-reduced if
M
is not of the second or third kind. For instance, consider a simple abelian variety
A
with
\End(A)\simeq\mathbb{Q}
, and the motive
[\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}\oplus A]
where we mark a non-torsion point in each component. This motive has no nontrivial endomorphisms, but the underlying semi-abelian variety
\mathbb{G}_{m}\oplus A
is non-reduced.

5.6.
Expected period relations

(5.6.41) Let
M=[L\to G]
be a
1
-motive with toric part
T
and abelian core
A
.

(5.6.42)
Definition.
Consider the short exact sequence
T\hookrightarrow M\twoheadrightarrow[L\to A]
, and identify
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(T)
with its image in
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M)
. We define the
toric relations
as

(5.6.42.1)

\mathcal{R}_{T}(M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\ker\left(\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(T)\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M)\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(T)\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(T)\overset{\wp_{T}}{\to}\mathbb{C}\right).

(5.6.43) Note that
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(T)\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}([L\to A])
is contained in
\mathcal{R}_{T}(M)
, as it is the kernel of the projection onto
\mathcal{F}(T)
. The period map
\wp_{M}
restricted to
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(T)\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M)
factors through
\wp_{T}
by functoriality (
5.3
) applied to
T\hookrightarrow M
.

(5.6.44)
Definition.
Consider the short exact sequence
G\hookrightarrow M\twoheadrightarrow L
, and identify
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(L)
with its image in
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M)
. We define the
lattice relations
as

(5.6.44.1)

\mathcal{R}_{L}(M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\ker\left(\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M)\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(L)\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(L)\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(L)\overset{\wp_{L}}{\to}\mathbb{C}\right).

Observe that
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(G)\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(L)
is contained in
\mathcal{R}_{L}(M)
.

(5.6.45)
Definition.
We define the
trivial relations

\mathcal{R}_{\triv}(M)
as
\mathcal{R}_{\triv}(M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{R}_{L}(M)+\mathcal{R}_{T}(M)
.

(5.6.46)
Remark.
The space of trivial relations is straightforward to compute. For the toric relations, the pairing
\wp_{T}
is induced from the duality pairing
\Xi(T)^{\vee}\otimes\Xi(T)\to\mathbb{Z}
, tensored with
\mathbb{Q}\langle 2\pi{\tt i}\rangle
under the canonical identifications (
5.8
). The quotient leading up to
\wp_{T}
is simply the quotient by
W_{1}\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M)
. Similarly, for the lattice relations, we consider the
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
-tensor of the duality pairing
L\otimes L^{\vee}\to\mathbb{Z}
and the quotient by
W_{-1}\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M)
. See also (
5.7
).

(5.6.47)
Definition.
We define the
endo-relations

\mathcal{R}_{\End}(M)
as the subspace

(5.6.47.1)

\mathcal{R}_{\End}(M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\phi_{*}\gamma\otimes\omega-\gamma\otimes\phi^{*}\omega:\phi\in\End(M),\;\gamma\in\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M),\;\omega\in\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M)\},

which is finite-dimensional and contained in
\mathcal{R}(M)
by (
5.3
).

(5.6.48)
Definition.
We define the
expected relations
of
M
as

\mathcal{R}_{e}(M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{R}_{\End}(M)+\mathcal{R}_{\triv}(M)
.

(5.6.49)
Theorem.
If
M
is a saturated motive, then the space of period relations coincides with the expected relations, that is,
\mathcal{R}(M)=\mathcal{R}_{e}(M)
.

(5.6.50) The proof of this theorem will occupy the rest of this section. We also state the following corollary, which extends the theorem to composite motives; see (
5.9
) for a more explicit statement.

(5.6.51)
Corollary.
If
M
is a composite motive
M_{B}\oplus M^{{\operatorname{sat}}}
, then
\mathcal{R}(M)
is generated by the trivial relations, the cross-terms (
5.9
) arising from the direct sum decomposition, the Baker relations
\mathcal{R}(M_{B})
, and the expected relations of
M^{{\operatorname{sat}}}
.

Proof.
This is the content of (
5.9
), where we substitute
\mathcal{R}_{e}(M^{{\operatorname{sat}}})
for
\mathcal{R}(M^{{\operatorname{sat}}})
in light of Theorem (
5.6
).
∎

(5.6.52)
Remark.
Recall that the determination of
\mathcal{R}_{\triv}(M)
is straightforward (
5.6
). We will later show that the period relations of a Baker motive can be computed explicitly (
8.8
). The expected relations of a saturated motive can also be computed directly,
provided
that the action of the endomorphism algebra on (co)homology is known. If, further, one a direct sum decomposition
\operatorname{H}_{1}(M)=\operatorname{H}_{1}(M_{B})\oplus\operatorname{H}_{1}(M^{{\operatorname{sat}}})
, then
\mathcal{R}(M)
can be computed using Corollary (
5.6
).

5.7.
Filtrations and period matrices

(5.7.53) Let
M=[L\to G]
be a
1
-motive with toric part
T
and abelian core
A
.

(5.7.54) The
motivic weight filtration
on
M
is the ascending filtration

(5.7.54.1)

0=W_{-3}M\subset W_{-2}M=T\subset W_{-1}M=G\subset W_{0}M=M.

It induces the weight filtration on Betti homology, satisfying
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(W_{k}M)=W_{k}\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M)
.
The
motivic weight cofiltration

Q_{-k-1}M\coloneqq M/W_{k}M
gives

(5.7.54.2)

M\to[L\to A]\to[L\to 0]\to 0,

and induces the weight filtration on de Rham cohomology, satisfying
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(Q_{j}M)=W_{j}\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M)
.

(5.7.55) A basis
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
for a filtered vector space
V
with filtration
V_{\bullet}
is said to
respect the filtration
if for each
i
, the first
\dim V_{i}
elements
v_{1},\dots,v_{\dim V_{i}}
form a basis of
V_{i}
.

(5.7.56) The short exact sequences

(5.7.56.1)

0\to W_{k}M\to M\to Q_{-k-1}M\to 0

induce
weight relations
in the period relations:

(5.7.56.2)

W_{k}\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M)\otimes W_{-k-1}\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M)\subset\mathcal{R}(M).

(5.7.57)
Period matrix and block form.
Choose bases
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{m}
for
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M)
and
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{m}
for
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M)
, both respecting the weight filtrations.
The period isomorphism is represented by the
period matrix

(5.7.57.1)

\mathcal{P}_{M}=\left(\int_{\gamma_{j}}\omega_{i}\right)_{i,j=1,\dots,m}.

By the weight relations, the matrix
\mathcal{P}_{M}
has block form

(5.7.57.2)

\mathcal{P}_{M}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\mathcal{P}_{L}\\
0&\mathcal{P}_{A}&\mathcal{Q}_{AL}\\
\mathcal{P}_{T}&\mathcal{Q}_{TA}&\mathcal{Q}_{TL}\end{pmatrix},

where
\mathcal{P}_{T}
,
\mathcal{P}_{A}
, and
\mathcal{P}_{L}
are period matrices for
T
,
A
, and
L
, respectively.

(5.7.58) The only nontrivial weight relations in the period relations induced by the motivic weight filtration are
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(T)\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}([L\to A])
and
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(G)\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(L)
, and both are contained in the trivial relations
\mathcal{R}_{\triv}(M)
. In particular, the vanishing of the three
0
blocks in the period matrix
\mathcal{P}_{M}
follows from the trivial relations.

(5.7.59)
Toric and lattice periods.
We refer to the entries of
\mathcal{P}_{L}
as
lattice periods
and to the entries of
\mathcal{P}_{T}
as
toric periods
. The matrix
\mathcal{P}_{L}
consists of algebraic numbers, while
\mathcal{P}_{T}
consists of algebraic multiples of
2\pi{\tt i}
.

5.8.
Period relations of Baker motives

(5.8.60) If
T
is an algebraic torus with character lattice
\Xi(T)
, then there are canonical isomorphisms

(5.8.60.1)

\displaystyle\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(T)

\displaystyle=\Xi(T)^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(\mathbb{G}_{m})=\Xi(T)^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q},

(5.8.60.2)

\displaystyle\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(T)

\displaystyle=\Xi(T)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(\mathbb{G}_{m})=\Xi(T)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}.

The isomorphism (
5.8.60.2
) is obtained by pulling back
\mathrm{d}z/z
from
\mathbb{G}_{m}
along characters, and the isomorphism (
5.8.60.1
) is obtained by pushing forward loops along characters.
With respect to dual bases for
\Xi(T)
and
\Xi(T)^{\vee}
, the period matrix
\mathcal{P}_{T}
is a
2\pi{\tt i}
-multiple of the identity matrix.

(5.8.61) If
L
is a lattice, viewed as the
1
-motive
M=[L\to 0]
, then there are canonical isomorphisms

\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(L)=L\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q},\quad\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(L)=L^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}.

With respect to dual bases for
L
and
L^{\vee}
, the period matrix
\mathcal{P}_{L}
is the identity matrix.

(5.8.62) If
M=[L\to T]
is a Baker motive, then with respect to bases compatible with the weight filtrations, the period matrix
\mathcal{P}_{M}
has block form

(5.8.62.1)

\mathcal{P}_{M}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathcal{P}_{L}\\
\mathcal{P}_{T}&*\end{pmatrix},

where
\mathcal{P}_{L}
is a matrix of algebraic numbers,
\mathcal{P}_{T}
is a matrix of algebraic multiples of
2\pi{\tt i}
, and
*
denotes a matrix whose entries are logarithms of algebraic numbers.

5.9.
Independence statements for periods of saturated motives

(5.9.63) If
M
is saturated and the abelian core
A
is simple, we say that
M
is a
simple motive
. Then
E\coloneqq\End(M)\simeq\End(A)
is a skew field. The weight filtration and cofiltration on
M
, being functorial, are stable under the action of
E
. Thus, the weight filtered
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M)
inherits a weight filtration as an
E
-vector space. Let
\gamma_{\bullet}
be an
E
-basis for
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M)
and
\omega_{\bullet}
a
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
-basis for
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M)
, both respecting the weight filtrations. We define the
reduced period matrix

\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{M}
as the matrix representing the period pairing with respect to these bases:

(5.9.63.1)

\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{M}=\left(\int_{\gamma_{j}}\omega_{i}\right)_{i,j}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{L}\\
0&\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{A}&*\\
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{T}&*&*\end{pmatrix}.

(5.9.64) We now recall the key technical ingredient behind the “Dimension estimate” theorem
[
HW22
, Theorem 15.3]
, adapted to our notation.

(5.9.65)
Lemma
[
HW22
, Lemma 15.6]
.

Except for the
trivial relations
(
5.6
), there are no nonzero
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
-linear relations among the entries of the reduced period matrices
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{L}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{A}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{T}
, and the
*
blocks. Here, the trivial relations refer specifically to the relations among entries of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{T}
, a matrix with entries in
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}\langle 2\pi{\tt i}\rangle
, and among entries of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{L}
, a matrix with entries in
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
. ∎

(5.9.66) A stronger statement holds, with an analogous proof; both the statement and the method of proof are indicated in
[
HW22
, Propositions 15.9 and 15.20]
.

(5.9.67)
Lemma.

Let
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
be
1
-motives with respective abelian cores
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
. Assume that
M_{1}
is saturated and that
\mathop{Hom}\nolimits_{\mathbb{Q}}(A_{1},A_{2})=0
. Then we have

(5.9.67.1)

\im\wp_{M_{1}}\cap\im\wp_{M_{2}}\subset{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}\langle 1,2\pi{\tt i}\rangle.

Moreover, letting
L_{1}
and
T_{1}
denote the lattice and toric parts of
M_{1}
, respectively:

(1)

If
L_{1}=0
, then
\im\wp_{M_{1}}\cap{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}=0
.

(2)

If
T_{1}=0
, then
\im\wp_{M_{1}}\cap{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}\langle 2\pi{\tt i}\rangle=0
.

Sketch of proof.

By Lemmas 15.22 and 15.24 of
[
HW22
]
, we may replace
M_{2}
by a direct sum of a saturated motive and a Baker motive without changing its abelian core, and only enlarging its space of periods.
The desired statement follows by adapting the argument of Lemma 15.6 of
loc.cit.
, specifically by examining submotives of a suitably constructed auxiliary motive, as indicated in Propositions 15.9 and 15.20.
∎

(5.9.68) Suppose
M
is composite, that is, a direct sum
M=M_{B}\oplus M^{{\operatorname{sat}}}
where
M_{B}
is a Baker motive and
M^{{\operatorname{sat}}}
is a saturated motive. The direct sum decomposition induces a decomposition of Betti homology and de Rham cohomology:

(5.9.68.1)

\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M)=\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M_{B})\oplus\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M^{{\operatorname{sat}}}),\qquad\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M)=\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M_{B})\oplus\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M^{{\operatorname{sat}}}).

Thus, the space of formal periods
\mathcal{F}(M)=\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M)\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M)
decomposes naturally as the direct sum of four components:

(5.9.68.2)

\mathcal{F}(M)=\mathcal{F}(M_{B})\bigoplus\mathcal{F}(M^{{\operatorname{sat}}})\bigoplus\big(\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M_{B})\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M^{{\operatorname{sat}}})\big)\bigoplus\big(\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M^{{\operatorname{sat}}})\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M_{B})\big).

(5.9.69) The period relations
\mathcal{R}(M_{B})
and
\mathcal{R}(M^{{\operatorname{sat}}})
are embedded into
\mathcal{R}(M)
via the identifications
\mathcal{F}(M_{B})\subset\mathcal{F}(M)
and
\mathcal{F}(M^{{\operatorname{sat}}})\subset\mathcal{F}(M)
. Furthermore, the
cross-terms

\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M_{B})\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M^{{\operatorname{sat}}})
and
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M^{{\operatorname{sat}}})\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M_{B})
are contained in
\mathcal{R}(M)
: they pair a homology class from one summand with a cohomology class from the other, and thus vanish under the period pairing.

(5.9.70)
Proposition.
We have an equality of period relations:

\mathcal{R}(M)=\mathcal{R}_{\triv}(M)+\mathcal{R}(M_{B})+\mathcal{R}(M^{{\operatorname{sat}}})+\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M_{B})\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M^{{\operatorname{sat}}})+\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M^{{\operatorname{sat}}})\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M_{B}).

Proof.
The right-hand side is contained in
\mathcal{R}(M)
by construction.
The equality asserts that there are no further relations among the periods of
M_{B}
and
M^{{\operatorname{sat}}}
.
This follows from Lemma (
5.9
), since
M^{{\operatorname{sat}}}
is saturated and the abelian core of
M_{B}
is zero.
∎

5.10.
Period relations of a power of a simple motive

(5.10.71)
Proposition.
If
M
is a saturated motive with simple abelian core, then the space of period relations and the expected relations coincide, i.e.,
\mathcal{R}(M)=\mathcal{R}_{e}(M)
.

Proof.

In a suitable basis, the period map
\wp_{M}\colon\mathcal{F}(M)\to\mathbb{C}
is represented by the vector of entries of the period matrix
\mathcal{P}_{M}
. Reducing modulo the endo-relations, we can consider
\widetilde{\wp}_{M}\colon\mathcal{F}(M)/\mathcal{R}_{\End}(M)\to\mathbb{C}
which is represented by the vector of entries of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{M}
as given in (
5.9.63.1
). By Lemma (
5.9
), the only remaining relations are coming from the zero blocks and the relations between
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{T}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{L}
. These remaining relations are contained in the trivial relations.
∎

(5.10.72) Let
A=B^{n}
where
B
is a simple abelian variety and
n\geq 1
. For the skew field
E=\End(B)
, the endomorphism algebra
\End(A)
is isomorphic to the algebra
\Mat_{n}(E)
of
n\times n
matrices over
E
. Let
m_{ij}\in\End(A)
denote the
n\times n
matrix with a
1
on the
(i,j)
-th entry and zeros everywhere else. Equivalently, let
p_{i}
be the projection onto the
i
-th factor,
\iota_{i}
the identification of
B
onto the
i
-th component, so that
m_{ij}=\iota_{i}\circ p_{j}
.

(5.10.73)

If
M
is a saturated
1
-motive with abelian core
A=B^{n}
as in (
5.10
), the identification
\End(M)\simeq\End(A)
furnishes
M
with the endomorphisms algebra
\Mat_{n}(E)
. Consequently,
M
must be of the form
(M^{\prime})^{n}
where
M^{\prime}
has abelian core
B
. The operators
p_{i}
,
\iota_{i}
,
m_{ij}
lift to
M
in the natural way.

(5.10.74)
Let
M=(M^{\prime})^{n}
as in (
5.10
). Then, we can choose bases so that the period matrix of
M
is block diagonal,

(5.10.74.1)

\mathcal{P}_{M}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\mathcal{P}_{M^{\prime}}&0&\dots&0\\
0&\mathcal{P}_{M^{\prime}}&\dots&0\\
&&\ddots&\\
0&0&&\mathcal{P}_{M^{\prime}}\end{array}\right).

(5.10.75)
Proposition.
For
M
as above, the space of period relations coincides with the space of expected relations.

Proof.

We will use the elements
m_{ij}\in\End(M)\simeq\Mat_{n}(E)
from (
5.10
). Proposition (
5.10
) implies that the entries of
E\cdot m_{11}
give all the non-trivial relations amongst the entries of the first copy of
\mathcal{P}_{M^{\prime}}
in (
5.10.74.1
). There can be no other relations except those suggested by the repeated blocks. We will show that these obvious relations can all be accounted by the endo-relations.

We will first show that endomorphisms give enough relations to deduce that the
i
-th diagonal block is equal to the
1
-st diagonal block. Let
\gamma\in\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M^{\prime})
and
\omega\in\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M^{\prime})
. We will use the identities
\iota_{i}=m_{i1}\circ\iota_{1}
,
p_{i}=p_{1}\circ m_{1i}
,
m_{1i}\circ m_{i1}\circ\iota_{1}=\iota_{1}
to deduce the following identities modulo the endomorphism relations:

(5.10.75.1)

\iota_{i,*}\gamma\otimes p_{i}^{*}\omega=m_{i1,*}\iota_{1,*}\gamma\otimes m_{1i}^{*}p_{1}^{*}\omega\equiv m_{1i,*}m_{i1,*}\iota_{1,*}\gamma\otimes p_{1}^{*}\omega=\iota_{1,*}\gamma\otimes p_{1}^{*}\omega.

To see that the
(i,j)
-th block is zero for
i\neq j
, use the identities
m_{jj}\circ\iota_{i}=0
and
p_{j}\circ m_{jj}=p_{j}
. Then, modulo endomorphism relations, we have

(5.10.75.2)

\iota_{i,*}\gamma\otimes p_{j}^{*}\omega=\iota_{i,*}\gamma\otimes m_{jj}^{*}p_{j}^{*}\omega\equiv m_{jj,*}\iota_{i,*}\gamma\otimes p_{j}^{*}\omega=0.

The trivial relations account for the remaining relations between the lattice and toric periods.
∎

5.11.
Period relations for saturated motives

(5.11.76) We will decompose a saturated motive as indicated in
[
HW22
, §15.2.2]
and read off the relations in terms of endomorphisms.

(5.11.77)
Let
A
be a non-zero abelian variety. Then
A
decomposes (up to isogeny) as

(5.11.77.1)

A=B_{1}^{n_{1}}\oplus\dots\oplus B_{m}^{n_{m}}

where
B_{i}
’s are simple,
n_{i}\geq 1
, and
\mathop{Hom}\nolimits_{\mathbb{Q}}(B_{i},B_{j})=0
for
i\neq j
. Let
E_{i}=\End(B_{i})
so that

(5.11.77.2)

\End(A)\simeq\Mat_{n_{1}}(E_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus\Mat_{n_{m}}(E_{m}).

(5.11.78)
Let
M
be a non-zero saturated
1
-motive with abelian core
A
. Necessarily,
A
is non-zero and decomposes as in (
5.11
). Since
\End(M)\simeq\End(A)
,
M
has a similar structure

(5.11.78.1)

M=(M_{1}^{\prime})^{n_{1}}\oplus\dots\oplus(M_{m}^{\prime})^{n_{m}},

with the abelian core of
M_{i}^{\prime}
being simple
B_{i}
. Let us write
M_{i}=(M_{i}^{\prime})^{n_{i}}
so that
M_{i}
belongs to the case considered in §
5.10
.

(5.11.79)
Let
\jmath_{i}\colon M_{i}\hookrightarrow M
be the inclusion of the
i
-th component into
M
and
q_{i}\colon M\twoheadrightarrow M_{i}
be the projection onto the
i
-th component. For each
i,j=1,\dots,m
we define the following element of
\End(M)
:

(5.11.79.1)

\mathfrak{m}_{ij}=\begin{cases}0&i\neq j\\
\jmath_{i}\circ q_{i}\end{cases}.

We have
q_{j}\circ\mathfrak{m}_{jj}=q_{j}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{jj}\circ\jmath_{i}=0
if
i\neq j
.

(5.11.80)
Remark.

Since
\mathop{Hom}\nolimits(B_{i},B_{j})=0
for
i\neq j
the same holds for the saturated motives
M_{i}
and
M_{j}
. The maps
\mathfrak{m}_{ij}=0
for
i\neq j
correspond to projecting onto the
j
-th factor and then mapping as
0
to the
i
-th component.

(5.11.81)
The period matrix for
M
is of the form

(5.11.81.1)

\mathcal{P}_{M}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\mathcal{P}_{M_{1}}&0&\dots&0\\
0&\mathcal{P}_{M_{2}}&\dots&0\\
&&\ddots&\\
0&0&&\mathcal{P}_{M_{m}}\end{array}\right).

Proof of Theorem
5.6
.

All relations within the blocks
\mathcal{P}_{M_{i}}
are contained in the expected relations via Proposition (
5.10
).

The zero blocks in (
5.11.81.1
) can be explained by the endomorphism relations. The proof of this fact is similar to the argument at the end of Proposition (
5.10
), but this time we use the identities
q_{j}\circ\mathfrak{m}_{jj}=q_{j}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{jj}\circ\jmath_{i}=0
if
i\neq j
.

There are no further relations between the periods in distinct blocks
\mathcal{P}_{M_{i}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{M_{j}}
,
i\neq j
, except for the trivial relations, by Lemma (
5.9
).
∎

6.
Push-pull Jacobian motives

(6.0.1) In this section, we describe a mild form of pushout and pullback constructions on
1
-motives, sufficient to realize a given
1
-motive as the push-pull of a composite motive. This reduction allows us to apply Theorem (
5.6
) to the composite and compute period relations for the original motive via (
6.6
). We carry curves, motives, and their mixed Hodge structures effectively along these constructions without losing track of periods or relations.

(6.0.2) To a curve
(C\setminus{}D,E)
we associate a Jacobian motive (
6.1
), a
1
-motive with abelian core
J_{C}
, the Jacobian of
C
. Our push-pull constructions only change the lattice and the torus around this core.

(6.0.3) There are three results in this section. The first is an explicit description of the “points” of the push-pull of a Jacobian motive in terms of restricted divisor classes on a curve (
6.3
). The second is an explicit description, in terms of the MHS of a curve, of the MHS of the push-pull Jacobian motive (
6.5
). The third result states that if a motive
M_{1}
is realized as the push-pull of another motive
M_{2}
then the period relations
\mathcal{R}(M_{1})
of the former can be computed from the period relations
\mathcal{R}(M_{2})
of the latter by linear algebra on (co)homology groups (
6.6
).

6.1.
Jacobian motive of a punctured relative curve

(6.1.4) Let
C/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
be a smooth proper curve and let
D,E\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
be disjoint finite sets. The group of invertible rational functions on
C
are
\kappa(C)^{\times}
and we will write
\kappa^{\times}
for the invertible locally constant functions on
C
.

(6.1.5) We define the multiplicative subgroup

(6.1.5.1)

\kappa(C)^{\times}_{D}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{f\in\kappa(C)^{\times}:f|_{D}\in\mathbb{G}_{m}^{D}\}\subset\kappa(C)^{\times}

of rational functions without zeros or poles on
D
and the subgroup

(6.1.5.2)

\kappa(C)^{\times}_{D,1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{f\in\kappa(C)^{\times}:f|_{D}=1\}\subset\kappa(C)^{\times}_{D}

of functions constant
1
along
D
.

(6.1.6) The group of divisors (over
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
) on
C\setminus{}D
is denoted by
\Div(C\setminus{}D)
. Let
\Div^{0}(C\setminus{}D)
be the subgroup of divisors on
C\setminus{}D
of multidegree
0
on
C
, i.e., of degree
0
on each component.

(6.1.7) For a set
S\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
, denote by
L_{S}\subset\Div^{0}(C)
the lattice of multidegree
0
divisors supported on
S
.

(6.1.8) There exists
[
Ser88
]
a semi-abelian variety
J_{C}^{D}
, the
Jacobian of
(C\setminus{}D)
, defined over
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
whose
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
-points coincide with the quotient

(6.1.8.1)

\frac{\Div^{0}(C\setminus{}D)}{\{\divv(f):f\in\kappa(C)^{\times}_{D,1}\}}.

Note the restricted use of rational functions in the quotient. See (
6.3
) for the realization of
J_{C}^{D}
as a toric extension of the Jacobian of
C
.

(6.1.9) The
Abel–Jacobi map
is the quotient map

(6.1.9.1)

\AJ\colon\Div^{0}(C\setminus{}D)\to J_{C}^{D}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}):\xi\mapsto\xi\mod\kappa(C)^{\times}_{D,1}.

(6.1.10)
Definition.
The
Jacobian motive of
(C\setminus{}D,E)
is
J_{C,E}^{D}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}[L_{E}\overset{\AJ}{\to}J_{C}^{D}]
, where the the marking is given by restricting the Abel–Jacobi map
\AJ
to
L_{E}\subset\Div^{0}(C\setminus{}D)
, divisors of multidegree
0
supported on
E
(
6.1
).

(6.1.11) When either
D
or
E
is empty, we will drop it from notation. In particular,
J_{C}
is the Jacobian of the (smooth proper) curve
C
.

(6.1.12)
Remark.
We considered it would be jarring to refer to
J_{C}^{D}
and
J_{C,E}^{D}
of
J_{C}
as Albanese varieties or complexes but, technically, that is how we defined them. The usual Jacobian
J_{C}
is both the Albanese and the
\Pic^{0}
of
C
. On the other hand,
J_{C}^{D}
is the Albanese of
C\setminus{}D
but the
\Pic^{0}
of the “nodal curve”
(C,D)
, where
D
is contracted to a single node with transversal branches
[
Ser88
]
. The complex
[L_{D}\to J_{C}]
is the Albanese of the “nodal” curve
(C,D)

[
BS99
]
and
\Pic^{0}
of
C\setminus{}D
, morally standing for the quotient of
J_{C}
by
L_{D}
. More generally, the Jacobian motive
J_{C,E}^{D}
is best viewed as the Albanese of the punctured “nodal” curve
(C\setminus{}D,E)
, although it is also the
\Pic^{0}
of
(C\setminus{}E,D)
.

6.2.
Push-pull constructions

(6.2.13) Let
M=[L\to G]
be a
1
-motive with toric part
T
and abelian core
A
.

(6.2.14) Let
\psi\colon Y\to L
be a morphism of lattices. Then
\psi^{*}M\to M
, the
\psi
-pullback of
M
, is defined by the pullback diagram

(6.2.14.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\psi^{*}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

We will often write
\psi^{*}M
for the pullback, leaving the map
\psi^{*}M\to M
(which includes
Y\to L
) implicit.

(6.2.15) Let
\lambda\colon M\to L=M/G
denote the projection map. The pullback
\psi^{*}M
is canonically isomorphic to the kernel of the map
(\lambda-\psi)\colon M\oplus Y\to L
. This is a general construction valid in any abelian category.

(6.2.16) Let
\chi\colon X\to\Xi(T)
a morphism of lattices. Recall that the Cartier dual
M^{\vee}
of
M
has lattice part
\Xi(T)
. The
\chi
-pullback of
M^{\vee}
is
\chi^{*}M^{\vee}\to M^{\vee}
. Taking Cartier duals gives the
\chi
-pushout of
M
,
M\to\chi_{*}M
, where
\chi_{*}M\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\chi^{*}M^{\vee})^{\vee}
. As with pullbacks, we will often write
\chi_{*}M
for the pushout, leaving the map implicit.

(6.2.17) An alternative description of the
\chi
-pushout is as follows. The map
\chi
induces the map
\widehat{\chi}\colon T\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{X}
of tori, where
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{X}=\mathop{Hom}\nolimits(X,\mathbb{G}_{m})
is an algebraic torus with dimension equal to the rank of
X
. Then the
\chi
-pushout of
M
is the pushout of
M
via
\widehat{\chi}
,

(6.2.17.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[L\to A]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\chi_{*}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[L\to A]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\chi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

(6.2.18) Let
\tau\colon T\to M
be the inclusion map. The pushout
\chi_{*}M
is the cokernel of the map
(\widehat{\chi},-\tau)\colon T\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{X}\oplus M
. This is a general construction valid in any abelian category.

(6.2.19)
Definition.
Given morphisms of lattices
\chi\colon X\to\Xi(T)
and
\psi\colon Y\to L
then the
push-pull
of
M
with respect to
(\chi,\psi)
is
\chi_{*}\psi^{*}M
.

(6.2.20) The universal properties of the pushout and pullback give a canonical isomorphism
\chi_{*}\psi^{*}M\simeq\psi^{*}\chi_{*}M
. We will implicitly identify the two.

6.3.
Points of a push-pull Jacobian motive

(6.3.21) Consider two morphisms of lattices
\chi\colon X\to L_{D}
and
\psi\colon Y\to L_{E}
. We will describe the
push-pull Jacobian motive

J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\chi_{*}\psi^{*}J_{C,E}^{D}
using divisors on
C
, see Proposition (
6.3
). To be more precise, we will describe the points of the underlying semi-abelian variety and the marking, but this distinction will be blurred.

(6.3.22) To make the pushout clearer, we need to give an alternative construction of the Jacobian motive of
(C\setminus{}D,E)
.

(6.3.23) For any
f\in\kappa(C)^{\times}_{D}
, the restriction of
f
to
D
defines an element
f|_{D}\in\mathbb{G}_{m}^{D}=\mathop{Hom}\nolimits(D,\mathbb{G}_{m})
. For any divisor
\xi=\sum a_{i}p_{i}
supported on
D
, define
f(\xi)=\prod f(p_{i})^{a_{i}}
. In particular, this defines an element
f|_{D}\in\mathbb{G}_{m}^{L_{D}}=\mathop{Hom}\nolimits(L_{D},\mathbb{G}_{m})
. Note that the locally constant functions
\kappa^{\times}
map to
1\in\mathbb{G}_{m}^{L_{D}}
since
L_{D}
consists of multidegree
0
divisors.

(6.3.24) We have a map
\kappa(C)^{\times}_{D}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{L_{D}}\oplus\Div^{0}(C\setminus{}D):f\mapsto(f|_{D},\divv(f))
, which restricts to the map
\kappa(C)^{\times}_{D,1}\to 1\oplus\Div^{0}(C\setminus{}D)
, where
1
stands for the trivial group. In particular, by definition,

(6.3.24.1)

J_{C,E}^{D}=\frac{1\oplus\Div^{0}(C\setminus{}D)}{\kappa(C)^{\times}_{D,1}}.

(6.3.25)
Lemma.
There is a natural isomorphism

(6.3.25.1)

\frac{\Div^{0}(C\setminus{}D)}{\kappa(C)^{\times}_{D}}\overset{\sim}{\to}J_{C}

Proof.

Every divisor on
C
is linearly equivalent to a divisor on
C\setminus{}D
.
∎

(6.3.26)
Lemma.
The Jacobian of
(C\setminus{}D)
fits into an exact sequence

(6.3.26.1)

0\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{L_{D}}\to J_{C}^{D}\to J_{C}\to 0.

Proof.

Surjectivity is by Lemma
6.3
. The kernel of
J_{C}^{D}\to J_{C}
is the quotient

(6.3.26.2)

\frac{\{\divv(f):f\in\kappa(C)^{\times}_{D}\}}{\{\divv(f):f\in\kappa(C)^{\times}_{D,1}\}}

which is easily seen to be the torus
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{L_{D}}=\mathbb{G}_{m}^{D}/\kappa^{\times}
.
∎

(6.3.27)
Lemma.
The inclusion

(6.3.27.1)

1\oplus\Div^{0}(C\setminus{}D)\hookrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}^{L_{D}}\oplus\Div^{0}(C\setminus{}D)

induces an isomorphism

(6.3.27.2)

J_{C}^{D}\overset{\sim}{\to}\frac{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{L_{D}}\oplus\Div^{0}(C\setminus{}D)}{\kappa(C)^{\times}_{D}}.

Proof.

Both sides surject onto
J_{C}
with kernel
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{L_{D}}
. The proof of Lemma (
6.3
) makes it clear that the map induced from the left to the right induces the identity on
J_{C}
and
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{L_{D}}
. Now use the five lemma.
∎

(6.3.28) Lemma (
6.3
) gives an alternate construction of the Jacobian motive
J_{C,D}^{E}=[L_{E}\overset{\AJ}{\to}J_{C}]
. Using the isomorphism described in Lemma (
6.3
) we see that the marking for the alternative description of
J_{C}^{D}
is
\xi\mapsto(1,\xi)\mod\kappa(C)^{\times}_{D,1}
.

(6.3.29) The lattice morphism
\chi\colon X\to L_{D}
induces a map
\kappa(C)^{\times}_{D}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{X}:f\mapsto f|_{D}\circ\chi
. Therefore, modifying (
6.3
) we get a map

(6.3.29.1)

\kappa(C)^{\times}_{D}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{X}\oplus\Div^{0}(C\setminus{}D):f\mapsto(f|_{D}\circ\chi,-\divv(f)).

(6.3.30)
Lemma.
Let
\chi\colon X\to L_{D}
be a morphism of lattices. The pushout
\chi_{*}J_{C}^{D}
is naturally isomorphic to

(6.3.30.1)

\frac{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{X}\oplus\Div^{0}(C\setminus{}D)}{\kappa(C)^{\times}_{D}}.

Proof.

This is clear from the alternative construction of
J_{C}^{D}
in Lemma (
6.3
). To elaborate, it is convenient to view (the points of) both
J_{C}^{D}
and
\chi_{*}J_{C}^{D}
as pushouts of

(6.3.30.2)

0\to\kappa(C)^{\times}_{D}/\kappa^{\times}\to\Div(C\setminus{}D)\to J_{C}\to 0

via the restriction maps to
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{L_{D}}
and
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{X}
respectively.
∎

(6.3.31)
Proposition.
Let
\chi\colon X\to L_{D}
and
\psi\colon Y\to L_{E}
be morphisms of lattices. The push-pull Jacobian motive
J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}
is given by

(6.3.31.1)

Y\to\frac{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{X}\oplus\Div^{0}(C\setminus{}D)}{\kappa(C)^{\times}_{D}}:y\mapsto(1,\psi(y))\mod\kappa(C)^{\times}_{D}.

Proof.

This combines Lemma (
6.3
) and the observation made in (
6.3
).
∎

6.4.
A moving lemma

(6.4.32)
Definition.

For
\xi\in\Div^{0}(C\setminus{}D)
, let
[\xi]_{D}=\{\xi+\divv(f):f\in\kappa(C)_{D,1}\}
denote the
D
-linear equivalence class of
\xi
. This equivalence class represents a point of
J_{C}^{D}
. When we have a lattice morphism
\chi\colon X\to L_{D}
and
a\in\mathbb{G}_{m}^{X}
, then we let
[a,\xi]_{\chi}=\{(a-f|_{D}\circ\chi,\xi+\divv(f))\}
be the
\chi
-linear equivalance class of
(a,\xi)
, which defines a point of
J_{C}^{\chi}
. When
a=1
is the identity, we write
[\xi]_{\chi}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}[1,\xi]_{\chi}
, making the two notations compatible when
\chi=\id_{L_{D}}
.

(6.4.33) For
D\subset D^{\prime}
have a surjection
J_{C}^{D^{\prime}}\twoheadrightarrow J_{C}^{D}
. This implies that any
D
-linear equivalence class
[\xi]_{D}
can be represented by a divisor
\xi^{\prime}
supported outside of
D^{\prime}
. We need to make the determination of
\xi^{\prime}
from
\xi
effective.

(6.4.34)
Lemma.
Let
D\subset D^{\prime}\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
be finite sets. Then any divisor
\xi\in\Div(C\setminus D)
can be effectively moved away from
D^{\prime}
within its
D
-linear equivalence class.

Proof.
We may assume that
\xi
is supported only on
D^{\prime}\setminus D
, leaving the rest in place. Write
\xi=\xi_{+}-\xi_{-}
where
\xi_{+},\,\xi_{-}
are effective and have disjoint support. Let
\xi_{-}^{\epsilon}
be obtained by increasing each coefficient in
\xi_{-}
by
1
.

Let
\widetilde{D}=D^{\prime}\setminus(D\cup\supp(\xi))
. Fix a divisor
\mathfrak{p}
of positive degree on every component of
C
, and choose
n\gg 0
such that

(6.4.34.1)

h^{1}(n\mathfrak{p}-\xi_{-}^{\epsilon}-\widetilde{D}-D)=0.

Consider the exact sequence

(6.4.34.2)

0\to\mathcal{O}_{C}(n\mathfrak{p}-\xi_{-}^{\epsilon}-\widetilde{D}-D)\to\mathcal{O}_{C}(n\mathfrak{p}+\xi)\to\mathcal{O}_{\xi_{+}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\supp(\xi_{-})}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{D}\cup D}\to 0.

By our choice of
n
, the global sections of the middle term surject onto those of the right term. Hence, there exists
f\in\kappa(C)^{\times}
such that

(6.4.34.3)

\divv(f)+\xi+n\mathfrak{p}\geq 0

and

(6.4.34.4)

\divv(f)-\xi_{-}^{\epsilon}-\widetilde{D}-D+n\mathfrak{p}\not\geq 0.

In particular,
f
has just enough zeros to cancel
\xi_{-}
, but no extra zeros on
\widetilde{D}
or
D
. Surjectivity onto
\mathcal{O}_{\xi_{+}}
allows us to arrange poles cancelling
\xi_{+}
. Finally, surjectivity to
\mathcal{O}_{D}
ensures we can arrange
f|_{D}=1_{D}
.

Thus,
\xi+\divv(f)
is
D
-linearly equivalent to
\xi
and supported away from
D^{\prime}
.
∎

(6.4.35)
Remark.
This construction gives an explicit algorithm: given
\xi\in\Div(C\setminus D)
, it produces
f\in\kappa(C)_{D}^{\times}
such that
\xi^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\xi+\divv(f)\in\Div^{0}(C\setminus D^{\prime})
. It follows that, for any lattice morphism
\chi\colon X\to L_{D}
, the divisor class
[\xi]_{\chi}
can be represented by
[f|_{D}\circ\chi,\xi^{\prime}]_{\chi}
.

6.5.
Homology and cohomology of a push-pull Jacobian motive

(6.5.36) Consider two morphisms of lattices
\chi\colon X\to L_{D}
and
\psi\colon Y\to L_{E}
. We wish to describe the (co)homology of the push-pull motive
J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\chi_{*}\psi^{*}J_{C,E}^{D}
.

(6.5.37) Recall that we have a canonical isomorphism
\operatorname{H}^{1}(J_{C,E}^{D})\simeq\operatorname{H}^{1}(C\setminus{}D,E)
(
5.2.15.1
). We will combine our explicit representation of
\operatorname{H}^{1}(C\setminus{}D,E)
(
4.1
), with the push-pull constructions below to give an explicit representation of
\operatorname{H}^{1}(J_{C,\psi}^{\chi})
.

(6.5.38) The
\operatorname{H}_{1},\,\operatorname{H}^{1}
functors from
1\text{-Mot}
to
(\mathbb{Q},{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\text{-MHS}
are fully-faithful and exact. Therefore, the homology and cohomology of the push-pull motives can be described by making use of the descriptions of the pullback as a kernel (
6.2
) and the pushout as a cokernel (
6.2
).

(6.5.39) For a lattice
L
we will write
L_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}L\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
and
L_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}L\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
. Similarly, use subscripts for the
\mathbb{Q}
or
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
tensor of maps between lattices.

(6.5.40) We will need to refer to parts of the usual exact sequence involving the (co)homology of
(C\setminus{}D,E)
. The quotient map to the lattice
\lambda\colon J_{C,E}^{D}\twoheadrightarrow L_{E}
and the inclusion of the torus
\tau\colon\mathbb{G}_{m}^{L_{D}}\hookrightarrow J_{C,E}^{D}
induce the following maps

(6.5.40.1)

\displaystyle\partial\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\lambda_{\operatorname{B}}

\displaystyle\colon\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D,E)\to L_{E,\mathbb{Q}},

(6.5.40.2)

\displaystyle\Gy\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\tau_{\operatorname{B}}

\displaystyle\colon L_{D,\mathbb{Q}}^{\vee}\to\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D,E),

(6.5.40.3)

\displaystyle\delta\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\lambda^{\operatorname{AdR}}

\displaystyle\colon L_{E,{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}^{\vee}\to\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E),

(6.5.40.4)

\displaystyle\Res\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\tau^{\operatorname{AdR}}

\displaystyle\colon\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)\to L_{D,{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}.

The map
\partial\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\lambda_{\operatorname{B}}
is the usual boundary map on chains and
\Res\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\tau^{\operatorname{AdR}}
is the residue map on differential forms. The map
\Gy\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\tau_{\operatorname{B}}
is the Gysin map, putting circles around the punctures
D
of
C
. The map
\delta\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\lambda^{\operatorname{AdR}}
is the connecting morphism in cohomology, corresponding to the inclusion of constants on
E
, see (
3.4.31.1
) and (
3.5.41.1
), after the obvious identification
L_{E,{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}^{\vee}={\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}_{0}^{E}
(
3.4
).

(6.5.41)
Lemma.
For a morphism
\chi\colon X\to L_{D}
of lattices, we have

(6.5.41.1)

\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(J_{C}^{\chi})=\{(\eta,x)\in\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D)\oplus X_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}:\Res(\eta)=\chi(x)\}.

Proof.

Applying
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}
to
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{L_{D}}\hookrightarrow J_{C}^{D}\twoheadrightarrow J_{C}
and making use of the identification (
5.2.15.1
) we get

(6.5.41.2)

0\to\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C)\to\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D)\overset{\Res}{\to}L_{D}\otimes{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}\to 0.

The pullback of this exact sequence via
\chi_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
will give the cohomology of
J_{C}^{\chi}
by functoriality of
\operatorname{H}^{1}
and (
6.2
).
∎

(6.5.42) The general case can be proven in the same manner. We will simply state the result.

(6.5.43)
Proposition.
Let
\chi\colon X\to L_{D}
and
\psi\colon Y\to L_{E}
be morphisms of lattices. The mixed Hodge structre on the push-pull Jacobian motive
J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}
is given by:

(6.5.43.1)

\displaystyle\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(J_{C,\psi}^{\chi})

\displaystyle=\frac{\{(s,\eta,x)\in Y^{\vee}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}\oplus\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)\oplus X_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}:\Res(\eta)=\chi(x)\}}{\im(-\psi_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\vee},\delta,0)},

(6.5.43.2)

\displaystyle\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(J_{C,\psi}^{\chi})

\displaystyle=\frac{\{(t,\gamma,y)\in X^{\vee}_{\mathbb{Q}}\oplus\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D,E)\oplus Y_{\mathbb{Q}}:\partial\gamma=\psi(y)\}}{\im(-\chi_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\vee},\Gy,0)},

and, by denoting the class of triplets by square brackets, the period pairing

(6.5.43.3)

\wp_{J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}}([s,\eta,x]\otimes[t,\gamma,y])=y(s)+\int_{\gamma}\eta+(2\pi{\tt i})\,t(x),

where we used the integration pairing (
3.4
). The weight and Hodge filtrations are deduced immediately from those of the (co)homology of
(C\setminus{}D,E)
. ∎

6.6.
Pushing and pulling period relations

(6.6.44) We are going to show in this subsection that if
M
is the push-pull of a motive
M^{\prime}
then
\mathcal{R}(M)
can be computed from
\mathcal{R}(M^{\prime})
using just linear algebra on (co)homology spaces.

(6.6.45) Suppose
M=\psi^{*}M^{\prime}\overset{f}{\to}M^{\prime}
is a pullback by a lattice map
\psi
. Let
I^{\operatorname{B}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\im f_{*}^{\operatorname{B}}\subset\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M^{\prime})
and
I_{\operatorname{AdR}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\im f^{*}_{\operatorname{AdR}}\subset\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M)
denote the images of the induced maps on (co)homology. Choose a splitting of the surjections

(6.6.45.1)

\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M)\twoheadrightarrow I^{\operatorname{B}},\quad\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M^{\prime})\twoheadrightarrow I_{\operatorname{AdR}},

and denote the lifts of the images by
\widetilde{I}^{\operatorname{B}}\subset\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M)
and
\widetilde{I}_{\operatorname{AdR}}\subset\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M^{\prime})
. This gives an isomorphism

(6.6.45.2)

\sigma\colon I^{\operatorname{B}}\otimes\widetilde{I}_{\operatorname{AdR}}\overset{\sim}{\to}\widetilde{I}^{\operatorname{B}}\otimes I_{\operatorname{AdR}}.

(6.6.46)
Lemma.
We have
\mathcal{R}(M)=\mathcal{R}_{\triv}(M)+\ker(f_{*}^{\operatorname{B}})\otimes\im(f^{*}_{\operatorname{AdR}})+\sigma\left(\mathcal{R}(M^{\prime})\cap(I^{\operatorname{B}}\otimes\widetilde{I}_{\operatorname{AdR}})\right)
.

Proof.

Let
K=\ker(f)\subset M
be the kernel of
f
. Notice that
K
is the lattice
[\ker\psi\to 0]
and, therefore, has periods in
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
. Let
I=\im(f)\subset M^{\prime}
be the image of
f
. The lattice part of
I
is
\im(\psi)
and if we pick a splitting of the lattice of
M
as
\ker(\psi)\oplus\im(\psi)
then we can embed
I
into
M
by identifying
I
with the submotive of
M
obtained from the pullback via
\im(\psi)\hookrightarrow\ker(\psi)\oplus\im(\psi)
. Call this submotive
\overline{I}\subset M
. We now have a decomposition
M=K\oplus\overline{I}
which allows us to view
\mathcal{F}(\overline{I})
and
\mathcal{F}(K)
as subspaces of
\mathcal{F}(M)
.

The period matrix
\mathcal{P}_{M}
of
M
is of the block diagonal form

(6.6.46.1)

\mathcal{P}_{M}=\begin{array}[]{ccc}&\ker(f_{*}^{\operatorname{B}})&\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(\overline{I})\\
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(K)&\mathcal{P}_{K}&0\\
\ker(f^{*}_{\operatorname{AdR}})&0&\mathcal{P}_{I}\\
\end{array}\vskip-8.61108pt

The lower left zero block in the matrix
\mathcal{P}_{M}
corresponds to
\ker(f_{*}^{\operatorname{B}})\otimes\im(f^{*}_{\operatorname{AdR}})\subset\mathcal{R}(M)
. Let
M=[L\to G]
.

The space
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(K)\simeq\ker(f_{*}^{\operatorname{B}})\subset\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M)
is a lift of
\ker(\psi)_{\mathbb{Q}}\subset\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(L)
via the quotient
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M)\to\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(L)
. Modulo
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(G)\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(L)\subset\mathcal{R}_{\triv}(M)
, the space
\ker(f_{*}^{\operatorname{B}})\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(L)\subset\mathcal{F}(M)
is independent of the lift and the period pairing on
\ker(f_{*}^{\operatorname{B}})\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(L)
factors through
\wp_{L}
. Therefore,
(\ker(f_{*}^{\operatorname{B}})\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(L))\cap\mathcal{R}(M)
is contained in the trivial relations.

The submotive
\overline{I}\subset M
contains the underlying semi-abelian variety
G=W_{-1}M
, regardless of the splitting chosen for
\im\psi
. Therefore, the projection
M\to K
factors through
M\to L
. In particular,
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(K)\subset\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(L)\subset\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M)
, regardless of the splitting.

Moreover, modulo
\ker(f_{*}^{\operatorname{B}})\otimes\im(f^{*}_{\operatorname{AdR}})\subset\mathcal{R}(M)
, the space
\ker(f_{*}^{\operatorname{B}})\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(K)\subset\mathcal{F}(M)
is independent of our choice of splitting.

All combined, we see that the space of relations arising from the entries of
\mathcal{P}_{K}
are contained in
\mathcal{R}_{\triv}(M)+\ker(f_{*}^{\operatorname{B}})\otimes\im(f^{*}_{\operatorname{AdR}})
. This explains all relations in the first column of (
6.6.46.1
).

Every zero entry in the upper right zero block in
\mathcal{P}_{M}
is coming from trivial relations: Since,
G\subset\overline{I}
and
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(K)\subset\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(L)
the weight relation
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(G)\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(L)
explains a portion of this zero block. The splitting
L=\ker(\psi)\oplus\im(\psi)
identifies
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(K)
with
\im(\psi)^{\perp}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}\subset\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(L)=L_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
. Modulo
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(G)
,
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(\overline{I})
is identified with
\im(\psi)_{\mathbb{Q}}\subset L_{\mathbb{Q}}
. This orthogonal pairing explains the rest of the zero block.

Furthermore, since
\mathcal{P}_{K}
consists of algebraic numbers and algebraic periods of any motive (modulo relations) must come from its lattice part, we conclude that any relations between the periods of
\mathcal{P}_{\overline{I}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{K}
must lie in the space
\mathcal{R}(\overline{I})+\mathcal{R}_{\triv}(M)\subset\mathcal{R}(M)
.

So far, we proved

(6.6.46.2)

\mathcal{R}(M)=\mathcal{R}_{\triv}(M)+\ker(f_{*}^{\operatorname{B}})\otimes\im(f^{*}_{\operatorname{AdR}})+\mathcal{R}(\overline{I}).

Observe,
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(\overline{I})=\im f^{*}_{\operatorname{AdR}}=I_{\operatorname{AdR}}
is independent of the splitting. As for
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(\overline{I})
, it is a lift of
I_{\operatorname{B}}
. The other lift
\widetilde{I}_{B}
will differ by elements of
\ker(f_{*}^{\operatorname{B}})
so

(6.6.46.3)

\widetilde{I}^{\operatorname{B}}\otimes I_{\operatorname{AdR}}\equiv\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(\overline{I})\otimes I_{\operatorname{AdR}}\mod\ker(f_{*}^{\operatorname{B}})\otimes\im(f^{*}_{\operatorname{AdR}}).

Therefore, in the expression (
6.6.46.2
), we can replace
\mathcal{R}(\overline{I})
with
\mathcal{R}(M)\cap(\widetilde{I}^{\operatorname{B}}\otimes I_{\operatorname{AdR}})
, which is equal to

(6.6.46.4)

\ker(\wp_{M}|_{I^{\operatorname{B}}\otimes\widetilde{I}_{\operatorname{AdR}}}).

Via the isomorphism
\widetilde{I}^{\operatorname{B}}\overset{\sim}{\to}I^{\operatorname{B}}
we can identify the period pairing
\wp_{M}
restricted to
\widetilde{I}^{\operatorname{B}}\otimes I_{\operatorname{AdR}}
with the period pairing of
\overline{I}\simeq I
. Of course, the period pairing on
I\subset M^{\prime}
is obtained analogously by the period pairing of
M^{\prime}
restricted to
I^{\operatorname{B}}\otimes\widetilde{I}_{\operatorname{AdR}}
. The kernel
\mathcal{R}(\overline{I})
of the period pairing on
\overline{I}
can now be identified with with

(6.6.46.5)

\mathcal{R}(M^{\prime})\cap(I^{\operatorname{B}}\otimes\widetilde{I}_{\operatorname{AdR}}).

The map
\sigma
makes the identifications explicit.
∎

(6.6.47) Suppose
M^{\prime}\overset{g}{\to}\chi^{*}M^{\prime}=M
is a pushout by a map
\chi
character lattices. Let
I^{\operatorname{B}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\im f_{*}^{\operatorname{B}}\subset\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M)
and
I_{\operatorname{AdR}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\im f^{*}_{\operatorname{AdR}}\subset\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M^{\prime})
. Once again, choose lifts
\widetilde{I}^{\operatorname{B}}\subset\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M^{\prime})
and
\widetilde{I}_{\operatorname{AdR}}\subset\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M)
of
I^{\operatorname{B}}
and
I_{\operatorname{AdR}}
respectively. This gives an isomorphism

(6.6.47.1)

\sigma\colon\widetilde{I}^{\operatorname{B}}\otimes I_{\operatorname{AdR}}\overset{\sim}{\to}I^{\operatorname{B}}\otimes\widetilde{I}_{\operatorname{AdR}}.

(6.6.48)
Lemma.
We have
\mathcal{R}(M)=\mathcal{R}_{\triv}(M)+\im(f_{*}^{\operatorname{B}})\otimes\ker(f^{*}_{\operatorname{AdR}})+\sigma\left(\mathcal{R}(M^{\prime})\cap(\widetilde{I}^{\operatorname{B}}\otimes I_{\operatorname{AdR}})\right)
.

Proof.

One can replicate the proof of Lemma (
6.6
), this time making reference to the toric periods and toric relations instead. Reference to algebraic periods must be replaced by algebraic multiples of
2\pi{\tt i}
.
∎

(6.6.49)
Proposition.
If
M
is the push-pull of a motive
M^{\prime}
then explicit linear algebra on the (co)homology spaces computes
\mathcal{R}(M)
from
\mathcal{R}(M^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Let
M
be the push-pull of
M^{\prime}
via the pair of lattice morphisms
(\chi,\psi)
. Let
M^{\prime\prime}=\psi^{*}M^{\prime}\to M^{\prime}
be the
\psi
-pullback. Compute
\mathcal{R}(M^{\prime\prime})
from
\mathcal{R}(M^{\prime})
via Lemma (
6.6
). Now,
M^{\prime\prime}\to\chi_{*}M^{\prime\prime}=M
so that
\mathcal{R}(M)
can be computed from
\mathcal{R}(M^{\prime\prime})
via Lemma (
6.6
).

The statement of the referenced lemmas require only the computation of kernels and images of vector space maps as well as making arbitrary choices to lift quotients of vector spaces. Recall that the trivial relations are also computed by pure linear algebra (
5.6
).
∎

7.
Correspondences

(7.0.1) In this section, we recall the classical theory of correspondences acting on Jacobian varieties of smooth proper curves, and the representation of the endomorphism algebra
\End_{\mathbb{Z}}(J_{C})
by correspondences. We also recall that the action of a correspondence on divisors and homology can be computed effectively. We extend these constructions to Jacobian motives associated to marked and punctured curves.

(7.0.2) Correspondences will be crucial for representing the action of
\End_{\mathbb{Z}}(J_{C})
on Jacobian motives explicitly. In particular, they allow us to compute the endo-relations introduced in (
5.6
). They also form the basis for the supersaturation process in §
9
.

(7.0.3) We will also recall the practical methods recently developed to compute a representation of
\End_{\mathbb{Z}}(J_{C})

[
CMSV18
,
CLV21
]
.

7.1.
Correspondences on Jacobian varieties

(7.1.4) Let
C_{1},\,C_{2}
be smooth proper irreducible curves over
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
.

(7.1.5)
Correspondences.
An
irreducible correspondence from
C_{1}
to
C_{2}
is a triple
(C,f_{1},f_{2})
where
C
is an irreducible curve and
f_{i}\colon C\to C_{i}
,
i=1,2
, are finite morphisms. A finite formal
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combination
\mathcal{C}=\sum a_{j}(C^{(j)},f_{1}^{(j)},f_{2}^{(j)})
of irreducible correspondences is a
correspondence
. The additive group of correspondences is
\Corr(C_{1},C_{2})
.

(7.1.6)
Remark.
A correspondence from
C_{1}
to
C_{2}
defines a divisor in
C_{1}\times C_{2}
with no horizontal or vertical components. Conversely, such a divisor will yield a correspondence by taking the sum of its resolved irreducible components together with the two projection maps. These two are
functionally
equivalent, the latter being the more common way to define correspondences. We chose a definition that is more compatible with our representations of curves as function fields.

(7.1.7)
Representing a correspondence.
An irreducible correspondence is represented by the induced inclusions of function fields
\kappa(C_{i})\to\kappa(C)
for
i=1,2
. A correspondence is represented by the formal
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combination of representations of its irreducible components
(C^{(j)},f_{1}^{(j)},f_{2}^{(j)})
.

(7.1.8)
Transpose of a correspondence.
The
transpose
of an irreducible correspondence
(C,f_{1},f_{2})
is the irreducible correspondence
(C,f_{2},f_{1})
. This operation is extended linearly to correspondences
\Corr(C_{1},C_{2})\overset{\sim}{\to}\Corr(C_{2},C_{1})
and we denote the transpose of
\mathcal{C}
by
\mathcal{C}^{t}
.

(7.1.9)
Action on divisors.
An irreducible correspondence
\mathcal{C}=(C,f_{1},f_{2})
induces a morphism
\mathcal{C}_{*}\colon\Div(C_{1})\to\Div(C_{2})
defined by
\xi\mapsto f_{2,*}f_{1}^{*}\xi
, namely, by pulling back divisors via
f_{1}
and pushing forward via
f_{2}
. By taking
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combinations, any correspondence
\mathcal{C}
induces a morphism
\mathcal{C}_{*}\colon\Div(C_{1})\to\Div(C_{2})
. We will also write
\mathcal{C}^{*}
to denote the transpose
\mathcal{C}^{t}_{*}\colon\Div(C_{2})\to\Div(C_{1})
.

(7.1.10)
Linear equivalence.
For
\mathcal{C}=(C,f_{1},f_{2})
we have
\mathcal{C}_{*}\divv(g)=\divv(\Nm_{f_{2}}(f_{1}^{*}(g)))
. In particular, any correspondence
\mathcal{C}
induces an action on divisor classes which we will denote by
[\mathcal{C}_{*}]\colon J_{C_{1}}\to J_{C_{2}}
.

(7.1.11)
Action on points is effective.
Given a representation of
\mathcal{C}
and
\xi\in\Div(C_{1})
represented as a formal sum of points, the computation of
\mathcal{C}_{*}(\xi)\in\Div(C_{2})
is effective since the pullback and pushforward maps on divisors are effective.

(7.1.12) An irreducible correspondence
\mathcal{C}=(C,f_{1},f_{2})
induces a morphism of MHS
\mathcal{C}_{*}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}f_{2,*}\circ f_{1}^{!}\colon\operatorname{H}_{1}(C_{1})\to\operatorname{H}_{1}(C_{2})
. This operation extends linearly to any correspondence.

(7.1.13)
Lemma.
Given bases for the Betti and de Rham realizations of
\operatorname{H}_{1}(C_{i})
the action of any correspondence
\mathcal{C}_{*}
can be effectively computed as matrices in the given bases.

Proof.
First, compute bases for the homology and cohomology of
C
as in §
3
and §
4
. Then apply Propositions (
3.8
), (
3.8
), (
4.5
), (
4.5
) to compute the pullback, pushforward, and transfer maps.
∎

(7.1.14) The group
\mathop{Hom}\nolimits_{\mathbb{Z}}(J_{C_{1}},J_{C_{2}})\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{\rho}
is torsion-free and of finite rank. It is classical that each morphism
J_{C_{1}}\to J_{C_{2}}
arises from a correspondence, hence the natural map
\Corr(C_{1},C_{2})\to\mathop{Hom}\nolimits_{\mathbb{Z}}(J_{C_{1}},J_{C_{2}})
is surjective.

(7.1.15) A
representation
of
\mathop{Hom}\nolimits_{\mathbb{Z}}(J_{C_{1}},J_{C_{2}})
consists of correspondences
\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{\rho}\in\Corr(C_{1},C_{2})
such that the induced maps
\mu_{i}=[\mathcal{C}_{i,*}]
form a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis for
\mathop{Hom}\nolimits_{\mathbb{Z}}(J_{C_{1}},J_{C_{2}})
.

(7.1.16) If
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are reducible, with irreducible components
\{C_{1,j}\}
and
\{C_{2,j}\}
respectively, then
\Corr(C_{1},C_{2})
is defined as the direct sum of correspondences between components:
\Corr(C_{1},C_{2})=\bigoplus_{i,j}\Corr(C_{1,i},C_{2,j})
. The group
\mathop{Hom}\nolimits_{\mathbb{Z}}(J_{C_{1}},J_{C_{2}})
admits the analogous decomposition and its representation is defined as in (
7.1
).

(7.1.17) Let
C=\coprod_{i}C_{i}
be a smooth proper curve over
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
with
C_{i}
’s the irreducible components of
C
. We view
\Corr(C)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Corr(C,C)=\bigoplus\Corr(C_{i},C_{j})
as a matrix of correspondences. Similarly, the endomorphism ring decomposes as
\End_{\mathbb{Z}}(J_{C})=\bigoplus\mathop{Hom}\nolimits_{\mathbb{Z}}(J_{C_{i}},J_{C_{j}})
.

(7.1.18) A
representation
of
\End_{\mathbb{Z}}(J_{C})
consists of a basis of correspondences
\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{\rho}\in\Corr(C)
as in (
7.1
)
and
a tensor
A\in\mathbb{Z}^{\rho\times\rho\times\rho}
such that
[\mathcal{C}_{i,*}]\cdot[\mathcal{C}_{j,*}]=\sum_{k}A_{ij}^{k}[\mathcal{C}_{k,*}]\in\End_{\mathbb{Z}}(J_{C})
.

(7.1.19)
Remark.
In other words, we represent
\End_{\mathbb{Z}}(J_{C})
by an abstract
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra together with a
\mathbb{Z}
-linear map to
\Corr(C)
such that the composition with
\Corr(C)\to\End_{\mathbb{Z}}(J_{C})
yields an isomorphism of
\mathbb{Z}
-algebras.

(7.1.20)
Effectivity.
Given a representation of
\End_{\mathbb{Z}}(J_{C})
, its action on
J_{C}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
—with points represented by divisor classes—and on
\operatorname{H}_{1}(C)
can be computed effectively, using (
7.1
) and (
7.1
).

7.2.
Computing a representation of the endomorphism algebra of a Jacobian

(7.2.21) Costa, Mascot, Sijsling, and Voight
[
CMSV18
]
give an algorithm to compute a representation of
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(J_{1},J_{2})
from the equations of two irreducible curves
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
,
provided
the rank
a=\rk\Hom(J_{1},J_{2})
is known. When
a
is unknown, the method becomes an algorithm only under the Mumford–Tate conjecture
[
CLV21
]
. The procedure is implemented and performs well in practice.

(7.2.22) While the algorithm of
[
CMSV18
]
is formulated for computing
\End_{\mathbb{Z}}(J_{C})
when
C
is irreducible over
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
, it also applies to
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(J_{C_{1}},J_{C_{2}})
for irreducible curves
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
. For a possibly reducible curve
C
, use the identification in (
7.1
) to reduce to the irreducible case and compute a representation of
