\End_{\mathbb{Z}}(J_{C})
.

(7.2.23)
Theorem.
(
[
CMSV18
,
CLV21
]
) There exists a (practical, implemented) semi-algorithm to compute
\End_{\mathbb{Z}}(J_{C})
from the equations of a smooth proper curve
C/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
. This becomes an algorithm assuming the Mumford–Tate conjecture, or unconditionally if
\rk\End_{\mathbb{Z}}(J_{C})
is known. ∎

(7.2.24)
Remark.
In practice, the semi-algorithm is expected to terminate even when the rank is not known in advance. When it does, the output is provably correct. A practical upper bound for the rank, that we expect to be sharp, can be obtained from the crystalline cohomology of a prime reduction of the surface
C\times C
; see the final section of
[
CS21
]
.

(7.2.25) Although the semi-algorithm of
[
CMSV18
]
generally suffices in practice, for
theoretical decidability
one may use a complete algorithm due to Lombardo
[
Lom18
, §3]
, which computes the full endomorphism algebra of an abelian variety. While impractical, it is unconditional. For our purposes, we require only the rank and need not recover Betti or de Rham realizations. See also
[
AL24
, §7.4]
for another impractical but complete method.

(7.2.26) Lombardo’s algorithm takes as input equations for an abelian variety
A
with respect to a projective embedding, together with equations for the subvariety
\Gamma_{A}\subset A^{3}
representing the graph of the group operation
(x,y,x\cdot_{A}y^{-1})
. It outputs a description of the endomorphism algebra
\End_{\mathbb{Z}}(A)
, including its rank.

(7.2.27)
Theorem.
(
[
Lom18
,
CMSV18
,
CLV21
]
) There exists an unconditional algorithm that takes as input the equations defining a smooth proper curve
C/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
and returns a representation of
\End_{\mathbb{Z}}(J_{C})
.

Proof.

Given an irreducible curve
C/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
, we can compute equations for
J_{C}
and its group law by following the approach of
[
Cho54
]
. For irreducible curves
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
, we compute projective embeddings of the
J_{C_{i}}
and embed their product
\bigoplus J_{C_{i}}
into projective space via the Segre embedding. Given equations for a smooth proper curve
C/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
, this procedure yields equations for
J_{C}
and its group operations. We then apply Lombardo’s algorithm to compute the rank of
\End_{\mathbb{Z}}(J_{C})
, and the algorithm of
[
CMSV18
]
to compute a representation of the endomorphism ring.
∎

(7.2.28)
Remark.

The algorithm of
[
CMSV18
]
computes a representation of the integral endomorphism algebra
\mathcal{E}=\End_{\mathbb{Z}}(J_{C})
together with the decomposition of its rational span

(7.2.28.1)

\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}\cong B_{1}\times\dots\times B_{r}

as a product of simple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebras
B_{i}
, each isomorphic to a matrix algebra
\Mat_{n_{i}}(D_{i})
over a division algebra
D_{i}
with center a number field; see
[
CMSV18
, Rem. 7.2.13]
.

Given a finitely generated
\mathcal{E}
-module
X
(e.g., a lattice with a
\mathcal{E}
-action), this decomposition induces a canonical and effective splitting

(7.2.28.2)

X_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}X\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}=X_{1,\mathbb{Q}}\oplus\dots\oplus X_{r,\mathbb{Q}},

where each
X_{i,\mathbb{Q}}
is a
B_{i}
-module. Intersecting with
X
, we obtain sublattices
X_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}X\cap X_{i,\mathbb{Q}}
of finite index in
X_{i,\mathbb{Q}}
, giving a corresponding decomposition up to isogeny:

(7.2.28.3)

X\sim X_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus X_{r}.

This splitting is explicitly computable from the data returned by
[
CMSV18
]
.

7.3.
Induced morphisms between Jacobian motives

(7.3.29) Suppose
p\colon C\to C^{\prime}
is a morphism of smooth proper curves over
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
, and let
D,E\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
and
D^{\prime},E^{\prime}\subset C^{\prime}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
be finite disjoint sets. Then
p
induces a morphism
p\colon(C\setminus D,E)\to(C^{\prime}\setminus D^{\prime},E^{\prime})
if and only if
p(E)\subset E^{\prime}
and
p^{-1}(D^{\prime})\subset D
.

(7.3.30) The morphism
p
induces a pushforward
p_{*}\colon\Div^{0}(C\setminus D)\to\Div^{0}(C^{\prime}\setminus D^{\prime})
, which restricts to a morphism of lattices
L_{E}\to L_{E^{\prime}}
. For
g\in\kappa(C)_{D,1}^{\times}
, we have
\Nm_{p}(g)\in\kappa(C^{\prime})_{D^{\prime},1}^{\times}
and
p_{*}(\divv(g))=\divv(\Nm_{p}(g))
. Thus, we have a well defined map

(7.3.30.1)

p_{*}\colon\frac{\Div^{0}(C\setminus D)}{\kappa(C)_{D,1}^{\times}}\to\frac{\Div^{0}(C^{\prime}\setminus D^{\prime})}{\kappa(C^{\prime})_{D^{\prime},1}^{\times}}

which respects the markings
L_{E}\to J_{C}^{D}
and
L_{E^{\prime}}\to J_{C^{\prime}}^{D^{\prime}}
.

(7.3.31) We denote the induced map on Jacobian motives by
p_{*}\colon J_{C,E}^{D}\to J_{C^{\prime},E^{\prime}}^{D^{\prime}}
. At the level of mixed Hodge structures, this corresponds to

(7.3.31.1)

p_{*}\colon\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{C,E}^{D})=\operatorname{H}_{1}(C\setminus D,E)\to\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{C^{\prime},E^{\prime}}^{D^{\prime}})=\operatorname{H}_{1}(C^{\prime}\setminus D^{\prime},E^{\prime}).

(7.3.32) The Cartier dual of the map
p_{*}
is a morphism
p^{!}\colon J_{C^{\prime},D^{\prime}}^{E^{\prime}}\to J_{C,D}^{E}
. On points, this is induced by the pullback map on divisors

(7.3.32.1)

p^{*}\colon\Div^{0}(C^{\prime}\setminus E^{\prime})\to\Div^{0}(C\setminus E),

which restricts to a lattice morphism
L_{D^{\prime}}\to L_{D}
. Observe that, for
g\in\kappa(C^{\prime})_{E^{\prime},1}
we have
p^{*}\divv(g)=\divv(g\circ p)
and
g\circ p\in\kappa(C)_{E,1}
because
p(E)\subset E^{\prime}
. Therefore, linear equivalence is preserved.

(7.3.33)
Remark.
To prove that the Cartier dual of
p^{*}
behaves as we described on points, recall that
J_{C,D}^{E}
can be described as a Picard group on
(C\setminus E,D)
and the pullback map on line bundles is given by divisorial pullback.

(7.3.34) On homology, the corresponding map is the transfer map

(7.3.34.1)

p^{!}\colon\operatorname{H}_{1}(C^{\prime}\setminus E^{\prime},D^{\prime})\to\operatorname{H}_{1}(C\setminus E,D).

(7.3.35)
Remark.
To prove this statement, note that
p^{!}
is the pullback on cohomology of
(C^{\prime}\setminus D^{\prime},E^{\prime})
, identified with homology of
(C^{\prime}\setminus E^{\prime},D^{\prime})
via Poincaré–Lefschetz duality. Recall that Cartier duality on motives correspond to the Poincaré–Lefschetz duality on
\operatorname{H}_{1}
.

(7.3.36)
Effectivity.
Both the action on the points and the action on
\operatorname{H}_{1}
for
p^{!}
and
p_{*}
is effective. The chain of arguments essentially follows that of (
7.1
).

7.4.
Correspondences on Jacobian motives

(7.4.37) For
i=1,2
let
C_{i}
be smooth proper irreducible curves over
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
and
D_{i},E_{i}\subset C_{i}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
be finite disjoint sets.

(7.4.38)
Definition.
An
irreducible correspondence
from
(C_{1}\setminus{}D_{1},E_{1})
to
(C_{2}\setminus{}D_{2},E_{2})
is a correspondace
(C,f_{1},f_{2})
from
C_{1}
to
C_{2}
such that
f_{1}(f_{2}^{-1}D_{2})\subset D_{1}
and
f_{2}(f_{1}^{-1}E_{1})\subset E_{2}
. A correspondence is a formal
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combination of irreducible correspondences.

(7.4.39) Given an irreducible correspondence
\mathcal{C}=(C,f_{1},f_{2})
as above, let
D=f_{2}^{-1}(D_{2})
and
E=f_{1}^{-1}(E_{1})
. Then, recalling (
7.3
), we have morphisms
f_{1}\colon(C\setminus{}E,D)\to(C_{1}\setminus{}E_{1},D_{1})
and
f_{2}\colon(C\setminus{}D,E)\to(C_{2}\setminus{}D_{2},E_{2})
. Note the flip in the role of punctures and markings for
f_{1}
.

(7.4.40) Consequently, applying the constructions in §
7.3
, we get maps

(7.4.40.1)

J_{C_{1},E_{1}}^{D_{1}}\overset{f_{1}^{!}}{\to}J_{C,E}^{D}\overset{f_{2,*}}{\to}J_{C_{2},E_{2}}^{D_{2}},

whose composition we will denote by

(7.4.40.2)

[\mathcal{C}_{*}]=f_{2,*}\circ f_{1}^{!}\colon J_{C_{1},E_{1}}^{D_{1}}\to J_{C_{2},E_{2}}^{D_{2}}.

This definition extends additively to formal
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combinations of irreducible correspondences.

(7.4.41) Using §
7.3
, we see that the map on divisor classes is defined at the level of divisors by
q_{*}p^{*}\colon\Div^{0}(C_{1}\setminus{}D_{1})\to\Div^{0}(C_{2}\setminus{}D_{2})
and this restricts to
L_{E_{1}}\to L_{E_{2}}
.

(7.4.42) Using §
7.3
, we see that the induced map on mixed Hodge structures is
q_{*}p^{!}\colon\operatorname{H}_{1}(C_{1}\setminus{}D_{1})\to\operatorname{H}_{1}(C_{2}\setminus{}D_{2})
which is effective by (
7.3
).

(7.4.43) Therefore, given a correspondence
\mathcal{C}
from
(C_{1}\setminus{}D_{1},E_{1})
to
(C_{2}\setminus{}D_{2},E_{2})
we can effectively compute the action on divisors representing the map
[\mathcal{C}_{*}]\colon J_{C_{1},E_{1}}^{D_{1}}\to J_{C_{2},E_{2}}^{D_{2}}
and the action on
\operatorname{H}_{1}
.

(7.4.44)
Lemma.
Let
\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{k}
be a tuple of correspondences on
C
and
D\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
a finite subset. Then we can effectively compute a finite set
D^{\prime}\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
containing
D
such that, for all
i=1,\dots,\rho
,
\mathcal{C}_{i}
induces a correspondence from
(C,D)
to
(C,D^{\prime})
, in particular
\mathcal{C}_{i,*}\colon L_{D}\to L_{D^{\prime}}
. Dually, the transpose correspondences
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{t}
induce correspondences from
C\setminus{}D^{\prime}
to
C\setminus{}D
, in particular,
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{*}\colon\Div^{0}(C\setminus{}D^{\prime})\to\Div^{0}(C\setminus{}D)
.

Proof.

Write
\mathcal{C}_{i}=\sum_{j}a_{ij}(C_{ij},p_{ij},q_{ij})
as a sum of irreducible correspondences. Let
D_{ij}\subset C_{ij}
be the preimage
p_{ij}^{-1}(D)
. Let
D^{\prime}
be the union
D\bigcup_{ij}q_{ij}(D_{ij})
. Then, for each
i,j
,
(C_{ij},p_{ij},q_{ij})
is a correspondence from
(C,D)
to
(C,D^{\prime})
. Therefore,
\mathcal{C}_{i}
is a correspondence from
(C,D)
to
(C,D^{\prime})
. The dual statement is clear.
∎

(7.4.45)
Remark.
It is tempting to choose a basis
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{d}
for
L_{D}
and define
D^{\prime}
to be the union of the supports of
\mathcal{C}_{i,*}\xi_{j}
. Although this ensures
\mathcal{C}_{i,*}\colon L_{D}\to L_{D^{\prime}}
, each irreducible component of
\mathcal{C}_{i,*}
may not induce such a map and then the construction of the action on
\operatorname{H}_{1}
requires an extra step.

8.
Explicit morphisms and decompositions of push-pull Jacobian motives

(8.0.1) In this section, we describe the operations on push-pull Jacobian motives that can be carried out explicitly, both at the level of points and mixed Hodge structures.

(8.0.2) We begin by introducing the notions of point-explicit and MHS-explicit morphisms (
8.1
). In this context, explicit means that the action on divisor classes and on
\operatorname{H}_{1}
can be computed effectively by linear algebra and divisor arithmetic on curves, without invoking higher-dimensional techniques. We also develop techniques to determine more suitable representatives for a given push-pull Jacobian motive in its isogeny class.

(8.0.3) We explain how to compute the kernel of the Abel–Jacobi map from a lattice (
8.3
), and build on this to explicitly split off Baker components from push-pull Jacobian motives (
8.4
), (
8.5
).

(8.0.4) We also explain how to compute the period relations of a Baker motive (
8.8
).

8.1.
Explicit representations of morphisms

(8.1.5)
Representing motives.
In this work, we represent a subclass of motives by push-pull Jacobian motives. Not all motives admit such a description; in fact, not even all abelian varieties are Jacobians. Nevertheless, the subclass we consider is evidently rich enough to represent any
1
-period and, as we will show, is enough to capture all relations.

(8.1.6)
Representing a push-pull.
To represent a motive
M=[Y\to[X\to A]^{\vee}]
as a push-pull of another motive
M^{\prime}=[Y^{\prime}\to[X^{\prime}\to A]^{\vee}]
is to represent
M^{\prime}
and lattice morphisms
\chi\colon X\to X^{\prime}
and
\psi\colon Y\to Y^{\prime}
such that
M=\psi^{*}\chi_{*}M^{\prime}
. If
M
already has another representation, then it is implied that the isomorphism
M\simeq\psi^{*}\chi_{*}M^{\prime}
(or its inverse) is explicit, at least at the level of
\operatorname{H}_{1}
.

(8.1.7)
Different representations, same motive.
Let
D\subset D^{\prime}\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
be finite sets. The motive
J_{C}^{D}
is represented as a Jacobian motive, while it is also isomorphic to the pushout
\iota_{*}J_{C}^{D^{\prime}}=J_{C}^{\iota}
via the inclusion of lattices
\iota\colon L_{D}\to L_{D^{\prime}}
. Although isomorphic, these motives have different representations, affecting how points and Betti or de Rham realizations are described. For example, points of
J_{C}^{D}
are represented by divisors on
C\setminus D
, whereas points of
\iota_{*}J_{C}^{D^{\prime}}
by divisors on
C\setminus D^{\prime}
. Such distinctions matter when applying morphisms or moving divisors away from problematic loci.

(8.1.8)
Representing morphisms.
We will need to single out representations of certain morphisms between push-pull Jacobian motives. We aim for an effective—even, efficient—computation of the action of the map on “points” and on
\operatorname{H}_{1}
. Prime examples are those induced from lattice pushout and pullback maps as well as correspondences. The map induced by a correspondence in the sense of (
7.4
) is represented by the correspondence itself as in (
7.1
).

(8.1.9)
Representing pullback and pushout maps.
Consider the push-pull Jacobian motive
M=J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}
where
\chi\colon X\to L_{D}
and
\psi\colon Y\to L_{E}
. A morphism of lattices
\mu\colon X^{\prime}\to X

represents
the pushout map
J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}\to\mu_{*}J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}=J_{C,\psi}^{\chi\circ\mu}
. Similarly, a lattice morphism
\nu\colon Y^{\prime}\to Y

represents
the pullback map
\nu^{*}J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}=J_{C,\psi\circ\nu}^{\chi}\to J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}
.

(8.1.10)
Points of a motive.
Let
M=[L\to G]
be a
1
-motive. By the
points of
M
, we mean the complex
L\to G({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
, or, up to isogeny, the complex
L\otimes\mathbb{Q}\to G({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\otimes\mathbb{Q}
. For push-pull Jacobian motives, both the group
G({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
and the map
L\to G({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
can be described explicitly in terms of divisor classes (
6.3
).

(8.1.11)
Point-explicit morphisms.
A morphism
M_{1}\to M_{2}
, or rather its representation, between two push-pull Jacobian motives is said to be
point-explicit
if the induced map
M_{1}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\to M_{2}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
is effective. In particular, given a divisor representing a divisor class on
M_{1}
, one can compute a divisor representing its image in
M_{2}
. We further require that the operations involved reduce to simple linear algebra or to divisor arithmetic on curves and do not rely on higher dimensional algebraic geometry. For instance, correspondences induce point-explicit morphisms (
7.3
).

(8.1.12)
Pushout and pullback are point-explicit.
Recall that we represent points of a push-pull Jacobian motive by the class of a tuple
(a,\xi)
, where
a\in\mathbb{G}_{m}^{X}
and
\xi\in\Div^{0}(C\setminus D)
for some data
X,C,D
. A pushout morphism induced by a lattice map
\chi\colon X^{\prime}\to X
acts on
a
via the dual map
\widehat{\chi}\colon\mathbb{G}_{m}^{X}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{X^{\prime}}
, while leaving
\xi
unchanged. That is, the point
[a,\xi]
is sent to
[\widehat{\chi}(a),\xi]
. The marking is adjusted accordingly, so pushout maps are point-explicit. Pullback maps induced by lattice morphisms are evidently point-explicit, as they only act non-trivially on the lattice part of the points.

(8.1.13)
MHS of a motive.
For a motive
M
, the associated mixed Hodge structure is given by
\operatorname{H}_{1}(M)=(\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M),\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M)^{\vee},\mathfrak{c}_{{\operatorname{B}},{\operatorname{AdR}}})
. When
M
is a push-pull Jacobian motive, we have explicit representatives for bases of both underlying vector spaces (
6.5
).

(8.1.14)
MHS-explicit morphisms.
A morphism
M_{1}\to M_{2}
between two push-pull Jacobian motives is
MHS-explicit
if the induced map between their mixed Hodge structures
\operatorname{H}_{1}(M_{1})\to\operatorname{H}_{1}(M_{2})
is effective. Once again, we require that the operations involved reduce to simple linear algebra or to divisor arithmetic on curves and do not rely on higher dimensional algebraic geometry. For instance, correspondences are MHS-explicit (
7.4
).

(8.1.15) Note that, given basis elements for the Betti and de Rham realizations of
M_{1}
, one can compute under an MHS-explicit map their images in the corresponding realizations of
M_{2}
. This means we have explicit linear maps: matrices in chosen bases that describe the induced morphisms on each realization.

(8.1.16)
Pullbacks and pushouts are MHS-explicit.
This is clear from the representation of the MHS given in (
6.5
). Pullback and pushout maps will act as the identity on the central component
\operatorname{H}_{1}(C\setminus{}D,E)
and act naturally via the lattice maps on the outer components.

(8.1.17)
Explicit map.
A morphism between two push-pull Jacobian motives is said to be
explicit
if it is both point-explicit and MHS-explicit. That is, we can compute its action on divisor classes representing points, and describe its induced morphism on mixed Hodge structures via explicit matrices. We established above that pullback and pushout morphisms as well as correspondences are explicit.

(8.1.18)
Period effective maps.
An MHS-explicit map
M_{1}\to M_{2}
will be called
period effective
if
\mathcal{R}(M_{1})
can be determined from
\mathcal{R}(M_{2})
by applying prescribed linear algebra operations. Trivially, MHS-explicit isomorphisms are period effective. We also proved that pushouts and pullbacks are period effective (
6.6
).

8.2.
Isogenies, direct sum decompositions, and making lattice maps injective

(8.2.19) Our primary concern is with period relations and, therefore, with the MHS
\operatorname{H}_{1}
. However, we cannot compute effectively with the comparison isomorphism and we resort to numerical approximations. The motives are used to remedy this defect: their points allow us to recover the exactness lost in working with the approximate comparison isomorphism. In this sense, motives serve as an effective model for the underlying
\operatorname{H}_{1}
. Naturally, we wish to allow ourselves to work up to isogeny, as this leaves
\operatorname{H}_{1}
unchanged.

(8.2.20)
Effectively interchanging isogenous motives.
Suppose
M_{1}\to M_{2}
is an MHS-explicit isogeny, so that the induced map
\operatorname{H}_{1}(M_{1})\to\operatorname{H}_{1}(M_{2})
is an effective isomorphism. Then we obtain an identification of the space of period relations:
\mathcal{R}(M_{1})\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{R}(M_{2})
. Thus, for our purposes, we may pass between
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
interchangeably.

(8.2.21)
Direct sum decompositions.
An
explicit direct sum decomposition
of a motive
M_{3}
consists of explicit morphisms
M_{1}\to M_{3}
and
M_{2}\to M_{3}
(or
M_{3}\to M_{1}
and
M_{3}\to M_{2}
) such that the induced map
M_{1}\oplus M_{2}\to M_{3}
(or
M_{3}\to M_{1}\oplus M_{2}
) is an isomorphism. In any case, we obtain an explicit identification
\operatorname{H}_{1}(M_{3})=\operatorname{H}_{1}(M_{1})\oplus\operatorname{H}_{1}(M_{2})
. Furthermore, we get a pointwise isomorphisms
M_{1}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\oplus M_{2}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\overset{\sim}{\to}M_{3}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
, or in the other direction. We call this giving an explicit direct sum decomposition
M_{3}\simeq M_{1}\oplus M_{2}
.

(8.2.22)
Isogeny direct sum decomposition.
An
explicit isogeny direct sum decomposition
of a motive
M_{3}
into
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
consists of explicit isogenies
M_{1}\sim M_{1}^{\prime}
,
M_{2}\sim M_{2}^{\prime}
, and explicit morphisms
M_{3}\to M_{1}^{\prime}
and
M_{3}\to M_{2}^{\prime}
such that
M_{3}\to M_{1}^{\prime}\oplus M_{2}^{\prime}
is an isogeny. This gives an explicit identification
\operatorname{H}_{1}(M_{3})=\operatorname{H}_{1}(M_{1})\oplus\operatorname{H}_{1}(M_{2})
and an explicit, pointwise, map
M_{3}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\otimes\mathbb{Q}\overset{\sim}{\to}(M_{1}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\oplus M_{2}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}))\otimes\mathbb{Q}
. As in (
8.2
), we also allow for maps in the other direction. We will refer to this as an explicit isogeny direct sum decomposition and denote it by
M_{3}\sim M_{1}\oplus M_{2}
.

(8.2.23)
Disjoint union decomposition.
Suppose
(C\setminus D,E)
is the disjoint union of
(C_{i}\setminus D_{i},E_{i})
’s. Then the associated Jacobian motive
J_{C,E}^{D}
naturally admits an explicit direct sum decomposition into the
J_{C_{i},E_{i}}^{D_{i}}
’s. This decomposition is induced by functoriality and is explicit on both points and mixed Hodge structures.

(8.2.24)
Making lattice maps injective.

Let
J_{C,\chi}
be a pullback Jacobian motive with
\chi\colon X\to L_{D}
. Then we may effectively construct a disjoint union
C^{\prime}
of
C
with finitely many copies of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, a finite set
D^{\prime}\subset C^{\prime}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
, and an
injective
lattice map
\chi^{\prime}\colon X\to L_{D^{\prime}}
, such that there exists an explicit isomorphism
J_{C,\chi}\cong J_{C^{\prime},\chi^{\prime}}
. This gives a model in which the lattice map is injective—a seemingly minor step that yields significant expository simplification.

Proof.

Let
\chi\colon X\to L_{D}
and let
K=\ker\chi
. Choose a basis
e_{1},\dots,e_{k}
for
K
, and a complement
X^{\prime}\subset X
such that
X=K\oplus X^{\prime}
. Let
C^{\prime}
be the disjoint union of
C
and
k
copies of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and let
D^{\prime}
be the union of
D
with
\{0,\infty\}
on each added copy.

Define
\chi^{\prime}\colon X\to L_{D^{\prime}}
by sending
e_{i}\mapsto\infty-0
on the
i
-th
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and restricting to
\chi
on
X^{\prime}
. Then
\chi^{\prime}
is injective by construction.

Observe that
J_{C,\chi}\cong[K\to 0]\oplus[X^{\prime}\to J_{C}]
is an explicit decomposition because the kernel of the lattice map splits from the representation of points and of
\operatorname{H}_{1}
by the pullback construction. On the other hand,
J_{C^{\prime},\chi^{\prime}}\cong[\mathbb{Z}\to 0]^{\oplus k}\oplus[X^{\prime}\to J_{C}]
by the disjoint union decomposition of
(C^{\prime},D^{\prime})
. The two motives are identified via identification of summands.
∎

(8.2.25)
Remark.
The construction in (
8.2
) is effective and leaves
J_{C}
unchanged. We will invoke this operation by saying that we may assume
\chi
is injective, without renaming
C
,
D
, or
\chi
.

8.3.
Computing the kernel of an Abel–Jacobi map

(8.3.26) Consider
J_{C,D}=[L_{D}\overset{\AJ}{\to}J_{C}]
. Let
K_{D}\subset L_{D}
be the kernel of the Abel–Jacobi map
\AJ\colon L_{D}\to J({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
, these are the divisors supported on
D
which are linearly equivalent to
0
. We will give an algorithm here to compute
K_{D}
. Let
T_{D}\subset L_{D}
be the kernel of
L_{D}\to J({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
, these are the divisors supported on
D
whose linear equivalence classes are torsion.

(8.3.27)
Saturating a lattice.
Once we know
K_{D}\subset L_{D}
, we can saturate it in
L_{D}
to compute
T_{D}
. If we choose bases and represent the inclusion
K_{D}\hookrightarrow L_{D}
by an integer matrix, the saturation of the image can be computed from the Smith normal form of the matrix.

(8.3.28)
Preparation.
Without loss of generality, we may assume
C
is irreducible. Fix a basis
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}
for
L_{D}
, these are divisors of degree
0
on
C
supported on
D
. Let
{\sf k}\subset{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
be a number field over which
C
and each point of
D
can be defined. In particular, the divisors
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}
are defined over
{\sf k}
.

(8.3.29)
The Néron–Tate height pairing.
The algorithm of
[
vBHM19
]
allows us to compute the Néron–Tate height pairing
\langle[\xi_{i}],[\xi_{j}]\rangle
of the divisor classes to arbitrary precision. By performing the relevant period computations with certified error bounds, we may compute complex balls containing each Néron–Tate height with desired precision. The Néron–Tate height pairing is positive definite on
J_{C}({\sf k})\otimes\mathbb{R}
. The matrix
\left(\langle[\xi_{i}],[\xi_{j}]\rangle\right)_{i,j=1}^{n}
gives the pullback of the height pairing to
L_{D}\otimes\mathbb{R}
. The rank of this pullback matrix is the rank of
L_{D}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\to J_{C}(\mathbb{Q})
despite the real coefficients, since the map is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
.

(8.3.30)
Testing membership in the kernel.

Given an element
\xi\in L_{D}
,
\AJ(\xi)=0
in
J_{C}
if and only if
\xi
is linearly equivalent to zero. This can be tested effectively by computing the Riemann–Roch space of
\xi
. In particular, given a sublattice
K^{\prime}\subset L_{D}
, performing this test on a basis for
K^{\prime}
we can decide whether
K^{\prime}\subset\ker\AJ
.

(8.3.31)
An efficient guess for the kernel.

We can use lattice reduction
[
LLL82
]
to efficiently guess a sublattice
K^{\prime}\subset L_{D}
likely to lie in
K_{D}
. Using the filtrations and the comparison isomorphism on
\operatorname{H}_{1}(C,D)
we have an approximation of the map

(8.3.31.1)

\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C,D;\mathbb{Z})\to(F^{1}\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C,D))^{\vee}=(F^{1}\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C))^{\vee}

Use LLL to guess the integral kernel of the map. Project the putative kernel to
L_{D}=\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C,D)/\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C)
to formulate the guess
K^{\prime}
. This guess
K^{\prime}
can be refined by increasing the working precision on the period matrix.

(8.3.32)
Computing the kernel.

Let
K=0
. We start by formulating a guess (
8.3
) of a kernel
K^{\prime}\subset L_{D}
and check (
8.3
) if a basis for
K^{\prime}
consists of divisors linearly equivalent to
0
. Update
K
by adding the basis elements of
K^{\prime}
that were linearly equivalent to
0
. Increase precision of the period matrix used for the guess and repeat. This gives an non-decreasing sequence of lattices
K
in
K_{D}
.

In parallel, we will compute a non-decreasing sequence of lower bounds on the rank of
\AJ\otimes\mathbb{R}
on
L_{D}\otimes\mathbb{R}
. To find the lower bounds, we simply check which minors of the height pairing (
8.3
) on
L\otimes\mathbb{R}
do not contain zero using higher and higher precision.

Whenever our lower bound matches the corank of
K
, we compute the saturation
T
of
K
, find finitely many elements in
T
dominating
T/K
and check which of these finitely elements are linearly equivalent to
0
. Then
K_{D}
is the span of
K
with these new elements.

The algorithm terminates because LLL will eventually find a lattice of full rank in
K_{D}
and our approximation of the minor of the correct corank will eventually stop containing
0
.

8.4.
Splitting Baker components, part I

(8.4.33) We will now explicitly split the kernel
K_{D}
of the Abel–Jacobi map from the Jacobian motive
J_{C,D}=[L_{D}\to J_{C}]
. This means giving a pullback Jacobian
J_{C,\aleph_{D}}
,
\aleph_{D}\colon N_{D}\to L_{D}
, and an explicit isogeny direct sum decomposition
J_{C,D}\sim[K_{D}\to 0]\oplus J_{C,\aleph_{D}}
, recall (
8.2
).

(8.4.34) We first need an explicit map from
[K_{D}\to 0]
into
J_{C,D}
, meaning it must be both point-explicit and MHS-explicit (
8.1
).

(8.4.35) At the level of points, the only non-trivial part of the inclusion of the motive
[K_{D}\to 0]
into
J_{C,D}
is the inclusion of lattices
K_{D}\subset L_{D}
. Having computed
K_{D}
explicitly in
L_{D}
in (
8.3
), there is nothing to prove here.

(8.4.36)
Lemma.
The injection
[K_{D}\to 0]\to J_{C,D}=[L_{D}\to J_{C}]
is MHS-explicit.

Proof.

Let
e_{1},\dots,e_{k}
be a basis for the kernel
K_{D}\subset L_{D}
. For each
e_{i}\in K_{D}
, compute a rational function
f_{i}\in\kappa(C)^{\times}
such that
e_{i}=\divv(f_{i})
.

Recall that the Jacobian motive of
(\mathbb{P}^{1},\{0,\infty\})
satisfies
J_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\{0,\infty\}}\cong[\mathbb{Z}\to 0]
. Let
\Gamma_{f_{i}}
denote the graph of the rational function
f_{i}\colon C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Consider the transpose correspondence
\Gamma_{f_{i}}^{t}
from
(\mathbb{P}^{1},\{0,\infty\})
to
(C,D)
. It acts (
7.3
) by pulling back the unique divisor class
[\infty-0]
to
\divv(f_{i})
, thus mapping the generator
1
from
J_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\{0,\infty\}}
to
e_{i}
in
J_{C,D}
.

Identify the motive
[K_{D}\to 0]
with the Jacobian motive of a disjoint union of
k
copies of
(\mathbb{P}^{1},\{0,\infty\})
. Define a correspondence
\Gamma\subset\left(\coprod_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{P}^{1}\right)\times C
as the disjoint union of graphs
\Gamma_{f_{i}}\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}\times C
. The transpose correspondence
\Gamma^{t}
defines a morphism from the Jacobian motive
J_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\{0,\infty\}}^{\oplus k}
to the Jacobian motive
J_{C,D}
, representing the injection
[K_{D}\to 0]\hookrightarrow J_{C,D}
.

This map is MHS-explicit since morphisms represented by correspondances have this property (
7.4
).
∎

(8.4.37)
Remark (Explicit interpretation of the vanishing condition (14.3)).
In the monograph
[
HW22
, p.137]
, a certain vanishing condition “(14.3)” is introduced for rational differentials of the second type. Explicitly, given a rational differential
\omega
on
C
of the second type, and a submotive
\iota\colon[\mathbb{Z}\to 0]\hookrightarrow J_{C,D},\quad 1\mapsto\divv(f),\quad f\in\kappa(C)^{\times}
, condition (14.3) asks for the vanishing of
\iota^{*}([\omega])
, where
[\omega]\in\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C,D)
is the cohomology class of
\omega
. The statement of Lemma (
8.4
) implies that this condition can be explicitly checked by arithmetic on curves. We will spell this out here, as it may be of independent interest.

Following the proof of the lemma above, we need the Cartier dual (
7.3
) of the pushforward map given by
f\colon C\setminus{}D\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus{}\{0,\infty\}
. On mixed Hodge structures the induced map is
f^{!}\colon\operatorname{H}_{1}(\mathbb{P}^{1},\{0,\infty\})\to\operatorname{H}_{1}(C,D)
, see (
7.3
).

On algebraic de Rham cohomology, this corresponds to the transfer map,

(8.4.37.1)

f_{!}\colon\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C,D)\to\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(\mathbb{P}^{1},\{0,\infty\}),

which evaluates to the
f
-trace on differentials (
3.8
) mapping
[\omega]
to
[\tr_{f}\omega]
. Note that the trace of
\omega
has no residual poles and must be exact on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

We have an identification
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(\mathbb{P}^{1},\{0,\infty\})\simeq{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{\{0,\infty\}}/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}\simeq{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
, which sends
\mathrm{d}h\mapsto h(0)-h(\infty)
, see (
3.4
). Thus, the condition
\iota^{*}([\omega])=0
is explicitly checked by computing the rational function
h
such that
\tr_{f}\omega=\mathrm{d}h
and evaluating the difference
h(0)-h(\infty)
.

(8.4.38)
Representing the quotient.
The quotient
J_{C,D}/[K_{D}\to 0]
admits an explicit representation as a pullback motive, which we describe here. This representation is not unique, but any two representations differ only by an isogeny. Let
K_{D}\subset T_{D}
be as computed previously, and define
n\in\mathbb{N}
to be the least common multiple of the orders of elements in the quotient
T_{D}/K_{D}
. Define
L_{nD}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}n\cdot L_{D}\subset L_{D}
and let
J_{C,nD}
denote the pullback associated to
L_{nD}\hookrightarrow L_{D}
. By construction,
L_{nD}\cap T_{D}=L_{nD}\cap K_{D}=K_{nD}
, the kernel of the Abel–Jacobi map on
L_{nD}
. Define
N_{D}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}L_{nD}/K_{nD}
, which naturally maps to
J_{C}
. The resulting motive
M\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}[N_{D}\to J_{C}]=J_{C,nD}/[K_{nD}\to 0]
is isogenous to the original quotient
J_{C,D}/[K_{D}\to 0]
. First note that for any splitting
\aleph_{D}\colon N_{D}\to L_{nD}\subset L_{D}
, the map
N_{D}\to J_{C}
will factor through
\aleph_{D}
, hence
M\simeq J_{C,\aleph_{D}}
. Moreover, the natural inclusion
J_{C,\aleph_{D}}\hookrightarrow J_{C,nD}
followed by the quotient map induces an explicit isomorphism

(8.4.38.1)

\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{C,\aleph_{D}})\overset{\sim}{\to}\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{C,nD})/\operatorname{H}_{1}([K_{nD}\to 0])=\operatorname{H}_{1}(M),

as well as the identity map on the points
J_{C,\aleph_{D}}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\overset{\sim}{\to}M({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
. To comply with our choice of representation, we will write
J_{C,\aleph_{D}}
for
M
, however, we could have slightly extended our representation to allow for quotients of lattices and cover
M
since the choice of
\aleph_{D}
plays no role here.

(8.4.39)
Lemma.
We have an explicit isogeny direct sum decomposition
J_{C,D}\sim[K_{D}\to 0]\oplus J_{C,\aleph_{D}}
. In fact, we can describe the decomposition maps in either direction

Proof.

The explicit maps
J_{C,\aleph_{D}}\to J_{C,D}
and
[K_{D}\to 0]\to J_{C,D}
give an explicit isogeny direct sum decomposition of
J_{C,D}
.

We can also realize the isogeny in the other direction. First, replace
J_{C,D}
with the explicitly isogenous representative
J_{C,nD}
. The lattice
L_{nD}
splits via
\aleph_{D}
into
K_{nD}\oplus N_{D}
. Projecting onto
[K_{nD}\to 0]
and onto the quotient
J_{C,nD}/[K_{nD}\to 0]=[N_{D}\to J_{C}]\simeq J_{C,\aleph_{D}}
recovers the decomposition in the other direction.
∎

8.5.
Splitting Baker components, part II

(8.5.40) We continue with the notation of the previous part and consider
J_{C}^{D}
. This time we will give an explicit isogeny direct sum decomposition
J_{C}^{D}\sim\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K_{D}}\oplus J_{C,\aleph_{D}}
.

(8.5.41)
Lemma.

The canonical surjection from
J_{C}^{D}
to
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K_{D}}
is explicit.

Proof.

The proof is dual to that of (
8.4
), using
\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,\infty\}
in place of
(\mathbb{P}^{1},\{0,\infty\})
. We repeat a part of the argument here to clarify the action on points.

Let
K_{D}=\langle\divv(f_{1}),\dots,\divv(f_{k})\rangle
for some
f_{i}\in\kappa(C)^{\times}
, and let
\Gamma_{f_{i}}
denote the graph of
f_{i}\colon C\setminus D\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,\infty\}
. Since
J_{\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,\infty\}}=\mathbb{G}_{m}
, we identify
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K_{D}}\simeq(J_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\{0,\infty\}})^{\oplus k}
as the Jacobian of
k
disjoint copies of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,\infty\}
.

Let
\Gamma=\coprod\Gamma_{f_{i}}
be the disjoint union of these graphs. Then
\Gamma
defines a correspondence from
C\setminus D
to
\coprod(\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,\infty\})
inducing the desired map
J_{C}^{D}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K_{D}}
. The induced map is explicit by virtue of being induced by a correspondance.
∎

(8.5.42)
Action on points made explicit.

The Abel–Jacobi map
\AJ\colon\Div^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,\infty\})\to J_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\{0,\infty\}}\simeq\mathbb{G}_{m}
is the one mapping
[1:a]-[1:b]
to
a/b
. Equivalently, any divisor
\divv h
is mapped to
h(\infty)/h(0)\in\mathbb{G}_{m}
. Given a divisor
\xi
representing a point of
J_{C}^{D}
the point
\AJ(f_{i,*}(\xi))\in\mathbb{G}_{m}
is the
i
-th coordinate of the map
J_{C}^{D}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K_{D}}
for
K_{D}=\langle\divv(f_{1}),\dots,\divv(f_{k})\rangle
.

(8.5.43)
Lemma.
We have an explicit isogeny direct sum decomposition
J_{C}^{D}\sim\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K_{D}}\oplus J_{C}^{\aleph_{D}}
. We can describe the decomposition maps in either direction.

Proof.

The map
J_{C}^{D}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K_{D}}
is made explicit in (
8.5
). The map
J_{C}^{D}\twoheadrightarrow J_{C}^{\aleph_{D}}
is explicit by pushout construction. This gives the explicit isogeny direct sum decomposition in one direction. To get the isogeny in the other direction repeat the argument in (
8.4
) by taking duals.
∎

(8.5.44) We explicate the inclusion
J_{C}^{\aleph_{D}}\hookrightarrow J_{C}^{D}
for a representative
[\xi]_{\aleph_{D}}
, where
\xi\in\Div^{0}(C\setminus{}D)
. Use the explicit map
\pi\colon J_{C}^{D}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K_{D}}
described in (
8.5
) to compute
\pi([\xi]_{D})\in\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K_{D}}
. Then, use the splitting
\iota\colon\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K_{D}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}^{L_{D}}\hookrightarrow J_{C}^{D}
induced from
L_{D}\sim K_{D}\oplus N_{D}
and compute
[\xi^{\prime}]_{D}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}[\xi]_{D}-\iota(\pi([\xi]_{D}))
which necessarily lies in
\ker\pi\sim J_{C}^{\aleph_{D}}
.

8.6.
Splitting Baker components, part III

(8.6.45) We continue with the notation of the previous parts. We will now split the Baker components from
J_{C,\chi}
and
J_{C}^{\chi}
for a lattice map
\chi\colon X\to L_{D}
.

(8.6.46) Let
K_{\chi}\subset X
be the pullback of
K_{D}\subset L_{D}
, i.e., the kernel of
X\to J_{C}
. Since
K_{D}\subset L_{D}
was computed,
K_{\chi}
is computable.

(8.6.47) Scaling
\chi
by an integer corresponds to passing to an isogenous representative of
J_{C,\chi}
. Then, taking
n>0
as in (
8.4
), work with
n\chi\colon X\to L_{nD}
so the pullback of torsion divisors classes and
0
divisor classes agree and give
K_{\chi}
. Thus
N_{\chi}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}X/K_{\chi}\to J_{C}
is well defined. Choose a lift
{\aleph_{\chi}}\colon N_{\chi}\to L_{nD}\subset L_{D}
to represent
[N_{\chi}\to J_{C}]
as the pullback
J_{C,{\aleph_{\chi}}}
. By construction
N_{\chi}\to J_{C}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\otimes\mathbb{Q}
is injective, thus the motives
J_{C,\aleph_{\chi}}
and
J_{C}^{\aleph_{\chi}}
are reduced (
5.5
).

(8.6.48) Recall (
8.2
) that by a small modification to
C,D,\chi
we may assume that
\chi\colon X\to L_{D}
is injective. We make this assumption now.

(8.6.49)
Proposition.
We have an explicit isogeny direct sum decompositions
J_{C,\chi}\sim[K_{\chi}\to 0]\oplus J_{C,{\aleph_{\chi}}}
and
J_{C}^{\chi}\sim\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K_{\chi}}\oplus J_{C}^{\aleph_{\chi}}
. We can describe the decomposition maps in either direction. Observe that
J_{C,{\aleph_{\chi}}}
is reduced.

Proof.

The maps
[K_{\chi}\to 0]\to J_{C,\chi}
and
J_{C,{\aleph_{\chi}}}\to J_{C,D}
are trivially point-explicit. To determine the decomposition at the level of
\operatorname{H}_{1}
observe that we have an inclusion, having made
\chi
injective,
\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{C,\chi})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{C,D})
. We may now use the decomposition on the right hand side from (
8.4
). For the maps in the reverse direction repeat the arguments there.

For the dual decomposition
J_{C}^{\chi}\sim\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K_{\chi}}\oplus J_{C}^{\aleph_{\chi}}
we make use of (
8.5
). For instance, injectivity of
\chi
implies
J_{C}^{D}\twoheadrightarrow J_{C}^{\chi}
and
K_{\chi}\hookrightarrow K_{D}
implies
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K_{D}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K_{\chi}}
. To describe the map
J_{C}^{\chi}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K_{\chi}}
lift the divisor to
J_{C}^{D}
, tautological at the level of representatives, map to
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K_{D}}
and then down to
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K_{\chi}}
. On the map of
\operatorname{H}_{1}
, use the splitting
\operatorname{H}_{1}(\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K_{D}})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{C}^{D})
followed by the surjection
\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{C}^{D})\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{C}^{\chi})
. The other map
J_{C}^{\chi}\twoheadrightarrow J_{C}^{\aleph_{\chi}}
is induced by pushforward. For the maps in the reverse direction we repeat the argument in (
8.4
) and use (
8.5
).
∎

8.7.
Splitting Baker components, part IV

(8.7.50) Given a push-pull motive
M=J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}
with
\chi\colon X\to L_{D}
and
\psi\colon Y\to L_{E}
, we will give an algorithm that produces a push-pull Jacobian motive
M^{\prime}
, an explicit isogeny direct sum decompoition of
M^{\prime}
into a Baker component and a reduced push-pull Jacobian, and a realization of
M
as a push-pull of
M^{\prime}
.

(8.7.51)
Remark.
Recall that period relations of
M
can be computed from that of
M^{\prime}
(
6.6
) and the periods of
M^{\prime}
can be computed once we can split it into a direct sum of a Baker and a reduced motive (
5.6
).

(8.7.52) We make use of the notation in the previous parts. Anything defined for
\chi
, e.g.,
\aleph_{\chi}
, will also be defined for
\psi
. Modify the representative as in (
8.2
) to assume both
\chi
and
\psi
are injective. Also, pass to an isogenous representative, as in (
8.2
), so as to assume that neither
\chi
or
\psi
hit non-trivial torsion divisors.

(8.7.53) Consider the map
L_{E}\to J_{C}^{\chi}
. Compute
K_{E}\subset L_{E}
and observe that
K_{E}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{X}
can be made explicit: write
K_{E}=\langle\divv(f_{1}),\dots,\divv(f_{k})\rangle
and recall (
6.3
) that
K_{E}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{X}
is described by
\divv(f_{i})\mapsto(f_{i})|_{D}\circ\chi
. Compute the kernel
K^{\chi}_{E}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\ker(K_{E}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{X})
. By scaling
\psi
again, we assume
K^{\chi}_{\psi}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\psi^{-1}K^{\chi}_{E}
is saturated in
Y
. Let
N_{\psi}^{\chi}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}Y/K_{\psi}^{\chi}
and choose a splitting
\aleph_{\psi}^{\chi}\colon N_{\psi}^{\chi}\to Y
. If
Y=L_{E}
and
\psi
was the identity (prior to scaling) then we write
N_{E}^{\chi}
and
\aleph_{E}^{\psi}
instead.

(8.7.54)
Lemma.
The map
[K^{\chi}_{\psi}\to 0]\to J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}
can be made explicit. In particular, we have an explicit direct sum decomposition
J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}\simeq[K^{\chi}_{\psi}\to 0]\oplus J_{C,\aleph_{\psi}^{\chi}}^{\chi}
.

Proof.

The action on the points is a triviality. The difficulty is in computing the image under
\operatorname{H}_{1}
. We can choose a basis of
K^{\chi}_{\psi}
consisting of divisors of functions
f\in\kappa(C)_{D}
. Therefore, it will be sufficient to fix one such
f
with
[\divv(f)]_{\chi}=0
with support in
Y\hookrightarrow L_{E}
and to determine the corresponding image of
[\mathbb{Z}\to 0]\to J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}
under
\operatorname{H}_{1}
.

Since
\divv(f)
is supported on
E
, we can view it as a map
f\colon(C\setminus{}E,D)\to(\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus{}\{0,\infty\},f(D))
. The pullback induces an explicit map
f^{*}\colon J_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\{0,\infty\}}^{f(D)}\to J_{C,E}^{D}
. Here
J_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\{0,\infty\}}^{f(D)}=[\mathbb{Z}\langle\infty-0\rangle\to J_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{f(D)}]
and
f^{*}
maps
\infty-0
to
\divv(f)
.

Note that
\chi\to L_{D}\overset{f_{*}}{\to}L_{f(D)}
gives a pushout
J_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\{0,\infty\}}^{f_{*}\circ\chi}
of the Baker motive
J_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\{0,\infty\}}^{f(D)}
which maps to
J_{C,E}^{\chi}
. Since
[\divv(f)]_{\chi}=0
, the pushout has a split
[\mathbb{Z}\to 0]
submotive. Making this splitting explicit by way of correspondences is possible but cumbersome. In practice, it is much easier to put the period matrix into block form, by change of bases in the de Rham and Betti realizations, using the fact that we can work effectively with the Baker periods (
8.8
). Once
\operatorname{H}_{1}([\mathbb{Z}\to 0])\hookrightarrow\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\{0,\infty\}}^{f_{*}\circ\chi})
is computed, map it to
\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{C,E}^{\chi})
using
f
, and then use the pullback via
\psi
to determine the image in
\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{C,\psi}^{\chi})\subset\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\{0,\infty\}}^{f_{*}\circ\chi})
(here we use
\divv(f)\in Y\hookrightarrow L_{E}
).

The map
J_{C,\aleph_{\psi}^{\chi}}^{\chi}\hookrightarrow J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}
is explicit and completes the direct sum decomposition together with
[K^{\chi}_{\psi}\to 0]\hookrightarrow J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}
.
∎

(8.7.55)
Lemma.
We can effectively realize
J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}
, up to isogeny, as a pullback of a motive
M^{\prime}=[Y\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K_{\chi}}]\oplus J_{C,\psi}^{\aleph_{\chi}}
given as a direct sum. Note that the submotive
J_{C}^{\aleph_{\chi}}
is reduced.

Proof.

Explicitly split
J_{C}^{\chi}\sim\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K_{\chi}}\oplus J_{C}^{\aleph_{\chi}}
as in (
8.6
). Since this isogeny is point explicit, we may map our marking
Y\to J_{C}^{\chi}
to the two components, at least after scaling
\psi
. The pullback via the diagonal
Y\to Y\oplus Y
recovers
J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}
.
∎

(8.7.56)
Lemma.
We can effectively realize
J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}
, up to isogeny, as a pushout of a motive
M^{\prime}=[K_{\psi}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{X}]\oplus J_{C,\aleph_{\psi}}^{\chi}
. Note that the quotient motive
J_{C,\aleph_{\psi}}
is reduced.

Proof.

The construction of
M^{\prime}
is, by now, clear. Pushout
M^{\prime}
via the diagonal
X\to X\oplus X
to recover
J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}
up to isogeny.
∎

(8.7.57)
Proposition.
Given
J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}
, we can effectively construct a Baker motive
M_{B}
and a
reduced
push-pull Jacobian motive
M^{{\operatorname{red}}}
together with lattice maps realizing
J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}
as a push-pull of
M_{B}\oplus M^{{\operatorname{red}}}
, up to isogeny.

Proof.

We note that
\aleph_{\chi}
and
\aleph_{\psi}
give reduced motives (
8.6
). Now apply the two lemmas (
8.7
) and (
8.7
), one after the other, removing the Baker components at each step to end up with
M^{{\operatorname{red}}}=J_{C,\aleph_{\psi}}^{\aleph_{\chi}}
.
∎

8.8.
Period relations of a Baker motive

(8.8.58) Let
M_{B}
be a Baker motive. Just like any other motive in our computational scheme, it is represented as a push-pull Jacobian motive. We will give an algorithm to compute the period relations
\mathcal{R}(M_{B})\subset\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(M_{B})\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(M_{B})
.

(8.8.59) Let
C=\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
D,E\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
be disjoint finite sets. Denote the points of
E
by
e_{i}
’s and
D
by
d_{i}
’s. To simplify notation, we will assume
d_{0}\in D
is
\infty
and identify the rest of the points in
E,D
with algebraic numbers
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
. For each
i,j
fix a branch of the logarithm
\log(e_{j}-d_{i})
. We will say that a period of
J_{\mathbb{P}^{1},E}^{D}
is in
standard form
if it is expressed as an explicit
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
-linear combination of
1
,
2\pi{\tt i}
, and
\log(e_{i}-d_{j})
’s.

(8.8.60)
Lemma.

Given a representation of
\operatorname{H}_{1}(\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus{}D,E)
, we can effectively express each entry in its period matrix in standard form.

Proof.

We are given representative elements for bases for
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus{}D,E)
and
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus{}D,E)
. We fix one of each: a representative form
(a,\omega)
and
1
-chain
\gamma
. We will write the period
\int_{\gamma}(a,\omega)=a(\partial\gamma)+\int_{\gamma}\omega
in standard form. The first term is an explicit algebraic multiple of
1
so we focus on the second term.

Write
\omega=P(x)/Q(x)\,\mathrm{d}x
, and decompose this into a sum of elementary fractions:

(8.8.60.1)

\omega=\sum_{i}\frac{P_{i}(x)}{(x-d_{i})^{n_{i}}}\,\mathrm{d}x,

with
n_{i}\geq 0
,
\deg P_{i}<n_{i}
, and
P_{i}
relatively prime to
(x-d_{i})
. If
n_{i}\neq 1
, then each summand admits an elementary antiderivative, and the corresponding integral can be computed by evaluating this antiderivative at the algebraic endpoints of
\gamma
giving an explicit algebraic number.

When
n_{i}=-1
,
P_{i}
is a constant, the residue of
\omega
at
c_{i}
, call it
r_{i}\in{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
. The local antiderivatives are branches of
r_{i}\log(x-d_{i})
. The integral of this term over
\gamma
will differ from the evaluation of the logarithm on
\partial\gamma
by an
integer
multiple of
r_{i}2\pi{\tt i}
. This integer multiple can be explicitly and rigorously determined, for instance by numerical approximation of the integral and of the logarithms with rigorous error bounds. The evaluation of
r_{i}\log(x-d_{i})
on
\partial\gamma
is an explicit
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
-linear combination of
\log(e_{j}-d_{i})
’s.
∎

(8.8.61)
Lemma.

Let
M_{B}
be a Baker motive. Then the space
\mathcal{R}(M_{B})
of
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
-linear relations between the entries of its period matrix can be computed effectively.

Proof.

Since a Baker motive has trivial abelian part, we may write it as a push-pull of
J_{C,E}^{D}
, with
C=\coprod_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
D,E\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
disjoint finite subsets. By (
6.6
), the period relations of
M_{B}
are determined by those of
J_{C,E}^{D}
, and thus we may assume
M_{B}=J_{C,E}^{D}
. The Jacobian motive
J_{C,E}^{D}
is a direct sum of irreducible Jacobian motives with the underlying curve
C=\mathbb{P}^{1}
. We may, therefore, apply Lemma (
8.8
) to write the entries of the period matrix of
J_{C,E}^{D}
in standard form.

Let
N=\dim\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}=\dim\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}
and identify the space
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}
with
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{N^{2}}
via the representing bases. The entries of the period matrix represents the period pairing in our coordinates
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{N^{2}}\to\mathbb{C}
. The kernel
\mathcal{R}(M_{B})
is the space of relations between the entries of the period matrix.

Using Baker’s theorem
[
Bak66
]
, see also
[
HW22
, Theorem 1.05]
, any
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
-linear relation among logarithms of algebraic numbers lies in the
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
-span of
\mathbb{Q}
-linear relations among the same logarithms, which are in turn spanned by the multiplicative relations among the arguments. Moreover, a non-zero
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
-linear combination of logarithms of algebraic numbers is never algebraic. Therefore, computing the desired space of relations reduces to finding the lattice of multiplicative relations between non-zero algebraic numbers.

To find multiplicative relations between
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{\times}\subset\mathbb{C}
, construct the abstract number field
K=\mathbb{Q}[x]/p(x)
and elements
k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\in K
such that
K
has an embedding that maps
(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})
to
(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
. A multiplicative relation between
a_{i}
’s hold if and only if the same relation holds between the
k_{i}
’s in
K
. Find a finite set of places
S
of
K
such that
S
contains all Archimedean places and the
k_{i}
’s are invertible outside of
S
. Now use Drichlet’s
S
-unit theorem, or rather its proof using logarithmic embeddings
[
Nar04
, Theorem 3.12]
, together with a day-and-night algorithm analogous to the one given in §
8.3
to find the lattice of all multiplicative relations between the
k_{i}
’s.
∎

9.
Supersaturation

(9.0.1) In this section, we will describe an algorithm that effectively supersaturates a given Jacobian motive
J_{C,E}^{D}
. By
effective supersaturation
we mean that the algorithm takes a Jacobian motive
M=J_{C,E}^{D}
and outputs the following:

(1)

A supersaturated motive
M^{\operatorname{ss}}=J_{C,\psi^{\prime}}^{\chi^{\prime}}
represented as a push-pull Jacobian motive.

(2)

Lattice morphisms that realize
M
as a push-pull of
M^{\operatorname{ss}}
.

(3)

An explicit isogeny direct sum decomposition of
M^{\operatorname{ss}}\sim M_{B}\oplus M^{{\operatorname{sat}}}
into the direct sum of a Baker motive and a
saturated
push-pull Jacobian motive
M^{{\operatorname{sat}}}=J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}
.

(4)

The action of
\End_{\mathbb{Q}}(J_{C})
on
\operatorname{H}_{1}(M^{\operatorname{sat}})
.

(9.0.2) Note that period relations of
M
can be determined effectively from those of
M^{\operatorname{ss}}
by (
6.6
). The isogeny splitting gives an effective isomorphism
\operatorname{H}_{1}(M^{\operatorname{ss}})\simeq\operatorname{H}_{1}(M_{B})\oplus\operatorname{H}_{1}(M^{{\operatorname{sat}}})
. Since
M_{B}
is Baker and
M^{{\operatorname{sat}}}
is saturated, we can compute the period relations of
M^{\operatorname{ss}}
from those of
M_{B}
and
M^{{\operatorname{sat}}}
(
5.6
). The period relations of
M_{B}
can be computed directly (
8.8
). The period relations of
M^{{\operatorname{sat}}}
can be computed since it is saturated and we know the endomorphism action (
5.6
). Therefore, once the supersaturation is complete, we can compute the period relations of
M
.

(9.0.3)
Remark.
In principle, during the algorithm, we change
C
by adding disjoint copies of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
to
C
, see (
8.2
). However, this does not change
J_{C}
and we will make this operation without changing the name of
C
.

(9.0.4) Throughout, we fix a representation of
\mathcal{E}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\End_{\mathbb{Z}}(J_{C})
. This can be computed (
7.2
) and involves correspondences
\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{\rho}
over
C
such that
\mu_{1}=[\mathcal{C}_{1,*}],\dots,\mu_{\rho}=[\mathcal{C}_{\rho,*}]
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-linear basis for
\mathcal{E}
. We will take
\mathcal{C}_{1}=\Delta_{C}
to be the diagonal, inducing the identity on divisors. We also have the integer tensor
(a_{ij}^{k})
representing the product,
\mu_{i}\cdot\mu_{j}=\sum_{k}a_{ij}^{k}\mu_{k}
.

9.1.
Supersaturation of Jacobian motives of the second kind

(9.1.5) We begin by giving an algorithm to supersaturate a Jacobian motive
J_{C,D}=[L_{D}\to J_{C}]
of the second kind. Let
X^{\prime}=\mathcal{E}\otimes L_{D}
, and define the supersaturated motive as
M^{\operatorname{ss}}=[X^{\prime}\to J_{C}]
via the map
\mu\otimes\xi\mapsto\mu([\xi])
. The motive
J_{C,D}
is the pullback of
M^{\operatorname{ss}}
via
L_{D}\overset{1\otimes\id}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{E}\otimes L_{D}
. The motive
M^{\operatorname{ss}}
coincides with the one considered in
[
HW22
, §15]
. We note that
M^{\operatorname{ss}}
can be explicitly represented as a pullback Jacobian motive, and while the induced action on the points of
J_{C}
is straightforward, the corresponding action of
\mathcal{E}
on
\operatorname{H}_{1}(M^{\operatorname{ss}})
expressed via the push-pull representation is significantly more involved.

(9.1.6) Determine (
7.4
) finite subsets
D^{\prime},D^{\prime\prime}\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
such that our chosen basis of correspondences (
9
) induce correspondences from
(C,D)
to
(C,D^{\prime})
and from
(C,D^{\prime})
to
(C,D^{\prime\prime})
. Explicitly, we obtain lattice maps:

(9.1.6.1)

\chi^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{E}\otimes L_{D}\to L_{D^{\prime}}:\mu_{i}\otimes\xi\mapsto\mathcal{C}_{i,*}(\xi),\quad\text{and}\quad\chi^{\prime\prime}\colon\mathcal{E}\otimes L_{D^{\prime}}\to L_{D^{\prime\prime}}:\mu_{i}\otimes\xi\mapsto\mathcal{C}_{i,*}(\xi).

Because
\mathcal{C}_{1}=\Delta_{C}
, we have
D\subset D^{\prime}\subset D^{\prime\prime}
. Let
\iota_{D^{\prime},D^{\prime\prime}}\colon L_{D^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow L_{D^{\prime\prime}}
be the natural inclusion map.

(9.1.7)
Pullback Jacobian representation.
By construction, the map
X^{\prime}\to J_{C}
factors through
\chi^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}\to L_{D^{\prime}}
. Consequently, we represent the motive
M^{\operatorname{ss}}
as the pullback Jacobian motive
\chi^{\prime,*}J_{C,D^{\prime}}
.

(9.1.8)
Action on points.
The induced action on the points of
M^{\operatorname{ss}}
is clear. The algebra
\mathcal{E}
acts on the lattice
\mathcal{E}\otimes L_{D}
through left multiplication, which is effective. For the points of
J_{C}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
, the action of each element of
\mathcal{E}
is explicitly realized via the chosen basis of correspondences.

(9.1.9)
Action on cohomology.
The goal of the rest of the subsection is to provide an algorithm explicitly computing the action of
\mathcal{E}
on
\operatorname{H}_{1}(M^{\operatorname{ss}})=\chi^{\prime,*}\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{C,D^{\prime}})
.

(9.1.10) To simplify exposition, we first ensure the lattice maps
\chi^{\prime}
and
\chi^{\prime\prime}
are injective (
8.2
), and scale these maps appropriately to avoid torsion divisors (
8.6
).

(9.1.11) Define
K\subset\mathcal{E}\otimes L_{D}
as the pullback of
T_{D^{\prime}}
(equivalently, of
K_{D^{\prime}}
). Since the
\mathcal{E}
-action maps torsion points of
J_{C}
to torsion points, the sublattice
K
must be
\mathcal{E}
-invariant. Use the semisimplicity of
\mathcal{E}
, and use (
7.2
), to identify a
\mathcal{E}
-invariant complementary sublattice
X\subset X^{\prime}
such that
K\oplus X
has full rank in
X^{\prime}
. We denote by
\chi\colon X\hookrightarrow L_{D^{\prime}}
the restriction of
\chi^{\prime}
to
X
, noting this restriction remains injective.

(9.1.12) Through the inclusion
K\oplus X\hookrightarrow X^{\prime}
, the original motive
M^{\operatorname{ss}}
explicitly decomposes, up to isogeny, into the direct sum
[K\to 0]\oplus J_{C,\chi}
, where
J_{C,\chi}=[X\to J_{C}]
, using (
8.6
). Since the
\mathcal{E}
-action on
[K\to 0]
and its corresponding
\operatorname{H}_{1}
is explicit, the
\mathcal{E}
-action on
\operatorname{H}_{1}(M^{\operatorname{ss}})=\operatorname{H}_{1}([K\to 0])\oplus\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{C,\chi})
can be deduced from that of
\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{C,\chi})
, which remains to be determined.

(9.1.13)
Our next goal is to realize the
\mathcal{E}
-
action on
J_{C,\chi}
by
correspondences
from
(C,D^{\prime})
to
(C,D^{\prime\prime})
. However, the obvious diagram of lattices

(9.1.13.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}\otimes X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}\otimes L_{D^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{D^{\prime\prime}}}
multiply
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\,\otimes\,\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{D^{\prime},D^{\prime\prime}}\circ\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi^{\prime\prime}}

fails to commute
. Indeed, for basis elements
\mu_{i},\mu_{j}\in\mathcal{E}\text{ and }\xi\in X\hookrightarrow\mathcal{E}\otimes L_{D^{\prime}}
the upper path is induced by

(9.1.13.2)

\mu_{i}\otimes\mu_{j}\otimes\xi\;\xmapsto{}\;(\mu_{i}\mu_{j})\otimes\xi\;=\;\sum_{k}a_{ij}^{k}\,\mu_{k}\otimes\xi\;\xmapsto{\;\chi^{\prime\prime}\;}\sum_{k}a_{ij}^{k}\,\mathcal{C}_{k}\!\cdot\!\xi,

whereas the lower path is induced by
\mu_{i}\otimes\mu_{j}\otimes\xi\mapsto\mathcal{C}_{i}\!\cdot\!\mathcal{C}_{j}\!\cdot\!\xi.

These two need not agree but, as the
\mathcal{E}
-
action on
J_{C}
is well
-
defined, the difference
\mathcal{C}_{i}\!\cdot\!\mathcal{C}_{j}\!\cdot\!\xi-\sum_{k}a_{ij}^{k}\,\mathcal{C}_{k}\!\cdot\!\xi
is
linearly equivalent to
0
in
L_{D^{\prime\prime}}
.

(9.1.14)
Recall
\chi\colon X\hookrightarrow L_{D^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow L_{D^{\prime\prime}}
avoids both
K_{D^{\prime}}
and
K_{D^{\prime\prime}}
(as well as torsion divisors). Use (
8.4
) to explicitly isogeny split
J_{C,D^{\prime\prime}}\sim[K_{D^{\prime\prime}}\to 0]\oplus J_{C,\aleph_{D^{\prime\prime}}}
where
\aleph_{D^{\prime\prime}}\colon N_{D^{\prime\prime}}\to L_{D^{\prime\prime}}
is defined as in (
8.4
). We may scale
\chi
to assume
X\to L_{D^{\prime\prime}}
factors through
n_{D^{\prime\prime}}L_{D^{\prime\prime}}
and hence
X\to J_{C}
factors through
\aleph_{D^{\prime\prime}}
. Since
N_{D^{\prime\prime}}
is a quotient of
n_{D^{\prime\prime}}L_{D^{\prime\prime}}
by classes linearly equivalent to
0
, the diagram (
9.1.13.1
)
will commute
when
L_{D^{\prime\prime}}
is replaced by
N_{D^{\prime\prime}}
(and
L_{D^{\prime}}
is replaced by a multiple).

(9.1.15) In particular, fixing the diagram (
9.1.13.1
) as in (
9.1
) and including the Jacobian components, we get the following diagram (defined up to isogeny), which commutes at the level of points:

(9.1.15.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}\otimes{[X\to J_{C}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[X\to J_{C}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}\otimes{[L_{D^{\prime}}\to J_{C}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[N_{D^{\prime\prime}}\to J_{C}]}.}

We will now realize the bottom arrow using correspondences.

(9.1.16) Let
\widetilde{C}=\coprod_{\rho}C
denote the disjoint union of
\rho
copies of
C
, indexed by the basis elements
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. Define
\widetilde{D}^{\prime}\subset\widetilde{C}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
as the disjoint union of
\rho
copies of
D^{\prime}
. Our choice of basis for
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{Z}}
identifies the bottom left motive in (
9.1.15.1
) with a Jacobian motive:

(9.1.16.1)

\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes[L_{D^{\prime}}\to J_{C}]\simeq[L_{\widetilde{D}^{\prime}}\to J_{\widetilde{C}}].

To describe the bottom map (
9.1.15.1
) first consider the correspondence
\mathcal{C}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\coprod\mathcal{C}_{i}
which induces a correspondence from
(\widetilde{C},\widetilde{D}^{\prime})
to
(C,D^{\prime\prime})
and hence an explicit map
\mathcal{C}_{*}\colon J_{\widetilde{C},\widetilde{D}^{\prime}}\to J_{C,D^{\prime\prime}}
.

(9.1.17) We modify diagram (
9.1.15.1
) to take into account the correspondence to
J_{C,D^{\prime\prime}}
followed by the explicit surjection coming from the splitting from Lemma (
8.4
):

(9.1.17.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}\otimes{[X\to J_{C}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[X\to J_{C}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}\otimes{L_{D^{\prime}}\to J_{C}}={[L_{\widetilde{D}^{\prime}}\to J_{\widetilde{C}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[L_{D^{\prime\prime}}\to J_{C}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[N_{D^{\prime\prime}}\to J_{C}]}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{*}}

With this arrangement, each map in the diagram is explicitly realized either by correspondences, lattice pullback, or Baker splitting, making the induced maps on
\operatorname{H}_{1}
’s effective. The commutativity of the diagram follows from that of (
9.1.15.1
).

(9.1.18) We recall that the diagram does not commute at
J_{C,D^{\prime\prime}}
but only at
J_{C,\aleph_{D^{\prime\prime}}}
. Nevertheless, since
X\hookrightarrow L_{D^{\prime}}
and
X\hookrightarrow N_{D^{\prime\prime}}
are injective, the downward arrows in (
9.1.17.1
) induce injections
\mathcal{E}\otimes\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{C,\chi})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{\widetilde{C},\widetilde{D}^{\prime}})
and
\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{C,\chi})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{C,\aleph_{D^{\prime\prime}}})
. Therefore, we can compute the action
\mathcal{E}\otimes\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{C,\chi})\to\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{C,\chi})
.

9.2.
Supersaturation of Jacobian motives of the third kind

(9.2.19) This time we give an algorithm to supersaturate a Jacobian motive
J_{C}^{D}=[L_{D}\to J_{C}]^{\vee}
of the third kind.

(9.2.20)
Remark.
On could imagine supersaturating
J_{C,D}
and then passing to the duals, however, we need to be able to do both on the “same side” to handle the general case. Moreover, we have not given an algorithm to compute the duality pairing
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D)\otimes\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C,D)\to\mathbb{C}
, therefore we do not use duality to transfer period relations.

(9.2.21) Let
X^{\prime}=L_{D}\otimes\mathcal{E}
and define the supersaturated motive as
M^{\operatorname{ss}}=[X^{\prime}\to J_{C}]^{\vee}
where the map is
\xi\otimes\mu\mapsto[\xi]\cdot\mu
. The map
L_{D}\overset{\id\otimes 1}{\longrightarrow}\to L_{D}\otimes\mathcal{E}
realizes
M
as a pushout of
M^{\operatorname{ss}}
. We use the right action on the lattice so that when we dualize we get the left action of
\mathcal{E}
on the Jacobian.

(9.2.22) Determine (
7.4
) finite subsets
D^{\prime},D^{\prime\prime}\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
such that our chosen basis of correspondences (
9
) induce correspondences from
C\setminus{}D
to
C\setminus{}D^{\prime}
and from
C\setminus{}D^{\prime}
to
C\setminus{}D^{\prime\prime}
. Note that we have induced maps
\chi^{\prime}\colon L_{D}\otimes\mathcal{E}\to L_{D^{\prime}}
and
\chi^{\prime\prime}\colon L_{D^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{E}\to L_{D^{\prime\prime}}
defined as in (
9.1.6.1
).

(9.2.23)
Pushout Jacobian representation.
Once again, the map
X^{\prime}\to J_{C}
factors through
\chi^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}\to L_{D^{\prime}}
and, hence, the motive
M^{\operatorname{ss}}
is represented as the pushout motive
\chi^{\prime}_{*}J_{C}^{D^{\prime}}
.

(9.2.24)
The action.
It remains to compute the action of
\mathcal{E}
on the points and on
\operatorname{H}_{1}
of
M^{\operatorname{ss}}
. We do so following the dual construction for the second kind motives. We will give a quick summary on how build the dual diagram.

(9.2.25) Once again, we may assume
\chi^{\prime}
and
\chi^{\prime\prime}
are injective and avoid torsion divisors. We determine (
8.6
) an explicit isogeny splitting
M^{\operatorname{ss}}\sim\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K}\oplus J_{C}^{\chi}
for a
\mathcal{E}
-equivariant isogeny splitting
X^{\prime}\sim K\oplus X
where
K
is the kernel of
X^{\prime}\to J_{C}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
and
\xi=\xi^{\prime}|_{X}
. Since the identification of the points
M^{\operatorname{ss}}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\otimes\mathbb{Q}
with the isogeny splitting is effective as well the splitting
\operatorname{H}_{1}(M^{\operatorname{ss}})\simeq\operatorname{H}_{1}(\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K})\oplus J_{C}^{\chi}
, we reduce the problem to identifying the
\mathcal{E}
-action on each component separately. The
\mathcal{E}
-action on
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K}
is obvious, so we reduce to identifying the
\mathcal{E}
action on
J_{C}^{\chi}
.

(9.2.26)
Remark.
The right action
K\otimes\mathcal{E}\to K
is induced from multiplication on the right by the identification
K\subset L_{D}\otimes\mathcal{E}
. Dualizing, we get a map
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K}\to\mathcal{E}^{\vee}\otimes\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K}
so that tensoring with
\mathcal{E}
and then contracting
\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{E}^{\vee}\to\mathbb{Z}
gives the desired action
\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K}
.

(9.2.27)
Dual maps.
The map
J_{C,D^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{E}\to J_{C,D^{\prime\prime}}:\xi\otimes\mu_{i}\mapsto\xi\cdot\mathcal{C}_{i}=\mathcal{C}_{i}^{*}\xi
dualizes to give
J_{C}^{D^{\prime\prime}}\to\mathcal{E}^{\vee}\otimes J_{C}^{D^{\prime}}\simeq(J_{C}^{D^{\prime}})^{\oplus\rho}
which at the level of points is given by

(9.2.27.1)

\xi\mapsto\left(\mu_{i}\mapsto\mathcal{C}_{i,*}\xi\right)=(\mathcal{C}_{1,*}\xi,\dots,\mathcal{C}_{\rho,*}\xi).

This can be seen by identifying
\mathcal{E}\otimes J_{C,D^{\prime}}
with the Jacobian of a curve and describing the morphism
J_{C,D^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{E}\to J_{C,D^{\prime\prime}}
by a correspondence as in (
9.1
) and observing that taking duals uses the transpose action of the correspondence.

(9.2.28)
Dualize the action.
Repeat the argument in the previous section to arrive at the
commutative
diagram (
9.1.17.1
) on the side of lattices but using the right action of
\mathcal{E}
. Taking duals we get the following
commutative
diagram,

(9.2.28.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}^{\vee}\otimes{J_{C}^{\chi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{J_{C}^{\chi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}^{\vee}\otimes{J_{C}^{D^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{C}^{D^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{C}^{\aleph_{D^{\prime\prime}}}.}

We now tensor by
\mathcal{E}
and then contract with
\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{E}^{\vee}\to\mathbb{Z}
.

(9.2.29) Once again
\widetilde{C}=\coprod_{\rho}C
is the disjoint union of
\rho
copies of
C
. This time, let
\widetilde{D}^{\prime\prime}\subset\widetilde{C}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
be the disjoint union of
\rho
copies of
D^{\prime\prime}
. Using our basis
\mathcal{E}\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{\rho}
, we identify
\mathcal{E}\otimes J_{C}^{D^{\prime\prime}}\simeq(J_{C}^{D^{\prime\prime}})^{\oplus\rho}
with
J_{\widetilde{C}}^{\widetilde{D}^{\prime\prime}}
.

(9.2.30) We now arrive at the commutative diagram, which makes the action on points and on
\operatorname{H}_{1}
explicit:

(9.2.30.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}\otimes J_{C}^{\chi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{C}^{\chi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}\otimes J_{C}^{\aleph_{D^{\prime\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}\otimes J_{C}^{D^{\prime\prime}}=J_{\widetilde{C}}^{\widetilde{D}^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{C}^{D^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{*}}

(9.2.31)
Effectivity.
The inclusion map
J_{C}^{\aleph_{D^{\prime\prime}}}\hookrightarrow J_{C}^{D^{\prime\prime}}
is deduced from the explicit splitting (
8.5
). The vertical maps are induced by lattice pushouts and the map
\mathcal{C}_{*}
is induced by a correspondence. Therefore, all maps except the top horizontal map are explicit. Lifting to
J_{C}^{\aleph_{D^{\prime\prime}}}
then going through
\mathcal{C}_{*}
and down to
J_{C}^{\chi}
gives effective maps on points
\mathcal{E}\otimes J_{C}^{\chi}\to J_{C}^{\chi}
and on homology
\mathcal{E}\otimes\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{C}^{\chi})\to\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{C}^{\chi})
.

(9.2.32)
Action on points.
For future use, we spell out the action on the points.
Since
J_{C}^{\chi}
is realized as a pushout of
J_{C}^{D^{\prime}}
, a point
p\in J_{C}^{\chi}
is represented by a divisor
\xi\in\Div^{0}(C\setminus{}D^{\prime})
so that
p=[\xi]_{\chi}
. Move (
6.4
) the divisor
\xi
away from
D^{\prime\prime}
in its
D^{\prime}
-linear equivalence class, getting
\xi^{\prime}\in[\xi]_{\chi}
which lifts to
[\xi^{\prime}]_{D^{\prime\prime}}\in J_{C}^{D^{\prime\prime}}
.
The pushout map gives a point
[\xi^{\prime}]_{\aleph_{D^{\prime\prime}}}\in J_{C}^{\aleph_{D^{\prime\prime}}}
, this is a lift of
p
along the diagonal map in (
9.2.30.1
). Now, use the Baker splitting (
8.5
) to apply the (non-trivial!) inclusion
J_{C}^{\aleph_{D^{\prime\prime}}}\to J_{C}^{D^{\prime\prime}}
to the point
[\xi^{\prime}]_{\aleph_{D^{\prime\prime}}}
and get
[\xi^{\prime\prime}]_{D^{\prime\prime}}
. This operation moves
\xi^{\prime}
by linear equivalence but changes its
D^{\prime\prime}
-linear equivalence class. The point
\mu_{i}\otimes[\xi^{\prime\prime}]_{D^{\prime\prime}}
gets mapped to
[\mathcal{C}_{i,*}\cdot\xi^{\prime\prime}]_{D^{\prime}}
via the bottom horizontal map and then down to
[\mathcal{C}_{i,*}\cdot\xi^{\prime\prime}]_{\chi}
. Hence
\mu_{i}\otimes[\xi]_{\chi}\mapsto[\mathcal{C}_{i,*}\cdot\xi^{\prime\prime}]_{\chi}
, which extends linearly to
\mathcal{E}\otimes J_{C}^{\chi}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\to J_{C}^{\chi}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
.

(9.2.33)
Further notes on the action on points.
Note that
\xi^{\prime\prime}=\xi^{\prime}+\divv(u)
for some
u\in\kappa(C)_{D^{\prime\prime}}
and thus
\mathcal{C}_{i,*}\cdot\xi^{\prime\prime}=\mathcal{C}_{i,*}\cdot\xi^{\prime}+\divv(v)
where
\divv(v)=\mathcal{C}_{i,*}\cdot\divv(u)
and
v\in\kappa(C)_{D^{\prime}}
. Later, we will want to represent the point
\mu_{i}\cdot[\xi]_{\chi}
by a divisor in the image of
J_{C}^{\aleph_{D^{\prime}}}\hookrightarrow J_{C}^{D^{\prime}}
. Applying the Baker splitting again, compute
\xi^{\prime\prime\prime}=\xi^{\prime\prime}+\divv(w)
for
w\in\kappa(C)_{D^{\prime}}
so that
\mu_{i}\cdot[\xi]_{\chi}=[\xi^{\prime\prime\prime}]_{\chi}
and
[\xi^{\prime\prime\prime}]_{D^{\prime}}
is in the image of
J_{C}^{\aleph_{D^{\prime}}}
.

Summarizing, we have
\mu_{i}\cdot[\xi]_{\chi}=[\mathcal{C}_{i}\cdot\xi^{\prime}+\divv(f)]
where
f\in\kappa(C)_{D^{\prime}}
depends only on
i
and
\xi^{\prime}
. If
\xi
is already distinct from
D^{\prime\prime}
then we can take
\xi=\xi^{\prime}
. If,
\xi
already defines a point in the image of
J_{C}^{\aleph_{D^{\prime\prime}}}
then we may take
\divv(f)=0
,
\mu_{i}\cdot[\xi]_{\chi}=[\mathcal{C}_{i}\cdot\xi]
. In the general case, we may choose
f
so that
[\mathcal{C}_{i}\cdot\xi^{\prime}+\divv(f)]_{D^{\prime}}
is in the image of
J_{C}^{\aleph_{D^{\prime}}}
.

9.3.
The general case

(9.3.34) We now begin the supersaturation process for the motive
M=J_{C,D}^{E}
. Construct finite subsets
D^{\prime}\subset D^{\prime\prime}\subset C(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
as in (
9.2
). Extend this chain by constructing a further set
D^{\prime\prime\prime}
such that the
\mathcal{C}_{i}
’s induce correspondances from
(C\setminus D^{\prime\prime\prime})
to
(C\setminus D^{\prime\prime})
.

(9.3.35)
Moving
E
away from
D^{\prime\prime\prime}
.
We may assume without loss of generality that
E\cap D^{\prime\prime\prime}=\emptyset
. Indeed, we can move each divisor in
L_{E}
away from
D^{\prime\prime\prime}
within its
D
-linear equivalence class, as follows. For each
\xi\in L_{E}
, we can compute a divisor
\xi^{\prime}\in[\xi]_{D}
disjoint from
D^{\prime\prime\prime}
using (
6.4
). Move a basis of divisors of
L_{E}
away from
D^{\prime\prime\prime}
and let
\widetilde{E}\subset C\setminus D^{\prime\prime}
denote the joint support of the new divisors. This induces a map
L_{E}\to L_{\widetilde{E}}
compatible with the Abel–Jacobi maps to
J_{C}^{D}
. Hence
J_{C,E}^{D}
is realized as a pullback of
J_{C,\widetilde{E}}^{D}
. Supersaturating the latter supersaturates the former, and we may therefore replace
E
by
\widetilde{E}
from now on.

(9.3.36) Supersaturate the underlying motive of the third kind
J_{C}^{D}
following §
9.1
. We continue with the notation there:
\chi,\chi^{\prime},\chi^{\prime\prime},K,X
. This process realizes
J_{C}^{D}
, up to isogeny, as a pushout of
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K}\oplus J_{C}^{\chi}
. Note that
J_{C}^{\chi}
inherits a
\mathcal{E}
-action compatible with the usual action on
J_{C}
.

(9.3.37) Recall that, after a minor adjustment (
8.2
), we assume
J_{C}^{D^{\prime}}
dominates the supersaturation of
J_{C}^{D}
. Thus, the marking
L_{E}\to J_{C}^{D}
lifts to
J_{C}^{D^{\prime}}
(and, in fact, to
J_{C}^{D^{\prime\prime\prime}}
), yielding a marking
L_{E}\to J_{C}^{\chi}\oplus\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K}
. We thereby realize
J_{C,E}^{D}
as a push-pull of
[L_{E}\to J_{C}^{\chi}\oplus\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K}]
.

(9.3.38) Use the two projection maps to construct the motive
[L_{E}\to J_{C}^{\chi}]\oplus[L_{E}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K}]
. The diagonal map
L_{E}\to L_{E}^{2}
realizes
[L_{E}\to J_{C}^{\chi}\oplus\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K}]
as a pullback of
[L_{E}\to J_{C}^{\chi}]\oplus[L_{E}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K}]
. We set aside the Baker component
[L_{E}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{K}]
. What remains is to supersaturate
J_{C,E}^{\chi}=[L_{E}\to J_{C}^{\chi}]
to complete the supersaturation of
M
.

(9.3.39) Let
M^{\operatorname{ss}}=[\mathcal{E}\otimes L_{E}\to J_{C}^{\chi}]
, with map
\mu\otimes\xi\mapsto\mu\cdot[\xi]_{\chi}
. This motive
M^{\operatorname{ss}}
is clearly supersaturated. Moreover,
J_{C,E}^{\chi}
is the pullback of
M^{\operatorname{ss}}
along
L_{E}\xrightarrow{1\otimes\id}\mathcal{E}\otimes L_{E}
. The remainder of this section aims to make explicit the
\mathcal{E}
-action on points and (co)homology of
M^{\operatorname{ss}}
.

(9.3.40)
Jacobian representation.
Recall the pointwise action (
9.2
) on
J_{C}^{\chi}
. We will use
D^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
D^{\prime\prime}
instead of
D^{\prime\prime}
and
D^{\prime}
for the construction. Using the basis of correspondences
\mu_{i}=[\mathcal{C}_{i}]
for
\mathcal{E}
and a basis
\xi_{j}
of
L_{E}
, we have:

(9.3.40.1)

\mu_{i}\cdot[\xi_{j}]_{\chi}=[\mathcal{C}_{i}\cdot\xi_{j}+\divv(f_{ij})]_{\chi},

where
f_{ij}\in\kappa(C)_{D^{\prime\prime}}
, since
\xi_{j}
need not be moved away from
D^{\prime\prime\prime}
(
9.2
). Moreover, by choosing
f_{ij}
’s appropriately, we may assume
\xi_{ij}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{C}_{i}\cdot\xi_{j}+\divv(f_{ij})
defines a
D^{\prime\prime}
-linear equivalence class in the image of
J_{C}^{\aleph_{D^{\prime\prime}}}\hookrightarrow J_{C}^{D^{\prime\prime}}
.

Let
E^{\prime}\subset C(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})\setminus D^{\prime\prime}
be a finite set supporting the divisors
\xi_{ij}
for all
i,j
. Then, we define
\psi^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{E}\otimes L_{E}\to L_{E^{\prime}}:\mu_{i}\otimes\xi_{j}\mapsto\xi_{ij}
to realize
M^{\operatorname{ss}}
as the pullback
J_{C,\psi^{\prime}}^{\chi}
of
J_{C,E^{\prime}}^{\chi}
.

(9.3.41)
Further decomposing the supersaturated motive.
Once again apply (
8.2
) to ensure
\psi^{\prime}
is injective and avoids torsion (
8.6
). We now follow the argument in (
9.1
) to decompose
\mathcal{E}\otimes L_{E}
up to isogeny as
K_{\psi^{\prime}}\oplus Y
into two
\mathcal{E}
-invariant pieces:
K_{\psi^{\prime}}
is the kernel of
\mathcal{E}\otimes L_{E}\overset{\psi^{\prime}}{\to}L_{E^{\prime}}\to J_{C}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
and
Y
is a
\mathcal{E}
-invariant complement. Let
\psi\colon Y\to L_{E^{\prime}}
be the restriction of
\psi^{\prime}
to
Y
.

(9.3.42) Observe that we can realize
J_{C,\psi^{\prime}}^{\chi}
effectively as a pushout of the direct sum
[K_{\psi^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{X}]\oplus J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}
, where the pushout is by the diagonal
X\to X\oplus X
. Putting aside the Baker component, we will consider
J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}
, which is not only supersaturated but also reduced and hence saturated.

(9.3.43)
The action figure.
It remains to make the
\mathcal{E}
-action on
J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}
explicit. At this point, we only need the action on
\operatorname{H}_{1}
but we will also describe the action on points. This will be done via Figure
1
. The arrow
1
represents the
\mathcal{E}
-action we are after and we will show how to navigate the explicit arrows in this diagram to evaluate
9.3
by a composition of explicit maps. We recall the argument in (
9.1
) to emphasize the need to follow the outer arrows in the diagram to achieve commutativity.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}\otimes[Y\to J_{C}^{\chi}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[Y\to J_{C}^{\chi}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}\otimes[Y\to J_{C}^{\aleph_{D^{\prime\prime}}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}\otimes[Y\to J_{C}^{D^{\prime\prime}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[L_{E^{\prime\prime}}\to J_{C}^{\chi}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[N^{\chi}_{E^{\prime\prime}}\to J_{C}^{\chi}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}\otimes[L_{E^{\prime}}\to J_{C}^{D^{\prime\prime}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[L_{E^{\prime\prime}}\to J_{C}^{D^{\prime}}]}}
9.3
9.3
9.3
9.3
9.3
9.3
9.3
9.3
9.3
