9.3
9.3

Figure 1.
Making the action on a supersaturated motive effective.

(9.3.44)
The right hand side.
We will describe the arrows appearing on the right hand side of Figure
1
. Apply (
7.4
) to construct a finite set
E^{\prime\prime}\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\setminus{}D^{\prime}
which supports the image of
E^{\prime}\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\setminus{}D^{\prime\prime}
under our basis of correspondences. The inclusion
Y\hookrightarrow L_{E^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow L_{E^{\prime\prime}}
induces the map
2
.

(9.3.45) We adopt the notation from (
8.7
) to decompose
L_{E^{\prime\prime}}
into
K_{E^{\prime\prime}}^{\chi}\oplus N_{E^{\prime\prime}}^{\chi}
up to isogeny. The arrow
3
is trivially explicit, since it is the canonical inclusion defined by the pullback induced by the (non-canonical) splitting map
\aleph_{E^{\prime\prime}}^{\chi}\colon N_{E^{\prime\prime}}^{\chi}\to L_{E^{\prime\prime}}
. The projection
4
is not trivial but it is made explicit by (
8.7
). The composition of
9.3
and
9.3
gives the map
5
, which is an injection because the composition
Y\hookrightarrow L_{E^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow L_{E^{\prime\prime}}\twoheadrightarrow N_{E^{\prime\prime}}^{\chi}
is injective by choice of
Y
.

(9.3.46) The surjection
6
is simply the pushout representation of
J_{C,E^{\prime\prime}}^{\chi}
and is trivially explicit.

(9.3.47)
The left hand side.
By our choice of basis for
\mathcal{E}
we make the identification
\mathcal{E}=\mathbb{Z}^{\rho}
and therefore, for any motive
A
, make the identification
\mathcal{E}\otimes A=A^{\oplus\rho}
. For a map
f\colon A\to B
, we can define a map
\mathcal{E}\otimes A\to\mathcal{E}\otimes B
by simply acting as identity on
\mathcal{E}
, i.e., taking
f^{\oplus\rho}
. All the maps appearing solely on the left hand side of Figure
1
are of this form and we will describe the respective maps on the components.

(9.3.48) The map
7
is the pushout corresponding to
X\hookrightarrow L_{D^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow L_{D^{\prime\prime}}
. The pushout
J_{C}^{D^{\prime\prime}}\twoheadrightarrow J_{C}^{\aleph_{D^{\prime\prime}}}
is explicitly split (
8.5
) and the map
8
is the splitting inclusion. Recall that the map
9
is surjective, since
\chi
avoids torsion. Our construction of
\psi^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{E}\otimes L_{E}\to L_{E^{\prime}}
in (
9.3
) is such that we lifted our marking
Y\to J_{C}^{D^{\prime\prime}}
through
9.3
.

(9.3.49) The map
10
is used indirectly: in constructing
\psi^{\prime}
, we first lifted the marking to
J_{C}^{D^{\prime\prime}}
naively, mapped down to
J_{C}^{\aleph_{D^{\prime\prime}}}
, and then took the image marking in
J_{C}^{D^{\prime\prime}}
. The naive lift (through
9.3
) and the one we constructed (through
9.3
) are different markings on
J_{C}^{D^{\prime\prime}}
.

(9.3.50) The inclusion
11
is the natural pullback map via
\psi\colon Y\to L_{E^{\prime}}
.

(9.3.51)
Applying the correspondence.
Let
\widetilde{C}=\coprod_{i=1}^{\rho}C
. Define
\widetilde{D}^{\prime\prime}
,
\widetilde{E}^{\prime}
by taking a copy of
D^{\prime\prime}
and
E^{\prime}
per component of
\widetilde{C}
. Then, using our basis for
\mathcal{E}
, we identify
\mathcal{E}\otimes[L_{E^{\prime}}\to J_{C}^{D^{\prime\prime}}]
with
J_{\widetilde{C},\widetilde{E}^{\prime}}^{\widetilde{D}^{\prime\prime}}
. Define the correspondence
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
to be “disjoint union” of the correspondences
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,\rho
from
(\widetilde{C}\setminus{}\widetilde{D}^{\prime\prime},\widetilde{E}^{\prime})
to
(C\setminus{}D^{\prime},E^{\prime\prime})
. This correspondence defines the map
12
, and is the backbone for the diagram in Figure
1
.

(9.3.52)
Commutativity.
The
path of correspondence
is the map attained by composing
9.3
,
9.3
,
9.3
,
9.3
,
9.3
. The
path of action
is the map attained by composing
9.3
,
9.3
,
9.3
. We claim that these two maps commute. Commutativity at the level of semi-abelian groups was established in (
9.2
). At the level of markings, we appeal to the commutativity of the diagram on the semi-abelian varieties—that is, on divisor classes—and proceed as in (
9.1
) and (
9.1
).

We now expand on the proof of commutativity at the level of markings. Observe that
\mu_{i}\otimes y\in\mathcal{E}\otimes Y
is mapped to
\mathcal{C}_{i}\cdot\psi(y)\in L_{E^{\prime\prime}}
via the path of correspondence, whereas it is mapped to
\psi(\mu_{i}\cdot y)\in L_{E^{\prime}}\subset L_{E^{\prime\prime}}
via the path of action. Note, however, that
\mu_{i}\cdot[\psi(y)]\chi=[\mathcal{C}_{i}\cdot\psi(y)]\chi
, since
\psi(y)
defines a divisor lying in the image of
J_{C}^{\aleph_{D^{\prime\prime}}}
(
9.2
). On the other hand, the
\mathcal{E}
-action on
Y
and
J_{C}^{\chi}
are compatible by construction, so we also have
\mu_{i}\cdot[\psi(y)]\chi=[\psi(\mu_{i}\cdot y)]\chi
. It follows that
\mathcal{C}_{i}\cdot\psi(y)-\psi(\mu_{i}\cdot y)\in K_{D^{\prime\prime}}^{\chi}
, and hence the difference vanishes in
N_{D^{\prime\prime}}^{\chi}
, establishing the desired commutativity.

(9.3.53)
Effectivity.

We wish to evaluate the map
9.3
on points and on
\operatorname{H}_{1}
. We established the commutativity (
9.3
) of the path of action, the path involving
9.3
, with the path of correspondence, the path involving
9.3
. By lifting via
9.3
we may follow the path of correspondence and then, identifying
J_{C,\psi}^{\chi}
with its image via
9.3
, we get the desired action. We claim that this operation is effective at the level of points and on
\operatorname{H}_{1}
. Most of the maps are trivial at the level of representatives because they represent pushout or pullback maps. The non-trivial maps are the splitting maps
9.3
and
9.3
. These splittings have been made explicit at the level of points and at the level of
\operatorname{H}_{1}
when splitting Baker components. The map
9.3
is effective as it is induced by a correspondence (
9.3
).

(9.3.54) This completes the description and proof of the algorithm that effectively supersaturates
J_{C,D}^{E}
.

10.
Classifying first-order autonomous differential equations

(10.0.1) Let
P(x,y)\in{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}[x,y]
be an irreducible polynomial. The autonomous first-order differential equation
P(u(t),u^{\prime}(t))=0
falls into one of four classes (
10
). As noted in
[
NvdPT22
, p.1655]
, no algorithm is known to classify such equations in full generality. We resolve this problem by providing a classification algorithm based on our computational model for the mixed Hodge structure of curves.

(10.0.2) Let
C
be the smooth proper curve with function field
{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}[x,y]/P
and consider the rational differential
\omega=\mathrm{d}x/y
on
C
. For initial conditions
(u(0),u^{\prime}(0))
avoiding singularities and vertical tangents of the affine plane curve
Z(P)\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}
, the solution
u(t)
to
P(u,u^{\prime})=0
satisfies, and is determined by, the equation

(10.0.2.1)

t=\int_{u(0)}^{u(t)}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{y(x)},\quad\text{with }P(x,y(x))=0,\ \lvert t\rvert\ll 1.

For this reason, the differential equation
P(u,u^{\prime})=0
is classified by the isomorphism type of the pair
(C,\mathrm{d}x/y)
.

(10.0.3)
Classification.
Let
C/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
be a smooth proper irreducible curve and
\omega
a nonzero rational differential on
C
. Then
(C,\omega)
falls into one of the following classes:

(1)

\omega
is exact,

(2)

\omega=h^{*}(\mathrm{d}z/z)
for a non-constant
h\colon C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
,

(3)

\omega=h^{*}\eta
for a non-constant
h\colon C\to C^{\prime}
with
C^{\prime}
an elliptic curve and
\eta
regular on
C^{\prime}
,

(4)

none of the above.

The first two cases are effectively decidable using Riemann–Roch. Deciding between the last two cases is the problem.

(10.0.4) We can significantly refine the classification above. Observe that the second and third clauses detect whether
\omega
is the pullback of a translation invariant form from a semi-abelian variety of dimension
1
. Such forms are always of the third kind, see (
10
), and differentials of the third kind are unique in their cohomology class. The first clause can thus be viewed as asserting that the cohomology class of
\omega
is a pullback from a semi-abelian variety of dimension
0
. We generalise the classification at the level of cohomology classes, and specialise to pullbacks of translation invariant regular forms in (
10
).

(10.0.5) Let
D\subset C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
be the residual polar locus of
\omega
, and let
p\in C({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})\setminus D
. The Abel–Jacobi map
\AJ_{p}\colon C\setminus D\to J_{C}^{D}
identifies
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D,E)
with
\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(J_{C}^{D})
.

(10.0.6)
Refined classification.
There exists a minimal (up to isogeny) semi-abelian quotient
f\colon J_{C}^{D}\twoheadrightarrow G
and a translation invariant differential
\eta
on
G
such that
\omega\equiv\AJ_{p}^{*}f^{*}\eta
in
\operatorname{H}^{1}(C\setminus D)
. Let
a
and
b
be the dimensions of the toric and abelian parts of
G
, respectively. We define the
refined type
of
\omega
to be the pair
(a,b)
.

(10.0.7)
Comparing the classifications.
The original classification corresponds to the cases
(0,0)
,
(1,0)
with
\omega
of the third kind,
(0,1)
with
\omega
of the third kind, and
a+b>1
. Detecting whether
\omega
is of the third kind is comparatively easy, see (
10
) below, therefore we consider the refined type
(a,b)
to be the essential piece of information.

(10.0.8)
Refined classification algorithm.

Given
(C,\omega)
for a smooth proper irreducible curve
C/{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
and a rational differential
\omega
on
C
, compute its refined type as follows:

(1)

Compute a representation for
\operatorname{H}_{1}(C\setminus D)
.

(2)

Compute coordinates for
[\omega]\in\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus D)
.

(3)

Compute the kernel
K\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}[\omega]^{\perp}\subset\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D)
under the period pairing.

(4)

Compute
W_{-2}K\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}K\cap W_{-2}\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus D)
.

(5)

Return the tuple
(\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}W_{-2}K,\,\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}K/W_{-2}K)
.

(10.0.9) The required subroutines are described in the main algorithm (
2
). In step (
3
) of (
10
), compute the kernel of the period pairing and then restrict to
\operatorname{H}_{1}^{\operatorname{B}}(C\setminus{}D)\otimes{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}\cdot[\omega]
. We now prove correctness.

(10.0.10)
Theorem.
Algorithm (
10
) terminates and returns the correct refined type of
\omega
.

Proof.

Termination of each step follows from the subroutines cited in (
2
). For correctness, recall the identification
\operatorname{H}_{1}(J_{C}^{D})=\operatorname{H}_{1}(C\setminus D)
. By Wüstholz’s analytic subgroup theorem
[
Wüs89
]
, the orthogonal
K
is the Betti realization of a semi-abelian subvariety
G^{\prime}\subset J_{C}^{D}
, and
[\omega]
is a pullback from the quotient
G\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}J_{C}^{D}/G^{\prime}
. As the kernel is the largest possible, the quotient is the smallest possible. Hence
G
is the minimal isogeny quotient of
J_{C}^{D}
from which
[\omega]
can be pulled-back. The toric and abelian dimension of
G
are
(a,b)=(\dim W_{-2}K,\dim K/W_{-2}K)
.
∎

(10.0.11)
Pullback of translation invariant regular differentials.
Differentials of the third kind on
C
correspond exactly to pullbacks of translation invariant regular differentials from semi-abelian varieties. To see this, use that
J_{C}^{D}
is universal with respect to maps from
C\setminus{}D
to semi-abelian varieties and that translation invariant forms on
J_{C}^{D}
pullback to differentials of the third kind with residual poles at
D
.

(10.0.12)
Detecting differentials of the third kind.
We can check whether a given differential
\omega
is of the third kind or equivalent to one. A differential of the third kind will give an element in
F^{1}\operatorname{H}^{1}_{{\operatorname{AdR}}}
and a class in
F^{1}\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}
will contain precisely one differential of the third kind. Let
D
be the residual polar locus of
\omega
. If
[\omega]\notin F^{1}\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D)
then
\omega
is not equivalent to a differential of the third kind. If
[\omega]\in F^{1}\operatorname{H}^{1}_{\operatorname{AdR}}(C\setminus{}D)
then our reduction algorithm, §
3.7
,
returns a representative
\eta\in[\omega]
of the third kind and an exact form
\mathrm{d}h
such that
\omega=\eta+\mathrm{d}h
.

(10.0.13) Consequently, we can detect whether
\omega
is equal to, or equivalent modulo exact forms to, the pullback of a translation invariant differential form from a semi-abelian variety. Combined with the refined type of
\omega
, this is a strict refinement of the original classification (
10
).
